                                           3-DIMENSIONAL TQFTS FROM DERIVED CATEGORIES OF
                                                   QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS


                                                                                CRIS NEGRON




                                                 Abstract. For any finite modular tensor category A, we show that the asso-
                                                 ciated derived ∞-category D(A) supports a topological quantum field theory
                                                 in dimension 3. This TQFT takes the form of a symmetric monoidal functor
arXiv:2608.10285v1 [math.QA] 10 Aug 2026




                                                 from an ∞-category of surfaces with markings by objects in D(A), and appro-
                                                 priately decorated bordisms, to the ∞-category of dg vector spaces. We show
                                                 that the state spaces in this theory are naturally identified with linearized
                                                 mapping spaces for D(A).
                                                     Though our TQFT requires markings from the derived ∞-category, we show
                                                 that all markings can be removed after taking a homotopy truncation. The
                                                 resulting unmarked TQFT produces projective mapping class group actions
                                                 on cohomology in dimension 2, and in particular a projective SL2 (Z)-action
                                                 on Hochschild cohomology. We expect these mapping class group actions to
                                                 recover those of Lentner et al. [51] and Schweigert-Woike [72]. In dimension 3
                                                 we obtain power-series valued knot invariants, and power-series valued invari-
                                                 ants for modular tensor categories. We also obtain power-series invariants for
                                                 closed 3-manifold, though these can already be calculated at the abelian level.
                                                     Our derived field theories are proposed as mathematical formalizations
                                                 for topological A-twists of certain N = 4 supersymmetric QFTs, in dimen-
                                                 sion 3. Following physical principles, we discuss the possibility of deforming
                                                 our TQFTs along local systems via an analogous (conjectural) deformation of
                                                 quantum group representations along the Langlands dual group.

                                1. Introduction
   This paper concerns 3-dimensional topological field theories obtained from de-
rived modular tensor categories. Our favorite examples to consider are categories
of small quantum group representations (Repq G)small , for a semisimple algebraic
group G at an even order parameter q [33, 64], and categories of modules over rigid
C2 -cofinite vertex operator algebras [60]. New exotic examples of finite modular
tensor categories also appear in work of Ostrik and Utiralova [66].
   In short, we show that the derived category Df in = Db (Af in ) of finite length
complexes over a finite modular tensor category A supports a 3-dimensional topo-
logical field theory
                             LDf in : Bordnc
                                          3,2 → D(Vect)
which is valued in the derived category of linear cochains. Here the superscript nc
indicates that our theory is non-compact, i.e. is only defined on 3-manifolds with
non-trivial outgoing boundary. For the value on a connected genus g surface, we
exhibit a natural identification with the derived Homs
                                     ∼
                          LDf in (Σ) → RHomA (C ⊗g , 1),
where C is the so-called canonical coend. Thus, for example, the value on the torus
recovers Hochschild cohomology for A, and the value on the sphere recovers exten-
sions of the unit. Furthermore, in the case where the input category A is semisimple
the theory LDf in collapses to the non-compact part of classical Reshetikhin-Turaev
theory, and the full Reshetikhin-Turaev theory is the unique extension of LDf in to
the full bordism category.
Remark 1.1. The state spaces for LDf in are allowed to be infinite dimensional
precisely because of the omission of closed 3-manifolds in the non-compact bordism
category.
   Our TQFT LDf in is a projection of an analogous theory LDf in which is defined
at the level of ∞-categories, and which includes markings from the derived ∞-
category. Before continuing with an enumeration of our findings, however, let us
explain the appropriate context for our study. We return to a discussion of the
technical aspects of the text in Section 1.3 below.
1.1. Mathematics of non-semisimple TQFTs. Over the past 10 years or so
there have been a number of works on “non-semisimple” TQFTs in mathematics.
Arguably, the first results in this direction concern homology-dependent TQFTs
constructed from the unrolled quantum group [6, 24]. These TQFTs are not only
anomalous, but depend on the choice of a generic homology class over each surface
and bordism.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        3


   Such homology dependent theories were originally constructed in work of Blanchet,
Costantino, Geer, and Patureau-Mirand, specifically for sl2 , and were later gener-
alized to arbitrary semisimple g and also some super groups as well [24, 36, 38].
Though we won’t elaborate further on the nature of these TQFTs, for practical rea-
sons, let us only say that the genericity condition on the homology class translates
to a kind of projectivity constraint on representations for the unrolled quantum
group, and that projectivity plays a key role in the construction of the correspond-
ing theories.
   Following the homology dependent contributions from [6, 24], it was shown in
work of De Renzi, Gainutdinov, Geer, Patureau-Mirand, and Runkel that any finite
(non-semisimple) modular tensor category can be used to produce a 3-dimensional
Reshetikhin-Turaev type theory
                          Labelian
                           A       : Borddecorated
                                         3,2       → Vect .
The theory Labelian
               A       depends not on the choice of a homology class, but on the
presence of projectives in various strategic positions [22]. These projectives are
technically only required in order to generate closed 3-manifold invariants, via the
use of a modified trace, but should arguably also be present in dimension 2 [21, 37].
   It was subsequently shown in work of Costantino, Geer, Haı̈oun, and Patureau-
Mirand [14] that any finite modular tensor category determines, additionally, an
invertible 4-dimensional theory (see also [7, 5]), and that one can recover the 3-d
theory of De Renzi et al. as a boundary to its 4-d counterpart [39]. In 4-dimensions,
projectives are required in dimensions 4 and 3, and even 2 [10], via the implemen-
tation of modified traces and the required use of “admissible” skeins.
   What we’ll highlight here is the apparent asymmetry in the treatment of ob-
jects, so that each non-semisimple TQFT is arguably associated to the pairing of
a modular tensor category A with its Karoubian ideal of projectives, rather than
to A itself. (This distinction is most pronounced in De Renzi’s presentation of the
singly extended non-semisimple Reshetikhin-Turaev theory [21]. See alternatively
[50].) This suggests, to us at least, that there may be some derived component
which is being truncated when we choose to approach non-semisimple topological
theories directly at the abelian level.
Remark 1.2. Though we’ve focused on Reshetikhin-Turaev type TQFTs, there are
also non-semisimple Turaev-Viro type theories which similarly leverage projectives
in an essential manner [23, 15].
1.2. Derived topological theories from twisted QFTs. Alongside the math-
ematical developments discussed above, a parallel story emerged in mathematical
physics. In the physical setting, the derived nature of TQFTs is seemingly com-
monplace.
   The clearest presentation of our case of interest, from a strict mathematical
perspective, appears in work of Creutzig, Dimofte, Garner, and Geer [17]. However,
the main physical principles used in [17] are adapted from a general theory of
twisting for supersymmetric QFTs which was developed in work of Witten from
the early 90’s [80]. For any N = 4 supersymmetric theory–whatever such a thing
might be–we have two distinguished topological twists, an A-twist and a B-twist
[46, 35]. Both twists are interesting to consider, and are presumably connected by
a type of Langlands duality. However, in the present text we are interested in the
A-side of this story.
4                                    CRIS NEGRON


   Having A-twisted our favorite N = 4 supersymmetric theory, the resulting topo-
logical theory is very exotic from the perspectives of, say, Reshetikhin-Turaev or
the cobordism hypothesis. It is not only derived in nature, but the state spaces
appear as sheaves on a stack of local systems. See for example [59, 34, 35].
   In Creutzig et al. the authors posit that the A-twist for a particular supersym-
metric N = 4 theory associated to SLn , in 3-dimensions, recovers a derived variant
of the unrolled non-semisimple TQFT produced by Blanchet, Costantino, Geer,
Patureau-Mirand, and De Renzi [6, 24]. Here the presence of local systems has to
do with the unrolled-ness of the quantum group, and after taking fibers at trivial
local systems we reduce to a derived variant of the non-semisimple Reshetikhin-
Turaev theory from De Renzi, Gainutdinov, Geer, Patureau-Mirand, and Runkel
[22].
   Thus, from [17] and the surrounding physics literature, we anticipate the follow-
ing: (a) There are derived topological field theories which are, at the very least,
associated to derived categories of small quantum group representations. (b) These
derived theories should recover pre-existing mathematical theories after a homo-
logical truncation. (c) They should also deform naturally along local systems to
produce topological field theories which “couple” to flat connections, again at least
in the quantum group setting.
   In the present study we realize points (a) and (b), or more precisely versions of
points (a) and (b), via explicit mathematical construction. Conjectural approaches
to point (c) are also discussed in Section 18.
   Let us now turn our attention to the contents of this text.

1.3. TQFTs from derived ∞-categories. Let A be a finite ribbon tensor cate-
gory which is defined over an algebraically closed field k, and let Df in = D b (Af in )
be its associated derived ∞-category of bounded, finite length complexes.
Remark 1.3. For us, all tensor categories are presentable (cocomplete), compactly
generated, and abelian. Taking the compacts, or rigid objects A ⇝ Af in recovers
a tensor category in the usual sense of, say, [2] or [26]. See Section 3.1.
   From such A and Df in we produce corresponding symmetric monoidal ∞-categories
of labeled, anomalous, non-compact 3-dimensional bordisms
              Bordnc      nc              nc          nc
                  A = Bord3,2 (A) and BordDf in = Bord3,2 (Df in ).

In the case of A, for example, the objects in this bordism category are surfaces
Σx with markings by objects x1 , . . . , xn in A. Morphisms Mα : Σx → Σ′y are
bordism between the underlying surfaces M : Σ → Σ′ which are equipped with,
loosely speaking, embedded cylinders which travel from the markings on Σ to the
markings on Σ′ . The terminal ends of these cylinders, on the outgoing boundary,
are furthermore marked by morphisms αj : mj (x) → yj between specified products
of the incoming labels to the outgoing labels (see Sections 2 and 9.2).
   To say things simply, the surfaces are decorated by objects in A and bordisms
are decorated by morphisms in A. The ∞-ness comes in as we allow the embed-
ded cylinders in bordisms to move within the ambient space M under homotopy.
This movement institutes certain universal skein relations into the bordism cate-
gory which acknowledge the ribbon structure on A. In the case of Df in , one has
homotopical contributions both from the geometry of the bordisms and from the
higher structure on Df in itself.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                         5


  More generally, for any “sufficiently ribbon-like monoidal ∞-category” E we
construct a bordism ∞-category BordncE of surfaces with markings by objects in E ,
and 3-dimensional cobordisms with markings by morphisms in E (Proposition 9.3).
The bordism categories BordncE are non-compact in two regards: In top dimension
we disallow 3-manifolds without boundary, and in dimension 2 we require that each
component of a surface is equipped with at least one marking from E (cf. [21] and
Section 9.4).
Theorem (15.1, 15.5). For any finite modular tensor category A, with finite derived
∞-category Df in = D b (Af in ), there is a symmetric monoidal functor

to the ∞-category of dg vector spaces. Furthermore, for any connected genus g
surface Σ, we have a natural identification of the state spaces

   To explain the notation in the expression (2), the object C in Af in ⊆ Df in
is the coadjoint object, which we define generally as the value C = mR(1) for
m : A ⊗k A → A the product and R : A → A ⊗k A its right adjoint. This object
is more commonly referred to as the canonical coend. Additionally, for D = D(A)
the full unbounded derived ∞-category, the bifunctor
                              MapsD : D op × D → V ect
is the inner-Hom functor for the natural action of V ect on D. These are, more
colloquially, the derived Homs for D as a linear ∞-category, and we recover the
Ext groups via cohomology

of finite complexes. Taking finally I to be the underlying marking set for our surface

Remark 1.4. A version of Theorem 15.1 can also be proved at the 1-categorical
level. We emphasize the homotopical setting due to our interest in an expanded
analysis of TQFTs for modular ∞-categories, in both dimensions 3 and 4. See
Theorem 11.1, and Section 11.4 for further elaboration.
   Though our non-compactness assumption requires the appearance of non-trivial
markings on surfaces in Bordnc
                            Df in , we show that all markings can be removed after
a homotopy truncation.

   From our description of the state spaces, we understand that the unmarked the-

Remark 1.5. The TQFT from [22] is arguably already non-compact, as it only
allows for closed 3-manifold which come equipped with certain embedded algebraic
decorations. In particular, its unmarked part is explicitly non-compact.
Remark 1.6. The “L” in LDf in is in reference to Lyubashenko, and we refer to
LDf in as the derived Lyubashenko-Reshetikhin-Turaev (LRT) theory.
Remark 1.7. We conjecture that the unmarked theory from Theorem 17.10 lifts
to a TQFT at the ∞-categorical level LDf in : Bordnc  3,2 → V ect, and furthermore
that the marked and unmarked theories can be integrated into a single object. The
removal of markings at the ∞-categorical level is a highly substantive point from
our perspective, and we hope to iterate on these constructions in future works. (See
Sections 2, 11.4, 18.)
  As a final mathematical point for the introduction, let us highlight some of the
outputs of our work in dimensions 2 and 3.

1.4. Phenomena in 2-dimensions. To gain some kind of understanding of, say,
the unmarked TQFT LDf in , one can consider its behaviors one surfaces and (punc-
tured) 3-manifolds. In dimension 2 we obtain a projective action of the mapping
class group MCG(Σ) on each state space LDf in (Σ).
   To elaborate, we have the subgroup MCG(Σ) of the non-anomalous autobordisms
of Σ which is generated by the mapping cylinders of oriented self-diffeomorphisms
Diff(Σ)+ → MCG(Σ) ⊆ Autnon-anom (Σ). Pulling back along the forgetful functor
from the anomalous bordism category, we obtain a central extension
                  ˜
         1 → Z → MCG(Σ)                ˜
                        → MCG(Σ) → 1, MCG(Σ) ⊆ AutBordnc  (Σ).
                                                      3,2
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        7


This central extension acts on Σ in Bordnc
                                         3,2 , and hence acts on the state space L(Σ)
for any field theory L : Bordnc
                             3,2 → S.
   In our case, we recall that the state spaces for LDf in are identified with coho-
mology for the input category A. So we obtain the following.
Corollary (17.13). Let A be a finite modular tensor category. For any connected
genus g surface Σ, the derived maps RHomA (C ⊗g , 1) carry the natural structure of
                                                             ˜
a dg representation for the projective mapping class group MCG(Σ).
   In genus 0 nothing interesting happens, as the mapping class group is trivial.
In genus 1, the mapping class group of the torus is SL2 (Z), and there is a canon-
ical identification of RHomA (C, 1) with the derived lift of Hochschild cohomology
RHomEndVect (A) (idA , idA ). Hence we obtain, after taking cohomology, a projective
action of SL2 (Z) on Hochschild cohomology HH ∗ (A).
   These kinds of projective mapping class group actions on cohomology were al-
ready observed in works of Lentner, Mierach, Schweigert, and Sommerhäuser [51],
and of Schweigert and Woike [72]. So, from the perspective of those texts, we
are saying that the mapping class group actions from [51, 72] arise naturally as
2-dimensional projection of a full 3-dimensional derived field theory for finite non-
semisimple modular tensor categories.
Remark 1.8. We have not checked explicitly that our mapping class group rep-
resentations reproduce those of [51, 72], though we certainly expect this to be the
case.
1.5. Phenomena in 3-dimensions. We continue our investigation of the theory
LDf in in dimension 3. For a connected closed 3-manifold M , we do not consider M
directly, but fix a surface Σ and excise the interiors of two chosen embeddings

                                 Σ                    Σ̄                         (4)
                                          &       x
                                              M
to obtain a bordism M̌ : Σ → Σ. (Here we assume that the embeddings from
Σ extend to some filling Σ of the surface.) The resulting bordism is uniquely
determined by M when Σ = S 2 for example, though this is not the case in higher
genus.
   The bordism M̌ obtained from such an arrangement (4) determines an endo-
morphism LDf in (M̌ ) : LDf in (Σ) → LDf in (Σ) and, to wash out some cochain-level
ambiguities, we take cohomology to get an endomorphism
                   H ∗ LDf in (M̌ ) : H ∗ LDf in (Σ) → H ∗ LDf in (Σ).
To wash out further ambiguities in the definition of LDf in itself, we also take a
determinant to obtain a power-series valued invariant
                      X
        Inv(A|M̌ ) :=    det(H n LDf in (M̌ ) − X · idH n ) · tn ∈ O(A1k )[[t]], (5)
                       n≥0

where X is a formal parameter and we’ve adopted the notation O(A1k ) = k[X].
   The invariant Inv(A|M̌ ) simply records the eigenvalues of the endomorphism
H n LDf in (M̌ ) in each degree. One can use this construction to obtain 3-manifold
invariants and also invariants of framed knots.
8                                   CRIS NEGRON


Proposition (19.4, 19.6). Let A be a modular tensor category. In genus 0, i.e.
when Σ = S 2 , the above construction produces power-series valued invariants of
3-manifolds Inv(A|M ) := Inv(A|M̌ ).
   In genus 1, taking M = S 3 , the above construction produces power-series valued
invariants of framed knots ν : S 1 → R3 ⊆ S 3 , Inv(A|ν) := Inv(A|Š 3 ).
   Though the construction Inv(A|M ) for 3-manifolds is clearly “derived”, and
also a natural output for any V ect-valued TQFT, we show that these invariants
are actually determined by their constant term in t, Inv(A|M ) = c0 (X) + O(t).
The constant term is furthermore a scalar which is already obtainable from the
corresponding abelian theory Labelian
                                   A      from [22]. So we do not truly access any
derived information in this case. (See Sections 19.2 and 19.3 below.)
   In genus 1 however, the resulting knot invariants do involve the higher Hochschild
cohomologies HH >0 (A), at least in principle. We discuss the situation for A =
(Repq SL2 )small in detail in Section 19.4, where a number of interesting points arise.

1.6. A world of homotopical TQFTs. From a strict mathematical perspective,
we have a generic interested in TQFTs for “modular” ∞-categories–a notion which
we’ll leave somewhat ambiguous for the moment. For any modular ∞-category A ,
we conjecture the existence of a 3-dimensional TQFT from marked and unmarked
bordism categories

                           A → V ect and LA : Bord3,2 → V ect
                  LA : Bordnc                     nc


which we furthermore conjecture extend down to closed curves, and recovers A as
the value on S 1 . We are also interested in homotopical field theories in dimension
4, skein modules for modular ∞-categories, and their connections to phenomena in
dimension 3. Examples of modular ∞-categories should include derived categories
of finite modular 1-categories, and presumably (quantized) derived categories of
sheaves on compact symplectic varieties.
   If we take this broader point of view, the conclusions of the present text verify
one explicit claim in this direction, or maybe a first such claim. We elaborate on
the topics proposed above, and also the integration of local systems, in Sections
11.4 and 18 below.

1.7. Some metacommentary. This paper is, to some extent, a re-articulation of
non-semisimple TQFT through the lense of ∞-categories. We understand, however,
that this perspective lies outside of the realm of expertise for many intended readers.
For this reason we have taken a number of steps to make the text more legible for
the homotopical non-expert.
   First, we provide specific referencing to the literature on ∞-categories through-
out. In our ideal moments, all homotopical arguments are directly supported by a
concrete simplicial analysis or an explicit reference. Second, we provide an exten-
sive review of basic principles for simplicial categories and ∞-categories–specifically
in the guise of weak Kan complexes–in Section 3. Cocartesian fibrations and the
un/straightening equivalence are given special attention. For the ambitious reader,
we hope that the paper is more-or-less self-contained.

1.8. Outline. Throughout the text A♡ generally denotes a ribbon (or modular)
tensor category, rather than the fraktur notation A employed above. We swap over
to our standard notation A♡ from this point on.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        9


   The theory LDf in is obtained from the corresponding abelian theory Labelian
                                                                           A♡     via
a somewhat subtle derivation process, the zeroth step of which already occurred in
preceding work with Agustina Czenky [20]. The first point for us is the construction
of a bordism category which is sufficiently responsive to basic manipulations within
the theory of higher algebra. We then apply a “vertical” localization to move from
the discrete setting to the homotopical setting, followed by a relative Kan extension
to move from bordisms over the homotopy ∞-category to the derived setting. The
subsequent removal of markings is a rather idiosyncratic process which has to do
with further localization along a class of rather poorly behaved morphisms. The
paper proceeds as follows:
     • Background. We discuss the efficacy of markings in topological field the-
       ories in Section 2, just to clarify some confusion around these issues before
       we begin our study in earnest. In Section 3 we discuss basic categorical
       backgrounds, and provide a brisk overview of ∞-categories. In Section
       4 we recall basic notions for symmetric monoidal ∞-categories, then in
       Sections 5 and 6 we introduce a symmetric monoidal ∞-category f rDisk
       of framed disks which classifies “compatible pairs” of “homotopically bal-
       anced monoidal” ∞-categories. In Section 7 we show that any ribbon tensor
       category A♡ , in the 1-categorical sense of the term, naturally determines
       f rDisk-categories (A♡ )⊗ → f rDisk⊗ and A⊗ = Ch(A♡ )⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
     • Bordism and discrete topological theories. We construct the triv-
       ially marked, 3-dimensional, non-compact, anomalous bordism ∞-category
       Bordnc∗ in Section 8. We subsequently construct the symmetric monoidal
       ∞-category Bordnc E of E -marked 3-dimensional bordisms in Section 9, where
       the markings come from any f rDisk-monoidal ∞-category E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
       We explicitly describe the A = Ch(A♡ )-marked bordism category associ-
       ated to any ribbon tensor category A♡ , then explain how results from
       [22, 20] provide a TQFT

                             LA : Bordnc
                                      A → Ch(Vect)                               (6)

        in Section 10. We derive the TQFT LA at the 1-categorical level, to pro-
        duce a symmetric monoidal functor from the bordism category Bordnc   D with
        labels from the discrete derived category D(A♡ ), in Section 11. We subse-
        quently discuss the process, and rationalle, for lifting to the ∞-categorical
        level.
     • Localization and homotopical theories. In Section 12 we present a
       robust method of localization for monoidal ∞-categories, and more gener-
       ally for cocartesian fibrations, along well-behaved classes of maps. Here we
       follow work of Hinich [43] in an essential way. We apply this general theory
       of localization to obtain from (6) a symmetric monoidal functor

                                        K → V ect
                               LK : Bordnc

        for the 3-dimensional bordism category with labels in the homotopy ∞-
        category K = K (A♡ ). As before, the target V ect is the derived ∞-
        category of linear cochains.
10                                 CRIS NEGRON


     • Kan extension and derived theories. We provide an analysis of Kan ex-
       tensions over a base in Section 14. Then, in Section 15, we apply Kan exten-

       physics literature on the subject, we further discuss couplings of derived
       field theories to moduli of local systems.

read the paper line-by-line in order to understand either the results or the argu-
ments. We suggest a particular reading of the paper, where one might skim certain
materials and read others more thoroughly, in recognition of the relative importance
of the details:

                        2. Remarks on marked bordisms
   Before beginning, let us make some preliminary remarks on marked surfaces,
decorated bordisms, and skein relations in general. This topic is not necessarily
introductory, but we hope it will preempt some confusion around the issue.

2.1. Why marked surfaces? We’ll list here three perspectives through which one
might conceptualize marked surfaces. The first is through the lense of conformal
field theory. Here we understand that a Reshetikhin-Turaev type theory (generally)
admits a conformal boundary. The state spaces in the topological bulk are then
identified with the conformal blocks for the boundary conformal theory. See for
example [32, 69].
    The conformal blocks in a conformal field theory naturally depend on insertions
of VOA modules at points on a given surface. These insertion points are furthermore
equipped with local coordinates. This is to say, we expect the state spaces in a
Reshetikhin-Turaev theory to accept markings by objects in the defining category,
and we expect the marking points to be equipped with such local coordinates. These
local coordinates can be thought of as specifying a collection of disk embeddings,
rather than embeddings of points.
    Alternatively, we can think of a Reshetikhin-Turaev theory itself as a boundary
theory to a once extended topological theory in dimensiona 4. This 4-dimensional
theory should be skein theoretic (cf. [10, 14, 39]), and objects in any surface category
12                                 CRIS NEGRON


for the 4-dimensional theory should, in particular, consist of choices of markings
on the given surface (see [10]). To say that the Reshetikhin-Turaev type theory
is defined for marked surface is then to say that we have well-defined values for
arbitrary choices of maps from the unit Vect to the factorization homology on sur-
faces. (In short, markings appear in Reshetikhin-Turaev theories because markings
appear in factorization homology.)
   For a final perspective, we note that any extended topological field theory Z :
Bord⋆3,2,1 → Prk , valued in presentable ∞-categories say, determines and is de-
termined by an associated fibration Bord⋆Z → Bord⋆3,2,1 . For Z = Z(S 1 ), the
(∞, 2)-category Bord⋆Z is already explicitly constructible as an (∞, 2)-category of
Z -marked bordisms (cf. Section B.2). This (∞, 2)-category restricts, in various
ways, to define ∞-categories of surfaces with embedded Z -labeled disks and 3-
manifolds which are labeled–in one way or another–by morphisms in Z . Hence a
TQFT from a sufficiently robust bordism category for marked surfaces is closely
related to the existence of an extended topological field theory.

2.2. Extending into 3-manifolds. Having decided that we accept marked sur-
faces, one has some decisions to make regarding bordisms between such marked
surfaces Mα : Σx → Σ′y . Specifically, what kinds of decorations should we have in
M which connect the markings on the incoming boundary Σ to the markings on
the outgoing boundary Σ′ ?
   In this text we take a fairly minimalist approach to this question, and employ
a minimalist solution. In particular, we construct (in Section 9) a category of
bordisms Bordnc E with embedded cylinders which travel from the markings on the
incoming boundary to markings on the outgoing boundary. We later localize to
obtain a coarse approximation to bordisms with embedded skein diagrams (see
Section 16). The minimal skein relations which appear in this process are sufficient
to facilitate an unmarking procedure in Section 17, which leads to an unmarked
TQFT LDf in : Bordnc3,2 → D(Vect) in Theorem 17.10.
   A maximalist approach to this question would seek a truly skein theoretic real-
ization of bordisms. A “perfect” notion of skeins should facilitate a calculation of
factorization homology over surfaces for ribbon ∞-categories, as in the 1-categorical
setting [10].

2.3. Skein modules? The production of true skeins, or “perfect skeins”, for ribbon
∞-categories is, from our perspective, a deep question which warrants independent
consideration. One might define a ribbon ∞-category efficiently as an (anomalous)
2-dimensional topological field theory A valued in linear presentable ∞-categories
Prk [62, 73]. Here we would work specifically with the straightened fibration over
a 2-dimensional bordism category.
   While the author would state, somewhat emphatically, that they do not know
how to construct skein modules for ribbon ∞-categories, the proposed definition of
ribbon ∞-categories itself suggests certain constraints which one might attend to.
For example, rather than considering skeins as embedded graphs in a 3-manifold,
we might think of skeins as embedded anomalous surfaces in a manifold. Here
by an anomalous surface we specifically mean a surface with a chosen bounding
3-manifold, and we should assume that the bounding 3-manifold is “trivial” (e.g. a
ball in the case of S 2 and a doughnut in the case of the torus). One considers such
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                         13


an embedded surface skein, let us call it, along with its vector space obtained by
evaluating under the given field theory for our modular ∞-category A .
   Skein relations in this surface approach may then be facilitated by embedded
bordisms between such embedded surfaces, and so leverage some aspects of a
3-dimensional field theory for A . So, practically speaking, a perfect theory of
skeins and skein relations for a modular ∞-category may be contingent on the
pre-existence of a 3-d field theory from a somewhat restrictive marked bordism cat-
egory. In any case, this topic is completely open, and we encourage the interested
reader to consider this line of inquiry, even returning to the 1-categorical setting if
necessary.
Remark 2.1. See Section 18 for additional discussions of skeins and 4-dimensional
TQFTs, in the derived setting.
Remark 2.2. In this text we model ribbon tensor categories in the ∞-categorical
context by coupled pairs of framed E2 -categories, rather than by 2-dimensional
category valued TQFTs. This choice is purely practical, or even superficial, and
can be altered in accordance with what’s available at the 1-categorical level. (See
Section 9.4.)

                                3. (∞-)Categories
   To begin our study, we provide a brisk overview of tensor categories, simplicial
categories, ∞-categories, Kan complexes, and cocartesian fibrations. This is mostly
to set notations, and also to provide a coarse reference for the non-expert. For a
detailed account we suggest Kerodon [58].

3.1. Tensor categories. We follow the conventions of [64]. So, for us, a tensor
category is a linear presentable, compactly generated abelian monoidal category
for which the subcategories of compact and rigid objects agree, and for which the
compacts form a tensor category in the sense of [26]. So, all compact objects are of
finite length, have finite-dimensional Homs, and the unit is simple. We also require
that the product commutes with small colimits in each factor, and assume that the
base field k is algebraically closed. Via Ind-completion, and taking the compacts,
one can go back and forth between our tensor categories and those of [26].
   For a tensor category B we let Bf in denote the full subcategory of finite length
= rigid objects. We call a tensor category finite if it admits a compact projective
generator, in which case B is equivalent to the category of modules over a finite-
dimensional algebra. We have the standard notions of braided and ribbon tensor
categories (see [26]).
Definition 3.1. A tensor category B is called ribbon if it comes equipped with a
braiding c and balancing transformation θ [26, Definition 8.10.1] for which θx∗ = θx∗
whenever x is rigid. We call a finite ribbon tensor category modular if, in addition,
any object x with trivial double braiding
                                 c−,x ◦ cx,− = idx⊗−
is isomorphic to an additive power of the unit x ∼
                                                 = 1⊕Λ .
  Throughout all “modular” tensor categories are assumed to be finite.
14                                  CRIS NEGRON


3.2. Simplicial sets and simplicial categories. For ∆ the category of finite
linearly ordered sets and (weakly) order preserving morphisms, we let Set∆ denote
the category of functors Fun(∆op , Set). This is the category of simplicial sets.
For any finite linearly ordered set I we let ∆I denote the standard simplex ∆I =
Hom∆ (−, I). For each n ∈ Z≥0 we take [n] = {0, 1, . . . , n} and also ∆n = ∆[n] . For
any simplicial set K we let K[n] denote the value K([n]), and we note that there
is a bijection
                                              ∼
               {maps σ : ∆n → K in Set∆ } → K[n], σ 7→ σ(id[n] ),
by Yoneda.
   The symmetric monoidal structure on Set, under the cartesian product, induces a
symmetric monoidal structure on the category Set∆ of simplicial sets. The category
Set∆ is also complete and cocomplete, with (co)limits calculated via (co)limits in
the target category Set.
   Each object S in Set becomes a simplicial set via the constant functor S : ∆op →
Set and this gives a fully faithful, symmetric monoidal embedding
                                    Set ,→ Set∆ .
In this way our discussions of simplicial sets, and simplicial categories, specialize
to discussions of sets and plain old categories.
Definition 3.2. A simplicial category C is a category enriched in Set∆ and a
simplicial functor is a functor between Set∆ -enriched categories.
  For each n and index 0 ≤ i ≤ n we let Λni ⊆ ∆n denote the simplicial subset
whose simplices ∆m → Λni are precisely those simplices in ∆n which factor through
some face ∆I ⊆ ∆n with I ⊆ [n] and [n] − {i} ⊈ I. We call Λni the i-th horn in ∆n .
Definition 3.3. A map of simplicial sets X → S is called a Kan fibration if each
lifting problem
                                 Λni      /X                                 (7)
                                           =

                                               
                                    ∆n       / S,
with n arbitrary and 0 ≤ i ≤ n, admits a solution. We call a simplicial set X
a Kan complex if the unique map X → ∗ to the terminal simplicial set is a Kan
fibration. A morphism between Kan complexes is just a map of simplicial sets.
   To clarify our language, X → S is a Kan fibration if and only if each commuting
square as in (7) admits a map ∆n → X which split the diagram into two commuting
triangles. The terminal simplicial set ∗ is, as our notation suggests, the constant
simplicial set associated to the (or rather, any) singleton.
Remark 3.4. The singular complex functor Sing : Top → Kan provides a (Quillen)
equivalence between compactly generated, locally Hausdorff topological spaces and
Kan complexes. The inverse is given by geometric realization. For this reason
people often refer to Kan complexes as Spaces.
Definition 3.5. A simplicial category C is called fibrant if, for each pair of objects
x and y in C, the Hom complex HomC (x, y) is a Kan complex.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        15


  We note that any simplicial category C has an underlying plain old category C
whose morphisms are the 0-simplices
                     HomC (x, y) := HomSet∆ (∆0 , HomC (x, y)).

3.3. ∞-Categories.
Definition 3.6. A map of simplicial sets F : C → T is called an inner fibration if
any lifting problem
                                Λni        /C                                 (8)
                                           =
                                                   F
                                                  
                                     ∆n          /T,
with n arbitrary and 0 < i < n, admits a solution. We call C an ∞-category if the
map C → ∗ to the terminal simplicial set is an inner fibration. A functor between
∞-categories is just a map of simplicial sets.
   Note that the lifting problems here (8) only involve inner horns, i.e. the horns
Λni → ∆n with i between 1 and n − 1. For an ∞-category C , an object in C is a
0-simplex x : ∆0 → C , a morphism is a 1-simplex α : ∆1 → C , and for any two
maps α01 : x0 → x1 and α12 : x1 → x2 a composite is any third map α02 : x0 → x2
which fits into a 2-simplex

                                          = x1
                                    α01           α12


                               x0                      / x! 2 .
                                          α02

We note that the composite is not uniquely determined in a strict sense, but it
is determined up to a contractible space of choices [58, 01BW]. So, whenever such
ambiguities are acceptable, we might speak of “the” composite α02 and write simply
α02 = α12 α01 .
Definition 3.7. Given an object x in an ∞-category C , the identity map idx :
x → x is the unique degnerate 1-simplex idx : ∆1 → C with idx |{0} = idx |{1} = x.
An isomorphism in C is a map α : x → y which admits another map β : x → y for
which we have composites αβ = idy and βα = idx .
    An (∞-)subcategory in an ∞-category C is a simplicial subset C ′ ⊆ C in which a
given n-simplex σ : ∆n → C lies in C ′ if and only if each object σ|{i} and each edge
σ|∆{i,j} lies in C ′ . We assume additionally that each composable pair or maps in
C ′ [1] admits a composition in C ′ [1]. A subcategory is called full if each morphism
∆1 → C between objects in C ′ is also in C ′ .
Definition 3.8. Given a collection of objects X0 in an ∞-category C , the full
subcategory spanned by X0 in C is the unique full subcategory C ′ ⊆ C with
C ′ [0] = X0 . Given a collection of objects X0 in C and morphisms X1 between
objects in X0 which are stable under composition, the subcategory spanned by the
Xi in C is the unique subcategory C ′ ⊆ C with C ′ [0] = X0 and C ′ [1] = X1 .
  One checks directly that any subcategory C ′ in an ∞-category C is itself an
∞-category.
16                                  CRIS NEGRON


Proposition 3.9 ([58, 01H1]). For any ∞-category C the subcategory C Kan spanned
by all objects, but only isomorphisms between objects, is a Kan complex.
    The simplicial set C Kan is usually called the core of C , though we will just call
it the associated Kan complex.

3.4. Functor categories and mapping spaces. For any ∞-category C and sim-
plicial set K the complex of simplicial maps
                Fun(K, C ), Fun(K, C )[n] = HomSet∆ (∆n × K, C ),
is an ∞-category, and for any map of simplicial sets L → K the restriction functor
                              Fun(K, C ) → Fun(L, C )
is an inner fibration [58, 01BT]. For objects x, y : ∗ → C we obtain the standard
mapping complex via pullback

                          MapsC (x, y)          / Fun(∆1 , C )

                                                        restrict
                                                     
                              ∗⨿∗              / Fun(∂∆1 , C ).
                                       [x y]

As the pullback of any kind of “fibration” is again a fibration of the same type, we
understand that MapsC (x, y) is an ∞-category at all x and y. Since all morphisms
in MapsC (x, y) are isomorphisms [58, 01DK], this ∞-category is furthermore a Kan
complex.
Definition 3.10. For objects in an ∞-category x, y : ∗ → C , the associated
mapping space MapsC (x, y) is the Kan complex constructed as above.
   We note that, in general, the mapping spaces do not admit strict composition
functors. We do however have composition functors
               ◦ : MapsC (x1 , x2 ) × MapsC (x0 , x1 ) → MapsC (x0 , x2 )
which are well-defined up to homotopy [58, 01PQ].

3.5. Isofibrations.
Definition 3.11. An isofibration between ∞-categories is an inner fibration F :
C → D which satisfies either of the following (equivalent) lifting conditions:
     • For each isomorphism ᾱ : x̄ → ȳ in D, and object x in C with F (x) = x̄,
       there is an isomorphism α : x → y in C with F (α) = ᾱ.
     • For each isomorphism ᾱ : x̄ → ȳ in D, and object y in C with F (y) = ȳ,
       there is an isomorphism α : x → y in C with F (α) = ᾱ.
   Isofibrations are the “good” maps between ∞-categories, especially when one
wants to take limits of ∞-categories in a homotopically responsible manner. See
for example [58, 03GF, 03H3, 033P].
Remark 3.12. Isofibrations are also referred to as categorical fibrations in the
literature. Compare for example [56] to [11, 58].
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                       17


Remark 3.13. We’ve obviously chosen to work with “quasi-categories”, or “weak
Kan complexes” as our preferred model for ∞-categories. Though we are not con-
cerned with the sinful nature of such choices, there are practical points to consider
here. In particular, if we want to embed our analysis of 3-d TQFTs within a
larger study of extended field theories, complete Segal spaces are likely preferable
to quasi-categories as a model for ∞-categories.

3.6. Homotopy coherent nerves. From any simplicial category C one has an
associated simplicial set N(C) which is produced via a construction called the ho-
motopy coherent nerve. Simplices in the nerve are given by functors

                         N(C )[n] = FunCat∆ (Path ∆n , C)

from a certain path category construction [58, 00KN, 00KS]. We have Path ∆0 the
one point category and Path ∆1 the category with two objects, trivial endomor-
phisms, and a single map between them. Thus the 0 and 1-simplices in the nerve
consist of objects and maps in the underlying plain category C for C. A 2-simplex
in the nerve consists of a noncommuting diagram

                                        = x1
                                    f          g


                               x0                  / !x2
                                         h


in the plain category C, along with a choice of a 1-simplex ζ : ∆1 → HomC (x0 , x2 )
for which ζ|{0} = gf and ζ|{1} = h.
   The nerve construction is functorial in the sense that any simplicial functor
F : C → D determines a map of simplicial sets N(F ) : N(C) → N(D), and for any
composite of simplicial functors we have N(G) ◦ N(F ) = N(G ◦ F )

Theorem 3.14 ([13]). If C is a fibrant simplicial category (i.e. is enriched in Kan
complexes) then the homotopy coherent nerve N(C) is an ∞-category.

   Recall that we have the symmetric embedding Set ,→ Set∆ so that plain cat-
egories embed fully faithfully into simplicial categories. In the case where C
is a plain category the nerve N(C) has n-simplices specified by tuples of maps
{fij : 0 ≤ i < j ≤ n}, fij : xi → xj , with fjk fij = fik whenever i ≤ j ≤ k. The
∞-category C = N(C) has the property that any inner horn Λni → C admits a
unique filling to an n-simplex ∆n → C , and any ∞-category C with such unique
horn fillings is of the form C = N(C) for uniquely determined C.

Definition 3.15. We call an ∞-category C discrete if, for each n and 0 < i < n,
the restriction functor Fun(∆n , C ) → Fun(Λni , C ) is an isomorphism of simplicial
sets.

Example 3.16 (Kan complexes). For Kan complexes X and Y the functor com-
plexes Fun(X , Y ) are all Kan complexes [58, 00TN]. We therefore obtain a corre-
sponding fibrant simplicial category Kan of Kan complexes. We take the homotopy
coherent nerve to obtain the ∞-category of K an = N(Kan) of Kan complexes.
18                                  CRIS NEGRON


  As we explained above in the generic setting, objects in K an are Kan complexes
and morphisms are maps of Kan complexes, i.e. maps of simplicial sets. A 2-simplex

                                         = X1
                                   f01          f12

                                                   !
                              X0                  / X2
                                         f02

in K an consists of choices of maps between Kan complexes and an additional
homotopy ζ : ∆1 × X0 → X2 from f12 f01 to f02 . A map between Kan complexes
f : X → Y is an isomorphism in K an if and only if it is a homotopy equivalence,
in the expected sense [58, 00U1].
Definition 3.17. A Kan complex X is called contractible if the unique map X →
∗ is a homotopy equivalence.
Example 3.18 (∞-categories). For ∞-categories the functor complex Fun(C , D) is
not a Kan complex in general. However, we can take the associated Kan complexes
                                                         ∞ . The nerve Cat∞ =
Fun(C , D)Kan to obtain a fibrant simplicial category Cat+
N(Cat+∞ ) is the ∞-category of ∞-categories.

    Let C and D be ∞-categories. A transformation ζ : ∆1 × C → D between func-
tors F0 and F1 is a natural isomorphism in Cat∞ , and hence lies in Fun(C , D)Kan ,
if and only if it evaluates to an isomorphism ζx : F0 (x) → F1 (x) at each 0-simplex
x in C [58, 01DK]. So again, 0, 1, and 2-simplices in Cat∞ can be understood in
concrete terms.
Example 3.19 (Plain categories). For plain categories, the functor category of
the nerves Fun(N(C), N(D)) is just the nerve of the usual category N(Fun(C, D)) of
functors and natural transformations. The associated Kan complex N(Fun(C, D))Kan
is the nerve N(Fun(C, D)nat.isom ) of the groupoid of functors and natural isomor-
phisms. In any case, we take the associated Kan complexes of the functor categories
to get a fibrant simplicial category Cat+ of plain categories, and take the homotopy
coherent nerve to get the ∞-category Cat = N(Cat) of plain categories.
   Note that Cat sits in Cat∞ as the full subcategory spanned by discrete ∞-
categories, and K an sits in Cat∞ as the full subcategory spanned by Kan com-
plexes. Also, we can consider the underlying plain categories Kan and Cat∞ , of
Kan complexes and ∞-categories in Kan and Cat∞ respectively. Applying the
homotopy coherent nerve, we obtain inclusions of ∞-categories
                    N(Kan) ,→ K an and N(Cat∞ ) ,→ Cat∞ .
Remark 3.20. We have ignored all size constraints in the above examples. For-
mally, we fix a sequence of universes
                       Small ⊆ Medium ⊆ Large ⊆ Huge
in order to speak of small, medium sized, large, and huge simplicial sets. We
therefore have small, medium sized, large, and huge ∞-categories which are all
weak Kan complexes in their respective universes. The above examples are all
large ∞-categories. Your run-of-the-mill ∞-category is assumed to be of a medium
size.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        19


Definition 3.21. A functor between ∞-categories F : C → D is an equivalence
if it is an isomorphism in Cat∞ . More directly, F is an equivalence if there exists
                                                                 ∼                ∼
a functor F ′ : D → C which admits natural isomorphisms F ′ F → idC and idD →
     ′
FF .
   The following result is fundamental, and is used ad nauseam throughout the
text.
Lemma 3.22. Given an equivalence of ∞-categories p : S → T and a pullback
diagram
                              C
                                   F  /D
                                                       q
                                                  
                                    S            /T
                                             p

in Cat∞ , the map F is an equivalence provided one of p or q is an isofibration.
   We note that, for any pair of maps pi : Ci → T in Cat∞ , the pullback of
simplicial sets C0 ×T C1 is automatically an ∞-category whenever one of the pi
is an inner fibration. So, in almost-all situations, pullbacks in the plain category
Cat∞ are just pullbacks in Set∆ .
Proof of Lemma 3.22. If one of p of q is an isofibration then the pullback diagram is
a categorical pullback diagram, in the sense of [58, 032Y]. Hence the result follows
by [58, 033M].                                                                     □
3.7. Language: Discrete (∞−)categories. From this point on we don’t distin-
guish between a plain category and its nerve. A functor F : C → D from an
∞-category to a plain category, or vise-versa, is always understood to be a functor
from C to the nerve of D, or vise-versa. By a discrete category we either mean a
discrete ∞-category, or just a plain category, depending on the context.
Notation 3.23. A diagram in an ∞-category C is a map d : K → C from a
simplicial set K. When C is the nerve of a fibrant simplicial category C = N(C),
and C is the underling discrete/plain category for C, a diagram d : K → C is said
to strictly commute if d factors through the simplicial inclusion C → C ,

                                K
                                             d             /8 C
                                    ∃!   &
                                             C               .
   When we speak of diagrams in an ∞-category K → C we usually assume K is
a discrete category, so that a diagram in C is exactly what one expects intuitively.
We most often speak of strict commutativity in the cases where C = Cat∞ or K an.
3.8. Nerves of strict 2-categories. In the case of a plain category C, we saw that
there is no interesting information in an n-simplex ∆n → N(C) above dimension
1. One similarly finds that, for a 2-category, an n-simplex in the nerve is nothing
more than a compatible collection of 2-simplices. Let us elaborate.
   By a strict 2-category we mean a category enriched in plain categories. Taking
the nerves on morphisms, we can view strict 2-categories as precisely those simplicial
categories whose Hom complexes are all discrete ∞-categories. A strict 2-category
C is fibrant, when considered as a simplicial category, if and only if C is enriched
in groupoids.
20                                    CRIS NEGRON


Lemma 3.24. Let C be a strict 2-category. An n-simplex in the nerve σ : ∆n →
N(C) consists of the following data:
     • A choice of an object xi for each index i = 1, . . . , n.
     • For each pair of indices 0 ≤ i < j ≤ n, a choice of map αij : xi → xj in
       the underlying discrete category C.
     • For each triple 0 ≤ i < j < k ≤ n, the choice of a 2-morphism ζijk :
       αjk αij → αik .
These data are (only) required to satisfy the constraint
               ζikl (idαkl ◦ ζijk ) = ζijl (ζjkl ◦ idαij ) : αkl αjk αij → αil
for each quadruple 0 ≤ i < j < k < l ≤ n.
Proof. The above data specify an n-simplex in the Duskin nerve [58, 009T], and the
Duskin nerve is identified with the homotopy coherent nerve in the strict setting
[58, 00KY].                                                                     □

3.9. The homotopy category of an ∞-category. Given an ∞-category C , the
homotopy category h C is the discrete category with the same objects as C and
morphisms given by equivalence classes [α] : x → y of maps in C . Here α is
equivalent to α′ whenever there is a diagram of the form
                                                α′   /y
                                           x
                                     idx                      idy
                                                    /  y
                                           x    α

in C . This relation is stable under composition [58, 0048], so that h C is in fact a
discrete category.
   Equivalently, the homotopy category h C is defined by taking the same objects
as C and morphisms given by the connected components
                                                         
                          Homh C (x, y) = π0 MapsC (x, y) .
The homotopy category construction is functorial, and provides a left adjoint to
the inclusion Cat → Cat∞ .

3.10. Cocartesian fibrations and straightening.
Definition 3.25. Let q : C → T be a functor between ∞-categories. A morphism
α : x → y in C is called q-cocartesian if any lifting problem of the form

                                      Λn0
                                                σ̄    /C
                                                      =
                                                               q
                                                      
                                      ∆n             /T,

with n > 1 and specified initial edge σ̄|∆{0,1} = α, admits a solution. An inner
fibration q : C → T is called a cocartesian fibration if, for any map ᾱ : x̄ → ȳ in
T and any x in C with q(x) = x̄, there is a q-cocartesian morphism α : x → y in
C with q(α) = ᾱ.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                                    21


   One can argue directly that cocartesian lifts of morphisms are unique up to
isomorphism in Fun(∆1 , C ). In particular, for a fixed functor q : C → T , any two
q-cocartesian lifts α : x → y and α′ : x → y ′ of a given map ᾱ : x̄ → ȳ in the base
T fit into a diagram

                         @y                                                  @ ȳ
                     α              β                                   ᾱ          id
                                                       over
                                                                                        
                 x                          / y′                   x̄                    / ȳ
                             ′                                                ᾱ
                         α


in which β is an isomorphism. In fact, such lifts are unique up to a contractible
space of choices [58, 01VK].

Definition 3.26. A map of cocartesian fibrations is a strictly commuting diagram

                                     C
                                                       F           /D

                                                   q           p
                                                           ~
                                                       T

in which q and p are cocartesian, and in which F sends q-cocartesian edges in C
to p-cocartesian edges in D. We call such a map F an equivalence of cocartesian
fibrations if, after forgetting the base T , it is an equivalence of ∞-categories.

Remark 3.27. Any equivalence between cocartesian fibrations does admit an in-
verse over the base [58, 0285, 028B]. Furthermore, there is an ∞-category of cocarte-
sian fibrations over T in which the isomorphisms are precisely the equivalences, as
defined above (see Section C.1).

   Cocartesian fibrations serve, arguably, as the leverage point through which the
theory of ∞-categories functions. Essentially, every “thing” in Cat∞ is a cocartesian
(or cartesian) fibration over a specified base. We take a moment to discuss these
structures in more detail.

Notation 3.28. Given an inner fibration q : C → T and an object t : ∗ → T , we
let Ct denote the associated fiber Ct = {t} ×T C .

   We note that the fibers along any inner fibration are all ∞-categories, since inner
fibrations are stable under pullback. Of course, at the moment we are especially
interested in the case where the given map is a cocartesian fibration.
   For any cocartesian fibration q : C → T and any simplicial set K, the induced
functor
                                 q∗ : Fun(K, C ) → Fun(K, T )

is a cocartesian fibration as well. The q∗ -cocartesian edges in Fun(K, C ) are pre-
cisely those transformations ζ : ∆1 ×K → C which evaluate to a q-cocartesian edge
ζx : ∆1 → C at each 0-simplex x in K [58, 01VG]. Hence, for any map f : s → t in
T and corresponding functor f˜ = f p1 : ∆1 ×Cs → T , there is a unique cocartesian
22                                  CRIS NEGRON


transformation Tf : ∆1 × Cs → C which solves the lifting problem

                          Cs = {0} × Cs
                                                include                /6 C

                                                                              q
                                                                        
                             ∆1 × Cs                                   /T.
                                                  f˜


Evaluating this transformation at 1 in ∆1 , we “travel along f ” to obtain a transport
functor between the fibers f! : Cs → Ct . One sees, via uniqueness of cocartesian
lifts, that any sequence of maps gf : s → t → u in the base produces a diagram

                                         > Ct
                                    f!                     g!


                               Cs                               / Cu
                                         (gf )!


in Cat∞ .
   Taking this concept to its extreme, we find that any cocartesian fibration q :
C → T determines an associated functor St(q) : T → Cat∞ with Stq (s) = Cs ,
Stq (f ) = f! , etc. It turns out that the fibration q : C → T is also reconstructible
from its associated functor. Indeed, Grothendieck straightening tells us that the
assignment q 7→ St(q) extends to an equivalence
                                           ∼
                         St : Cocart(T ) → Fun(T , Cat∞ )

from an ∞-category of cocartesian fibrations over a given base T (see Section C.1)
to the ∞-category of Cat∞ -valued functors from T [56, 42, 12]. The inverse
                                                       ∼
                         Un : Fun(T , Cat∞ ) → Cocart(T )

is provided by pulling back along the universal fibration quniv : PCat∞ → Cat∞ of
pointed ∞-categories [58, 0205, 028K].
   Now, when we think of a “structured” (∞-)category C , like a monoidal (∞-
)category for example, we are usually thinking about a functor FC : T → Cat∞
from some specific category T which universally encodes a collection of functors and
transformations which constitute the given structure. Hence, from the perspective
of Grothendieck straightening and unstraightening, we now have two options: We
can think of such structured ∞-categories as a distinguished class of cocartesian
fibrations over T , or as a class of functors to Cat∞ .
   Though there is some utility in working on both sides of the straightening-
unstraightening equivalence, it is often preferable to work with cocartesian fibra-
tions rather than Cat∞ -valued functors. The reason is, arguably, that a cocartesian
fibration C → T contains less erroneous information than its associated functor to
Cat∞ .

Remark 3.29. There are some size constraints to consider here, all of which we’ve
ignored. For example, the total space C in a cocartesian fibrations C → T is
allowed to be large, but all of the fibers Ct are assumed to be of a medium size.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                       23


3.11. Functor categories for cocartesian fibrations. Consider inner fibrations
q : C → T and p : D → T . The structure map on D induces an inner fibration
p∗ : Fun(C , D) → Fun(C , T ) [58, 01BV] and we take the fiber along q to obtain
an ∞-category
                   FunT (C , D) := {q} ×Fun(C ,T ) Fun(C , D).
Objects in this ∞-category are functors over T , morphisms are transformations
over T , and general n-simplices are strictly commuting diagrams

                             ∆n × C
                                           σ               /D

                                    qp2                p
                                           $       ~
                                               T            .
Definition 3.30. For cocartesian fibrations as above, we take

                              T (C , D) ⊆ FunT (C , D)
                           Funcc
the full subcategory spanned by those functors which send q-cocartesian edges in
C to p-cocartesian edges in D.

                    4. Symmetric monoidal ∞-categories
   We recall basic information regarding symmetric monoidal ∞-categories. We
are especially interested in the transition from symmetric monoidal simplicial cate-
gories to symmetric monoidal ∞-categories. We also discuss equifibered symmetric
monoidal ∞-categories which, to a large extent, replace ∞-operads in our study
[41, 3].

4.1. Finite pointed sets.
Definition 4.1. The category of finite pointed sets Fin∗ is the category of finite
sets, along with morphisms f¯ : I → J given by the choice of a subset If¯ ⊆ I and
a set map If¯ → J. Given such a map f¯ : I → J in Fin∗ , we let Ij denote the
preimage f¯−1 (j) ⊆ I of an element j ∈ J.
    By a slight abuse of notation we let f¯ : If¯ → J denote the set map underlying
a map of finite pointed sets f¯ : I → J. The composition of maps f¯ : I → J and
ḡ : J → K in Fin∗ is defined by taking the subset Iḡf¯ = f¯−1 (Jḡ ) along with the
underlying set map
                               ḡ f¯|Iḡf¯ : Iḡf¯ → Jḡ → K.

Definition 4.2. A map of finite pointed sets f¯ : I → J is called active if If¯ = I,
and called inert if each preimage Ij = f¯−1 (j) is a singleton. For any index i in I
we let ρ̄i : I → {0} denote the unique inert morphism with preimage ρ̄−1i (0) = {i}.

   Fibrations over Fin∗ play a prominent role in higher algebra. We note that any
functor to Fin∗ is automatically an inner fibration, since Fin∗ is discrete.
Definition 4.3. Given a functor q : C → Fin∗ , a morphism f : x → y in C is
called inert if it is q-cocartesian and the image q(f ) is inert in Fin∗ . We call f
active if it has active image in Fin∗ .
24                                     CRIS NEGRON


4.2. Symmetric monoidal ∞-categories.
Definition 4.4. A symmetric monoidal ∞-category is a cocartesian fibration E ⊗ →
Fin∗ for which transport along the inert projections ρ̄i : I → {0} induce an equiv-
alence
                                          ∼
                                 ρ! : EI⊗ → E I .                               (9)
A map of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories is a map of cocartesian fibrations

                               E⊗
                                            F           / D⊗


                                          "   |
                                          Fin∗                ,
i.e. a functor over Fin∗ which preserves cocartesian edges.
  In the expression (9) I is an arbitrary finite set, E is the fiber over the singleton
      ⊗
E = E{0} , and E I is the exponent E I = Fun(I, E ).
Remark 4.5. We employ no special notation for ∞-categories of symmetric monoidal
functors, as the generic cocartesian functor category
                                        Fin∗ (E , D )
                                               ⊗   ⊗
                                     Funcc
from Section 3.11 already parametrizes maps of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories.
Remark 4.6. Given any symmetric monoidal ∞-category E ⊗ → Fin∗ , we note
that the fiber over the empty set E∅⊗ is a contractible Kan complex. This follows
by terminality of the empty product in Cat∞ and the requisite equivalence (9).
Hence the transport functor along the unique active map m̄ : {1, 2} → {0} provides
a homotopically associative, and homotopically commutative product functor
                          mE = m̄! : E × E ∼= E⊗ → E  {1,2}

and transport along the initial map ū : ∅ → {0} provides a unit for this product
                              uE = ū! : ∗ ∼
                                           = E⊗ → E.
                                                  ∅
  For any map of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories F ⊗ : E ⊗ → D ⊗ , compatibility
between transport functors ensures the existence of homotopy commuting diagrams

               E ×E
                         F2   / D ×D                              ∗
                                                           uE         uD
               mE                   mD     and
                                                                      
                    E          /D                      E               / D.
                         F                                        F

The existence of higher compatibilities, up to arbitrary depth, is articulated by
the corresponding transformation between the functors FE ⊗ , FD ⊗ : Fin∗ → Cat∞
under straightening.
  As one expects, the homotopy truncation of any symmetric monoidal ∞-category
remains symmetric monoidal.
Lemma 4.7. For any symmetric monoidal ∞-category p : E ⊗ → Fin∗ , the induced
map from the homotopy category h p : h E ⊗ → Fin∗ is also a symmetric monoidal
∞-category. We have the fibers (h E ⊗ )I = h(EI⊗ ), and the transport functors f¯! :
h EI⊗ → h EJ⊗ are induced by the transport functors for E ⊗ .
             TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                         25


Proof. It is immediate to see that every p-cocartesian edge in E ⊗ is h p-cocartesian
in h E ⊗ . Hence the fibration h E ⊗ → Fin∗ is cocartesian and the claim about
                                                                               ∼
transport follows as well. Since transport provides an equivalence ρ̄! : EI⊗ → E I
for each finite set I, by assumption, the induced map on homotopy categories
h EI⊗ → h E I is also an equivalence.                                               □
  One obtains examples of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories immediately from
both the discrete and simplicial settings. We provide details below.
4.3. Symmetric monoidal simplicial categories. A monoidal simplicial cate-
gory is a simplicial category E equipped with a simplicial functor
                                      ⊗:E×E →E
along with a unit 1, unit isomorphisms, and associativity. A symmetric structure
                                                                            ∼
on a simplicial monoidal category is a choice of a natural isomorphism ⊗ → ⊗op
which is an involution and satisfies the appropriate compatibilities with the unit
and associator.
Definition 4.8. For a symmetric monoidal simplicial category E we define a sim-
plicial category E ⊗ whose objects are given by pairs of a finite set I equipped with a
linear ordering and an object x in the product E I . The mapping complex between
objects x and y over finite sets I and J, with specified orderings, is the disjoint
union of the complexes
                                          Y
                        HomE ⊗ (x, y)f¯ =     HomE (xIj , yj )                     (10)
                                                j∈J

over all f¯ : I → J in Fun∗ .
  Here each xIj denotes the product
                 xi1 ⊗ · · · ⊗ xir := xi1 ⊗ (xi2 ⊗ · · · (xir−1 ⊗ xir ) · · · ),
where Ij = {i1 , . . . , ir } is ordered according to the ambient ordering on I. In the
case when Ij is empty we take xIj = 1E . We note that the map of pointed sets
f¯ : I → J underlying a map in E ⊗ is not required to respect the given orderings,
even though the sets which parametrize objects in E ⊗ are ordered.
    Composition is defined in the expected way, though it is a slightly delicate thing
to write down. Consider n-simplices
              σ : ∆n → HomE ⊗ (x, y)f¯ and σ ′ : ∆n → HomE ⊗ (y, z)ḡ
over maps f¯ : I → J and ḡ : J → K in Fin∗ . These simplices separate into tuples
σ = {σj : j ∈ J} and σ ′ = {σk′ : k ∈ K} relative to the product decompositions
(10). Then, for each k in K, we have the corresponding simplices
                                                          sym∗
             (σ ′ σ)k : ∆n → HomE ⊗ (⊗j∈Jk xIj , zk ) −→ HomE ⊗ (xIk , zk )        (11)
where the first map above is the composite simplex
                          O
                    σk′ (   σj ) : ∆n → HomE ⊗ (⊗j∈Jk xIj , zk )

and sym is precomposition with the symmetry isomorphism xIk → ⊗j∈Jk xIj . The
composite simplex σ ′ σ is, finally, the map in HomE ⊗ (x, z)ḡf¯ specified by the given
tuple {(σ ′ σ)k : k ∈ K}.
  The following is apparent.
26                                     CRIS NEGRON


Lemma 4.9. If E is a fibrant symmetric monoidal simplicial category, then the
associated simplicial category E ⊗ is also fibrant.
   By construction the simplicial category E ⊗ comes equipped with a forgetful
functor p : E ⊗ → Fin∗ which sends each object (x, I) to I, and collapses each
component in the mapping complex HomE ⊗ (x, y)f¯ to f¯.
Lemma 4.10. Given a finite set I, any choice of a linear ordering on I provides
                             ∼
an injective equivalence E I → (E ⊗ )I to the fiber of p : E ⊗ → Fin∗ over I.
  Here by an equivalence we mean a simplicial functor which is essentially surjective
on the underlying plain categories, say, and which induces homotopy equivalences
on the mapping complexes.
Proof. The inclusion just takes a tuple x in E I to the object (x, I), where I is given
its specified ordering. The induced map on Hom complexes
                                 Y
                 HomE I (x, y) =   HomE (xi , yi ) → HomE ⊗ (x, y)idI
                                 i∈I

is simply an equality. Hence the functor E I → (E ⊗ )I is fully faithful. The identi-
ties on both I and x provide isomorphisms between (x, I) and x paired with any
alternate ordering on I. So the inclusion is essentially surjective as well.       □
   We note that the fibers of the nerve are the nerve of the fibers, N((E ⊗ )I ) =
N(E ⊗ )I . Hence, taking the nerve of the simplicial equivalence from Lemma 4.10
                                            ∼
provides an equivalence of ∞-categories E I → (E ⊗ )I .
Proposition 4.11. For a fibrant symmetric monoidal simplicial category E, and
E ⊗ = N(E ⊗ ), the functor N(p) : E ⊗ → Fin∗ is a cocartesian fibration. Further-
more, given any map of finite pointed sets f¯ : I → J, choice of object x in (E ⊗ )I ,
and xf¯ = (xIj : j ∈ J) in (E ⊗ )J , the morphism
                                                Y
                    ix,f¯ ∈ HomE ⊗ (x, xf¯)f¯ =   HomE 1 (xIj , xIj )
                                                  j∈J

specified by the identities idxIj is a N(p)-cocartesian edge over f¯.

Proof. Here x is, precisely, an object in E I along with a choice of ordering on I.
The products xIj are all taken with respect to this ordering, exactly as above.
    It suffices to prove that, for any map of finite pointed sets f¯ : I → J and x in
E I , the proposed edge ix,f¯ : x → xf¯ is cocartesian over f¯. For this it suffices to
show that, given any z ∈ E ⊗ and K = p(z), the diagram
                                          −◦ix,f¯
                    MapsE ⊗ (xf¯, z)                    / MapsE ⊗ (x, z)


                                          −◦f¯
                                                                 
                    MapsFin∗ (J, K)                     / MapsFin (I, K)

is a pullback square in K an [58, 01TL]. Since the spaces in the bottom row are
discrete it suffices further to show that, for each ḡ : J → K and h̄ = ḡ f¯, the map
on the fibers
                      − ◦ ix,f¯ : MapsE ⊗ (xf¯, z)ḡ → MapsE ⊗ (x, z)h̄            (12)
is a homotopy equivalence.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                         27


   Whether or not the map (12) is an equivalence can be checked at the level of the
homotopy category h K an, at which point one can replace the mapping spaces for
E ⊗ with the mapping complexes for E ⊗ [58, 02LN]. We now ask if the map
                     − ◦ ix,f¯ : HomE ⊗ (xf¯, z)ḡ → HomE ⊗ (x, z)h̄
is an equivalence. However, since the symmetry transformations on E are isomor-
phisms, one can check directly from the defintion of composition (11) that this map
is simply an isomorphism of simplicial sets.                                      □

associated ∞-category E . The cocartesian fibration p : E ⊗ → Fin∗ from Proposition

4.5. Fiber products and transfer of symmetric monoidal structures. Through-
out the text we construct symmetric monoidal ∞-categories by combining data
from, say, a topological source and an algebraic source. Formally such categories
arise as fiber products of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories along pairs of symmetric
monoidal functors. To address such situations we have the following.
Proposition 4.15. Given a pair of symmetric monoidal functors Fi⊗ : Ei⊗ → T ⊗
in which one of the Fi is an isofibration, the fiber product E0⊗ ×T ⊗ E1⊗ inherits a
unique symmetric monoidal structure under which the two projections
                                      E0⊗ ×T ⊗ E1⊗ → Ei⊗
are symmetric monoidal functors
     We omit the details, but indicate some method of proof for the interested reader.
Idea of proof. This result can be proved by hand, or can be deduced from the fact
that the forgetful functor from SM∞ (Defintion C.1) to Cat∞ commutes with
pullbacks [57, Corollary 3.2.2.4].                                             □
   We also note that symmetric monoidality is stable under equivalence of isofibra-
tions.
Lemma 4.16. Consider a diagram

                                  C
                                                 F              / C′

                                        p                  p′
                                              !   |
                                              Fin∗
in which both p and p′ are isofibrations, and F is an equivalence. If one of p or
p′ realizes C or C ′ as a symmetric monoidal ∞-category, then both C and C ′ are
symmetric monoidal and F is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                      29


Proof. In this case F is an equivalence of isofibrations over Fin∗ [58, 0295], and
hence both p and p′ are cocartesian fibrations whenever one is [58, 028A]. Further-
more F preserves cocartesian edges by [58, 028B], and for any finite set I we have
a commuting diagram

                            CI
                                            FI
                                                                / C′

                                                      /

                                       Q
