in Cat∞ obtained
               Q from transport along the inert maps ρ̄i : I →Q{0}.′ It follows that
the map CI → i C{0} is an equivalence if and only if CI′ → i C{0}       is an equiv-
alence, and hence both transport functors induce equivalences by our hypothesis.
Taken together, we see that both C and C ′ are symmetric monoidal ∞-categories
and that F is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories.                  □

4.6. Equifibered ∞-categories. Below we consider the symmetric monoidal cat-
egory of finite sets Fin, under disjoint unions, and have the associated fibration
Fin⊗ → Fin∗ from Proposition 4.11.
Definition 4.17 ([3, Example 4.3.1]). A symmetric monoidal ∞-category over
Fin⊗ , i.e. a symmetric monoidal ∞-category with a fixed symmetric monoidal func-
tor π : T ⊗ → Fin⊗ , is said to be equifibered over Fin if the structure map π is an
isofibration and the diagram
                                 T ×T                     /T                   (13)

                                                        
                                 Fin × Fin            / Fin

for the product functors is a pullback diagram in Cat∞ . A map of equifibered
symmetric monoidal ∞-categories is a strictly commuting diagram of symmetric
monoidal functors
                           S⊗                   /T⊗

                                       #          {
                                           Fin⊗                  .
   By an equifibered ∞-category over Fin we always mean a symmetric monoidal
∞-category which is equifibered over Fin. Intuitively, a symmetric monoidal ∞-
category T ⊗ → Fin∗ admits an equifibered ∞-category structure over Fin provided
objects in the underlying ∞-category T are not too large, and the monoidal product
on T is given by a type of disjoint union.
Example 4.18. The category Topc of compact topological spaces with disjoint
union is almost equifibered over Fin, via the connected components functor π0 :
Topc → Fin. In particular, the product diagram (13) is a pullback diagram, but
the structure map Topc → Fin fails to be an isofibration. Hence, after taking an
                             ∼
isofibrant replacement Topc → Tc → Fin we find that Tc is equifibered over Fin.
Such a replacement can be constructed by considering, for example, the category
30                                       CRIS NEGRON


of pairs (X, α) where X is a compact topological space and α is a bijection α :
        ∼
π0 (X) → I from the connected components to a finite set.
   Similarly, the ∞-category K anc of compact Kan complexes with disjoint union
becomes an equifibered ∞-category over Fin, via the connected components functor,
after taking an isofibrant replacement.
Example 4.19. For any algebraic group G over a field k, one can check that the
category Rep(G) of G-representations admits no equifibered structure over Fin.
Definition 4.20. Fix an equifibered ∞-category over Fin, π : T ⊗ → Fin⊗ . A
T -monoidal ∞-category is an ∞-category equipped with a cocartesian fibration
q : E ⊗ → T ⊗ which satisfies the following:
       • For each object x in the fiber TK⊗ over a given set K in Fin∗ , and inert
         projections ρk : x → xk over the projections ρ̄k : K → {0}, transport
         provides an equivalence

     • For each object x in the fiber TK⊗ , and cocartesian edge f : x → x′ over
       the unique active map f¯ : K → {0}, transport provides an equivalence
                                                  ∼
                                         f! : Ex⊗ → Ex⊗′ .
A map of T -monoidal ∞-categories is a map of cocartesian fibrations over T ⊗ .
    We note that any T -monoidal ∞-category E ⊗ comes equipped with a cocarte-
sian fibration to Fin∗ , via composition. One can check, using [58, 023M] and the
first condition above, that the induced map E ⊗ → Fin∗ gives E ⊗ the structure
of a symmetric monoidal ∞-category and, subsequently, that q : E ⊗ → T ⊗ is a
symmetric monoidal functor. We compose further to observe a symmetric monoidal
functor E ⊗ → Fin⊗ .

4.8. Language and notation for monoidal ∞-categories. Given an ∞-category
E , we can speak of a symmetric monoidal structure on E . This is the choice of a
                                                         ⊗
symmetric monoidal ∞-category E ⊗ → Fin∗ with E = E{0}     , or more generally with
                                             ∼  ⊗
a specified isomorphism of simplicial sets E = E{0} . Having specified a symmetric
monoidal structure on a given ∞-category E , we may also just refer to E itself as
a symmetric monoidal ∞-category, by an abuse of language.
32                                      CRIS NEGRON


   Given a symmetric monoidal ∞-category T ⊗ → Fin∗ , we say T is equifibered
over Fin if T ⊗ → Fin∗ is a symmetric monoidal ∞-category which is equifibered
over Fin.
   Given ∞-categories E1 and E2 with specified symmetric monoidal structures, we
can also speak of a symmetric monoidal structure on a given functor F : E1 → E2 .
This is the choice of a map F ⊗ : E1⊗ → E2⊗ between cocartesian fibrations over
Fin∗ which fits into a diagram

                                E1
                                            F         / E2

                          include                           include
                                                       
                               E1⊗                / E ⊗.
                                                     2
                                            F⊗


                    5. ∞-Categories of disk embeddings
   We construct a symmetric monoidal ∞-category f rDisk of ±-colored framed
disks, and disk embeddings. Besides our addition of ±-colors, many of the con-
structions and results of this section are standard. See for example [1, Remark
2.10].
   Following these introductory materials, we show in Section 6 that f rDisk is
equifibered over Fin. Throughout this study we are generically interested in f rDisk-
monoidal ∞-categories as a coarse approximation to ribbon tensor categories, in
the homotopical setting. In particular, we show in Section 7 below that any rib-
bon tensor (1-)category admits the structure of a f rDisk-monoidal ∞-category,
and that the original ribbon structure can be recovered from this structure over
f rDisk.

5.1. Colored sets. A finite colored set is the pairing I of a finite set I with a
morphism of sets colorI : I → {±}. Maps of colored sets f : I → J are maps of
the underling uncolored sets f : I → J which preserve positively colored elements.
(So, we are allowed to send a negative index i to a positive index j, but not vise-
versa.) We let Fin denote the category of finite colored sets.
   When we write i ∈ I for a colored set I, we mean that i is an element in the
underlying uncolored set I. As in the uncolored setting, given a map f : I → J in
Fin we let Ij denote the the preimage f −1 (j) ⊂ I along with its induced coloring.
We have the analogously defined category Fin∗ of finite pointed colored sets.

5.2. The ∞-category of colored disk embeddings. We construct a symmetric
monoidal simplicial category f rDisk of framed colored disks. Objects are finite
colored sets I, and morphisms f : I → J (in the underlying discrete category) are
oriented embedding of disks
                                f : D ×I → D ×J.                                (15)
We assume that f sends positively colored disks in D × I to positively colored
disks in D × J , so that f recovers a map in Fin on connected components, and we
take specifically D to be the standard disk D = {z ∈ R2 : |z| ≤ 1}. The mapping
complexes are the subspaces
       Homf rDisk (I, J ) = Embed(D × I, D × J ) ⊆ Maps(D × I, D × J )
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        33


whose n-simplices consist of those continuous function
                                 σ : |∆n | × D × I → D × J
which evaluate at each t ∈ |∆n | to a smooth oriented embedding σt : D×I → D×J
which recovers a map in Fin on connected components.
Remark 5.1. One can think of negatively colored disks as copies of the standard
disk in R2 equipped with the negated orientation.
  The composition functions
           ◦ : Homf rDisk (J , K) × Homf rDisk (I, J ) → Homf rDisk (I, J )
are defined by taking a pair of n-simplices σ : |∆n | × D × I → D × J and σ ′ :
|∆n | × D × J → D × K to the usual composite
                      diag ×1                          1×σ           σ′
      |∆n | × D × I     →       |∆n | × |∆n | × D × I → |∆n | × D × J → D × K.
Lemma 5.2. The simplicial category f rDisk is fibrant.
Proof. One can fill horns by noting that the geometric realization |∆n | retracts
onto any given horn |Λni |.                                                    □
   We have the apparent symmetric monoidal structure on f rDisk provided by
disjoint union, and taking connected components provides a symmetric monoidal
functor π0 : f rDisk → Fin to the discrete category of finite pointed sets, with dis-
joint union. Applying the homotopy coherent nerve therefore produces a symmetric
monoidal ∞-category over Fin⊗ ,
             π0⊗ : N(f rDisk⊗ ) → Fin⊗ , (Ik : k ∈ K) 7→ (Ik : k ∈ K),
by Proposition 4.14.
   We give a slightly more efficient presentation of the symmetric monoidal struc-
ture on N(f rDisk) in Section 5.4 below, after recalling a colored variant of the
framed little disk operad.
5.3. The ∞-operad of colored disks. In addition to the symmetric monoidal
∞-category of framed disks we have a related disky ∞-operad. This is a framed
and colored variant of the usual little disk operad, which we recall here.
   Objects in the preceding (fibrant) simplicial category f rE2 are finite colored
sets and morphisms f : I → J consist of a choice of subset If ⊆ I along with an
oriented disk embedding f : D × If → D × J . For higher simplices, the mapping
complexes decompose over morphisms in Fin∗ ,
                                      a
            Homf rE2 (I, J ) =                 Embed(D × Iξ̄ , D × J )ξ̄ ,
                                   ξ̄∈HomFin∗ (I,J )

where an n-simplex in Embed(D × Iξ̄ , D × J )ξ̄ is a continuous function |∆n | × D ×
Iξ̄ → D × J which evaluates to an oriented embedding at each t ∈ |∆n |, and which
recovers the specified map ξ¯ on connected components.
    We have the apparent forgetful functor to the discrete category of pointed colored
sets f rE2 → Fin∗ , and can forget further to Fin∗ . Taking the homotopy coherent
nerve, we obtain an ∞-category with an isofibration f rE2 → Fin∗ , and one can
check that this fibration gives f rE2 the structure of an ∞-operad [57, Definition
2.1.1.10].
34                                     CRIS NEGRON


     Now, taking disjoint unions of continuous functions
                                                         
                                          σ
                |∆n | × D × (⨿k∈Kl Ik ) →l D × Jl : l ∈ L 7→
                                                                     
                              ⨿l σl : |∆n | × D × (⨿k∈Kf¯Ik ) → D × J

defines a simplicial functor f rDisk⊗ → f rE2 . We similarly define a disjoint union
functor Fin⊗ → Fin∗ for the symmetric monoidal category of finite sets. Via these
disjoint union functors, we connect the symmetric monoidal simplicial disk category
to the simplicial little disk operad via a pullback diagram

of simplicial categories. Taking the homotopy coherent nerve then produces a

5.4. Snatched presentation of f rDisk⊗ . We construct a symmetric monoidal
∞-category p : f rDisk⊗ → Fin∗ and exhibit a symmetric monoidal equivalence

                       N(f rDisk⊗ )
                                               ∼               / f rDisk⊗      (17)

The point here is to provide a more user-friendly expression of the symmetric
monoidal structure on the underlying ∞-category f rDisk = N(f rDisk).
   An object in f rDisk⊗ is a colored set I equipped with a set map µ : I → K, or
equivalently a continuous map µ : D ×I → K, where K is a finite set which is taken
to be completely positive if one likes. A morphism (I, µ) → (J , ν) in f rDisk⊗ is
the pairing of a map f : D × If → D × J in f rE2 with a map f¯ : Kf¯ → L in Fin∗

                              D × If

and for which If = µ−1 Kf¯.

Lemma 5.3. The projection p : f rDisk⊗ → Fin∗ gives f rDisk the structure of
                                                                          ∼
a symmetric monoidal ∞-category, and there is an equivalence N(f rDisk⊗ ) →
f rDisk⊗ of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories over Fin⊗ .
Proof. Let ′ Fin⊗ denote the symmetric monoidal construction of Proposition 4.11
and Fin⊗ denote the alternate construction given above. We have the assignment
which sends an ordered tuple of sets (Ik : k ∈ K) to the disjoint union ⨿k Ik
equipped with the unique map µ : ⨿k Ik → K which has µ−1 (k) = Ik . This
assignment defines a functor ′ Fin⊗ → Fin⊗ over Fin∗ , in both ways, which is seen
to be essentially surjective and fully faithful, and thus an equivalence.
   Considering the identities on f rE2 and Fin∗ , we now obtained a unique functor
N(f rDisk⊗ ) → f rDisk⊗ which completes a transformation between the respective
pullback diagrams ∆1 ×∆1 → Cat∞ from (16) and (19). This completing functor is
an equivalence by [58, 033K]. The fact that the isofibration p : f rDisk⊗ → Fin∗ is
a cocartesian fibration which gives f rDisk⊗ the structure of a symmetric monoidal
∞-category now follows by the transfer result of Lemma 4.16.                     □
Warning 5.4. From now on the symmetric monoidal structure on Fin is always
realized via the fibration Fin⊗ → Fin of pairs (I, µ) as above, though the distinction
is of little technical importance.
5.5. Linear disk arrangements. We often consider the subcategory Linf rDisk⊗ ⊆
f rDisk⊗ spanned by all objects, and those morphisms
                                   f :D×I →D×J
whose constituent disk embeddings fi : Di → Dj send the x-axis of Di into the
x-axis of Dj , and have derivatives T0 fi : R2 → R2 equal to the identity. One can
check that the inclusion
                             Linf rDisk⊗ → f rDisk⊗
36                                    CRIS NEGRON


is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories (Lemma A.4).
   From homotopies between such linear arrangements of disks, i.e. from 2-simplices
in Linf rDisk, one can extract some useful combinatorial data.
5.6. Extracting framed braids. From any morphism f : (I, µ) → (J , ν) in
Linf rDisk⊗ , and j ∈ J , the linear ordering on the x-axis in the j-th disk Dj
induces a linear ordering on the preimage Ij → {j}. Any homotopy between two
maps in Linf rDisk⊗ , ζ : |∆1 | × D × If → D × J , then traces out framed braids in
the disks D×J . By a framed braid we mean an element in the fundamental group of
the space of framed configurations of points in the disk Brf r (K) = π0 (Conf K
                                                                              f r (D)),
                                 K
where we point the space Conf f r (D) via a choice of an ordering on the set K, a
map K → D which aligns the points according to the given ordering along the
x-axis, and the points inherit their framings from the ambient framing on D. We
note the decomposition Brf r (K) = Br(K) ⋉ (ZK ) where the integral factor tracks
the windings of the framing around each embedded singleton {k} → D.

5.7. A remark on pairings of uncolored disks. We note that there are two
symmetric embeddings ι± : f r Disk⊗ → f rDisk⊗ from the symmetric monoidal
∞-category of uncolored disks. These embeddings are isomorphisms onto the full
subcategories spanned by totally negative, and totally positive disks respectively.
(One defines the uncolored disk category in direct analogy with the colored presen-
tation from above.)
   We can consider further the full subcategory spanned by both collections of to-
tally negative and totally positive disks. The two embeddings ι± are then connected
by a transformation ι : ∆1 × f r Disk⊗ → f rDisk⊗ with ι|{0} = ι− and ι|{1} = ι+ ,
and which is furthermore an equivalence onto this mixed subcategory. For any
             TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                          37


tuple of negatively colored disks x = (−I, µ), the corresponding natural morphism
ιx : ∆1 → f rDisk⊗ is the negating of the identity −idx : (−I, µ) → (+I, µ).
   One can realize the map ι directly at the level of simplicial categories or by solving
a cocartesian lifting problem, though the precise details are not so important for
us. Our only point here is that f rDisk⊗ itself can be considered as a type of
coupling of two copies of the uncolored disk category along with certain transition
morphisms.

                        6. f rDisk-monoidal ∞-categories
   We show that f rDisk is equifibered over Fin (Defintion 4.17). We then provide
basic information regarding f rDisk-subcategories, and symmetric monoidal sub-
categories, in f rDisk-monoidal ∞-categories. We also discuss a relation between
f rDisk-monoidality and monoidality over the colored little disk ∞-operad f rE2 .
   As a fundamental example for the text, we show in Section 7 that any ribbon
tensor category, in the 1-categorical sense of the term, admits a natural f rDisk-
monoidal structure. Following Section 7, we provide constructions of marked bor-
dism categories, then begin our descent into TQFTs.

6.1. f rDisk is equifibered over Fin.
Proposition 6.1. The components functor f rDisk⊗ → Fin⊗ gives f rDisk the
structure of an equifibered symmetric monoidal ∞-category over Fin.
Proof. According to our discussion around Corollary 4.13, the product functors for
f rDisk and Fin are induced by the products on the preceding simplicial categories.
Hence it suffices to show that the diagram

                       f rDisk × f rDisk
                                                ⨿       / f rDisk


                                                             
                            Fin × Fin
                                                ⨿          / Fin

is a pullback diagram of simplicial categories, in the strict sense of the term. For
this we need to show that the corresponding diagrams on objects and morphisms
are pullbacks.
    The case of objects is obvious. Let us now fix finite sets Iε and Jε with colored
lifts Iε and Jε . Take I = I0 ⨿ I1 and J = J0 ⨿ J1 , and define I and J similarly.
We consider the diagram of simplicial sets

    Homf rDisk (I0 , J0 ) × Homf rDisk (I1 , J1 )
                                                    ⨿        / Homf rDisk (I, J )   (20)


                                                                     
        HomFin (I0 , J0 ) × HomFin (I1 , J1 )
                                                    ⨿          / HomFin (I, J).

We note that the vertical maps here are given explicitly by taking maps on con-
nected components and forgetting the colorings
                 (σ : |∆n | × D × L → D × M) 7→ (π0 (σ) : L → M ).
  Since Homs over Fin are discrete, (20) is a pullback diagram if and only if, for
chosen set maps (f¯0 , f¯1 ) 7→ f¯, disjoint union provides an isomorphism between the
38                                         CRIS NEGRON


fibers
         Homf rDisk (I0 , J0 )f¯0 × Homf rDisk (I1 , J1 )f¯1 → Homf rDisk (I, J )f¯.   (21)
This is clear however since both spaces decompose over J as the product space
                     Y
                         Homf rDisk (Ij , {±0}), ± = color(j),
                         j∈J

and under this decomposition the disjoint union map (21) is just the identity.           □
   The same arguments show that the uncolored disk category f r Disk is equifibered
over Fin as well.
6.2. f rDisk-categories. As a shorthand, by a f rDisk-category we mean a f rDisk-
monoidal ∞-category. Similarly, by a f r Disk-category we mean a f r Disk-monoidal
∞-category.
Definition 6.2. For any f rDisk-category q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ we let E − and E +
denote the respective fibers over the negatively and positively colored singletons
{±0} in f rDisk ⊆ f rDisk⊗ . Given a colored set I, with negative and positive
indices I− and I+ respectively, the colored exponent E I for E ⊗ is defined as the
product
                             E I = (E − )I− × (E + )I+ .
   The fibers E ± are the underlying ∞-categories for a given f rDisk-category
E → f rDisk⊗ . According to the discussion of Section 5.7, one can think of
 ⊗

a f rDisk-category as a pair of f r Disk-categories coupled by a f r Disk-monoidal
functor.
   To elaborate, we have the two embeddings ι± : f r Disk⊗ → f rDisk⊗ and as-
sociated transformation ι : ∆1 × f r Disk⊗ → f rDisk⊗ whose image is the full
subcategory spanned by consistently colored disks. Hence, any f rDisk-category
E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ pulls pack to a cocartesian fibration
                                   ι∗ E ⊗ → ∆1 × f r Disk⊗ .
   The fibers of the above fibration over {ε} × f r Disk⊗ exhibits f r Disk-structures

Remark 6.3. Straightening provides an identification between our f rDisk-categories

6.3. Structure maps for f rDisk-categories.
Definition 6.4. For a given f rDisk-category E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ , the unit and prod-
uct functors
                     = E ⊗ → E ± and m : E ± × E ± ∼
            unit : ∗ ∼  ∅                          = E⊗       → E±
                                                             {±0,±1}

on the underlying ∞-categories E are the transport functors along the respective
                                  ±

like colored embeddings ∅ → D and D ⨿ D → D in f rDisk ⊆ f rDisk⊗ , respec-
tively. The transition functor d : E − → E + is the transport functor along the color
negating identity id : D− → D+ .
  We commonly understand the tuple of functors
               (unit : ∗ → E ± , m : E ± × E ± → E ± , d : E − → E + )
as the underlying structure maps for such a fibration E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
   We note that, since all disk embeddings D ⨿ D → D are isotopic (Proposi-
tion A.2), the product functors from Definition 6.4 are well-defined up to natural
isomorphism. For the sake of specificity however, we can fix the disk embedding
D ⨿ D → D as the one which maps the first disks by z 7→ 31 z − 12 and the second
disk by z 7→ 31 z + 12 .
Remark 6.5. We commonly understand an (uncolored) f r Disk-category F ⊗ →
f r Disk⊗ as a homotopical variant of a balanced monoidal category. In topological
terms, the braiding transformation for the product functor is provided by the co-
cartesian transformation ∆1 × (F × F ) → F over the rotation of the two disks
within the ambient disk |∆1 | × (D ⨿ D) → D, and the balancing transformation
∆1 × F → F is provided by rotating a single disk within itself |∆1 | × D → D.
    Similarly, from a f rDisk-category E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ , we extract our two ho-
motopical balanced monoidal categories (E ± )⊗ → f rDisk⊗ and a homotopically
balanced monoidal functor d⊗ : (E − )⊗ → (E + )⊗ .
6.4. f rDisk-categories are f rE2 -monoidal ∞-categories. We take a moment
to clarify the relationship between our monoidal ∞-categories, over the equifibered
∞-category f rDisk, and monoidal ∞-categories over the ∞-operad of little disks.
For the purposes of this discussion, the reader need only understand that an
∞-operad is an ∞-category O ⊗ equipped with a type of isofibration to finite
pointed sets O ⊗ → Fin∗ [57, Definition 2.1.1.10]. As with symmetric monoidal
∞-categories, the underlying ∞-category for an ∞-operad is the fiber over the
                  ⊗
singleton O = O{0}   .
   Given an ∞-operad O ⊗ , an O-monoidal ∞-category is a cocartesian fibration
E ⊙ → O ⊗ which has consistent fibers, as in (9). See [57, Definitions 2.1.1.10 &
2.1.2.13]. We note that any O-monoidal ∞-category is itself an ∞-operad, and that
the structure map E ⊙ → O ⊗ is a map of ∞-operads [57, Proposition 2.1.2.12].
   We have the disjoint union functor Fin⊗ → Fin∗ , (I, µ) 7→ I, and for a given op-
erad O ⊗ an envelope for O ⊗ is any ∞-category Env(O)⊗ which fits into a pullback
diagram
                             Env(O)⊗              / O⊗


                                                       
                              Fin⊗                  / Fin∗
                                       disj union
40                                   CRIS NEGRON


in Cat∞ . We assume additionally that the given diagram strictly commutes, and
that the projection to Fin⊗ is an isofibration.
Remark 6.6. Since the structure map for any ∞-operad is an isofibration, the
strict pullback Fin⊗ ×Fin∗ O ⊗ always provides a pullback, i.e. limit diagram, in
Cat∞ [58, 03G7]. So, any envelope admits a unique equivalence to the strict pull-
back, as a fibration over Fin⊗ .
   For any envelope Env(O)⊗ , composing with the symmetric structure map (not
disjoint union map!) for Fin⊗ produces a functor Env(O)⊗ → Fin∗ which realizes
Env(O)⊗ as a symmetric monoidal ∞-category [57, Corollary 2.2.4.5.]. The under-
lying ∞-category for the resulting fibration Env(O)⊗ → Fin∗ is identified with the
non-full subcategory
                                Env(O) ∼= Act(O) ⊆ O ⊗
consisting of all objects, but only active maps in O ⊗ . We have our primary example
of interest.
Example 6.7. The pullback diagram from (19) realizes the symmetric monoidal
∞-category of framed colored disks as an envelope f rDisk⊗ = Env(f rE2 ) for the
∞-operad of framed colored disks.
   Now, it was shown in works of Barkan, Haugseng, Kock, and Steinebrunner that
the corresponding envelope functor
                    Env = Fin⊗ ×Fin∗ − : Op∞ → (SM∞ )/ Fin⊗
is fully faithful [41], and furthermore an equivalence onto the full subcategory
of equifibered ∞-categories over Fin [3]. Under this equivalence O-monoidal ∞-
categories, in the sense of [57], are identified with Env(O)-monoidal ∞-categories,
in the sense of Definition 4.20. Restricting to our particular case of interest, and
noting [58, 01ZS], we obtain equivalences between f rE2 -monoidal ∞-categories and
f rDisk-monoidal ∞-categories.
Theorem 6.8 ([41, 3]). The envelope functor induces equivalences of ∞-categories
                                           ∼
                        Env : f rE2 -Cats → f rDisk-Cats.
6.5. Symmetric subcategories in f rDisk-categories. Let E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be
a f rDisk-category and consider full subcategories L ± ⊆ E ± which are stable
under isomorphism in the underlying ∞-categories E ± . We define the associated
subcategory L ⊗ ⊆ E ⊗ generated by the L ± to be the (unique) full subcategory
whose fibers over f rDisk⊗ fit into strict pullback diagrams
                               Lt⊗              / LI                           (22)

                                                   
                               Et⊗              / E I,
                                         ρ!

where t = (I, µ) is arbitrary. Since the inclusion L I → E I is an isofibration in
this case, and since each ρ! is an equivalence, the above pullback diagram implies
                                                                     ∼
that the transport equivalences for E ⊗ restrict to equivalences Lt⊗ → L I for L ⊗
as well (Lemma 3.22).
   Now, according to Section 6.4, we might imagine E ⊗ as a symmetric monoidal ∞-
category of disks which are labeled by objects in the E ± . The symmetric structure
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                       41


is then given by taking disjoint unions of such labeled disks. From this perspective
one expects that the subcategory L ⊗ of disks with labels in L ± forms a symmetric
monoidal subcategory in E ⊗ . One can show that this is in fact the case.
Lemma 6.9. For full subcategories L ± ⊆ E ± which are stable under isomorphism,
the associated ∞-category L ⊗ is naturally symmetric monoidal and the inclusion
L ⊗ → E ⊗ is a symmetric monoidal functor. Furthermore, L ⊗ is stable under
isomorphism in E ⊗ .
   Note that the assertion that the inclusion L ⊗ → E ⊗ is symmetric monoidal
forces the symmetric structure map L ⊗ → Fin∗ to be the one obtained from E ⊗
via restriction. We delay the proof of Lemma 6.9 to Appendix A.5.
Remark 6.10. A version of Lemma 6.9 holds for non-full subcategories L ± ⊆ E ± .
The construction of the category L ⊗ is more delicate in this case, however.
6.6. f rDisk-subcategories.
Proposition 6.11. Let q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be a f rDisk-category and L ± be full
                                                      ⊗
subcategories in the underlying ∞-categories E ± = E{±0}   which are stable under
isomorphism. Suppose also that
      • The unit maps unit : ∗ → E ± have image in L ± .
      • The transition map d : E − → E + sends L − into L + .
      • The product functors m : E ± × E ± → E ± send L ± × L ± into L ± .
Then there is a unique, full f rDisk-subcategory L ⊗ in E ⊗ which is stable under
isomorphism and has underlying ∞-categories L ± .
   The f rDisk-subcategory L ⊗ is, as one expects, precisely the symmetric sub-
category constructed in Section 6.5. So we are giving necessary (and sufficient)
conditions for the associated symmetric subcategory L ⊗ from Lemma 6.9 to be a
f rDisk-subcategory. To assist with the proof we record a related lemma.
Lemma 6.12. For any f rDisk-category q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ and morphism f :
s = (I, µ) → t = (J , ν) in f rDisk⊗ , the corresponding transport functor fits in to
a diagram
                                Es⊗
                                         f!
                                                / Et⊗

                               ρ! ∼              ρ! ∼
                                                  
                                EI              / EJ
                                         mf

in Cat∞ in which mf decomposes as a composite of functors D : E I → E I0 ,
M : E I0 → E J0 , and U : E J0 → E J where
     • I0 is obtained from I by changing some negatively colored indices positive,
        and D applies the transition functor d : E − → E + at all such indices.
     • J0 ⊆ J is the image of f : D × If → D × J , or rather π0 (f ), and M is
        an iterated application of the product functors m : E ± × E ± → E ± .
     • U : E J0 → E J inserts the unit unit : ∗ → E ± at all indices in the comple-
        ment J \ J0 .
   The proof of Lemma 6.12 is time consuming but straightforward, and is provided
in Appendix A.4. We return to our original proposition.

             7. Disk categories from ribbon tensor categories
   We show that any discrete ribbon tensor category A♡ produces a gaggle of
                                                     ⊗
f rDisk-monoidal ∞-categories q⋆ : A⊗   ⋆ → f rDisk (Proposition 7.11 and Corol-
lary 7.12). Here A is the category of cochains A = Ch(A♡ ) and ⋆ is a modifier.
Our constructions follow from a general result which asserts that any functor be-
tween balanced monoidal categories d : E − → E + determines a f rDisk-monoidal
∞-category q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
   In order to support our analysis of unbounded complexes over cocomplete cate-
gories, like A♡ , we recall basic information concerning pro-finite completion. Pro-
finite objects are essential tools for us, as a pro-finite category is always on the
“other side” of a finitely generated linear abelian category under a duality.
7.1. f rDisk-categories from discrete balanced categories. Recall that a bal-
anced monoidal category E is a (discrete) braided monoidal category which is
equipped with a natural isomorphism θ : idE → idE which satisfies
                                 θx⊗y = (θx ⊗ θy )cyx cxy ,
for any pair of objects x and y in E, where c is the braiding. A balanced functor
between balanced monoidal categories E and D, with associated transformations
θE and θD , is a braided monoidal functor F : E → D which satisfies F θE = θD F .
    A balanced tensor category E is a ribbon tensor category if there is an equality
θx∗ = θx∗ whenever x is rigid in E.
    We consider a pair of balanced monoidal categories E − and E + equipped with a
balanced functor d : E − → E + . For such a pairing, and colored set I we consider
the colored exponent E I , as in Definition 6.2, and for any map f : (I, µ) → (J , ν)
in Linf rDisk⊗ , with underlying disk arrangement D × If → D × J , we let
                                      mf : E I → E J                                  (23)
denote the associated product with constituent factors
                mjf : E Ij → E color(j) , x 7→ dεi1 (xi1 ) ⊗ . . . ⊗ dεir (xr ).
Here each Ij = {i1 , . . . , ir } is given the ordering specified by the map f (see Section
5.6) and                           

                          dεi =
                                       d if color(i) = − color(j).
We refer the the map mf from (23) as the colored product functor for the triple
{E − , E + , d} over f .
   To be clear, mf sends the excess factors E I\If to a point, i.e. mf is a compos-
ite of the projection E I → E If with the aforementioned multiplications, and for
any index j ∈ J with empty preimage the map (23) just chooses the unit object

              ⊗

i.e. if there is a framed braid (β, n) from f to f ′ for which we have an equality
                                        α′ ◦ ζβ,n |x = α.
The symmetric monoidal structure is given by taking disjoint unions of disks and
(cartesian) products of morphisms in E ± .
   Here, again, by a framed braid (β, n) from f to f ′ we mean a framed braid for
which the associated elements in the symmetric groups β̄j ∈ SIj are the unique
permutations which exchange the ordering on Ij defined by f with the ordering
defined by f ′ .

7.3. Pro-finite objects. We show below that the category of unbounded cochains
A = Ch(A♡ ) over a ribbon tensor category produces a f rDisk-category A⊗ →
f rDisk⊗ . Here the positive fiber is a pro-finite variant of Ch(A♡ ) and the negative
fiber is Ch(A♡ )op . Both unboundedness and pro-finiteness cause some technical
issues, which we resolve over the next two subsections.
   Fix a tensor category A♡ , or more generally a finitely generated linear abelian
category (Definition C.8). First, the category A♡   pro-f in of pro-finite objects in A

consists of objects x in A which come equipped with a filtered collection Bx of
inclusions iλ : xλ → x, over a filtered category Λ, which satisfy the following:
      • An inclusion i′ : x′ → x belongs to Bx if and only if there is a map iλ :
         xλ → x in the collection which factors through i′ .

y ∗ ⊗ x∗ for arbitrary objects in A♡ . This gives −∗ its monoidal structure. Com-
patibility with the braiding follows from the identification (cxy )∗ = cx∗ y∗ whenever
x and y are of finite length [2, Lemma 2.1.11], which forces such an equality at
all objects, and the identification for the twist θx∗ = θx∗ is precisely the ribbon
condition.                                                                            □

   We note that the opposite category (A♡ )op itself is both complete and cocom-
plete, since A♡ is both complete and cocomplete. Hence Lemma 7.5 tells us that
the category of pro-finite objects A♡
                                    pro-f in is both complete and cocomplete as well,
and also abelian. When A♡ is finite, and thus has enough projectives, we see that
A♡pro-f in has enough injectives.

Lemma 7.6. Let A♡ be a tensor category.
    (1) The forgetful functor A♡           ♡
                               pro-f in → A preserves all limits.

    (2) Given any essentially small cofiltered diagram K op → A♡ f in in which all of
        the transition maps xα → xβ are surjective, and any pro-finite x, a map
        f : x → limα xα in A♡ is a (continuous) isomorphism in A♡      pro-f in if and
                  ←−
        only if the following hold:
         (a) Each composite fα : x → xα factors through a structural projection
             πµ : x → xµ , and the resulting map xµ → xα is surjective.
         (b) Each structural projection πλ : x → xλ factors through some composite
             fβ : x → x β .

Proof. (1) Follows from the fact that the duality functor −∗ = Hom(−, 1) : (A♡ )op →
A♡ is left exact and restricts to an equivalence onto A♡   pro-f in . (2) Continuity re-
quires that each composite fα factors through some πλ . The fact that the (now
continuous) map f : x → limα xα is an isomorphism if and only if each projection
                           ←−
πλ factor through some fβ follows from the dual statement in A♡ . Namely, one
sees that a map from a filtered colimit in which all transition functions are injective
limα yα → y is an isomorphism if and only if each constituent map yα → y factors
−→
through some inclusion yµ → y of a finite length subobject, and the resulting map
48                                      CRIS NEGRON


yα → yµ is injective, and each inclusion yλ → y from a finite length subobject fac-
tors through some yβ → y. The first condition tells us that the map limα yα → y
                                                                       −→
is injective and the latter forces surjectivity.                                 □

                   8. Anomalous bordisms with topology
   We construct a symmetric monoidal ∞-category Bordnc    ∗ of non-compact, anoma-
lous, trivially marked bordisms in 3-dimensions. In Section 9 we use this ∞-
category, and a canonical symmetric monoidal functor ∂out : Bordnc   ∗ → f rDisk, to
construct a symmetric monoidal ∞-category Bordnc      E  of  3-dimensional  E -marked
bordisms which we associate to any f rDisk-monoidal ∞-category q : E ⊗ →
f rDisk⊗ .
   The majority of this section is spent dealing with various technical minutiae and
general tomfoolery. So we actually suggest reading the synopsis provided in Section
8.1, then briskly skimming the remaining contents. The conscientious reader can
return to the section at a later point to digest the details, as the need arises.

8.1. Marked bordisms, in principle. At a basic level, our trivially marked bor-
dism ∞-category Bordnc
                     ∗ appears as follows: Objects are surfaces marked by colored
disks ζ : D × I → Σ. Maps between such marked surfaces Mf : Σζ → Σ′η are 3-
dimensional bordisms M : Σ → Σ′ which are equipped with an additional oriented
embedding of cylinders
                              f : Cyl ×I → M.
This embedding preserves boundaries, and hence defines two disk embeddings at
the boundaries fΣ : D × I → Σ and fΣ′ : D × J → Σ′ . We require that fΣ recovers
the given markings ζ on Σ, and that fΣ′ maps into the image of the markings η
on Σ′ . So, at the outgoing boundary we recover an embedding of colored disks
∂out (f ) : D × I → D × J , i.e. a map in f rDisk.
   For the higher structure, we allow the cylinders f : Cyl ×I → M to move
within M under homotopy. In this way, an n-simplex in Bordnc  ∗ traces out an n-
simplex in f rDisk at the outgoing boundary, and we observe a map of ∞-categories
∂out : Bordnc                                    nc
              ∗ → f rDisk. Furthermore, Bord∗ admits the expected symmetric
monoidal structure under disjoint union, and the map ∂out is the linear term of
a corresponding symmetric monoidal functor to the ∞-category of framed colored
disks
                             ⊗             ⊗         ⊗
                           ∂out  : (Bordnc
                                        ∗ ) → f rDisk .
   Below we take special care to accomodate the anomaly, deal appropriately with
collars around the boundary, mod out by diffeomorphism in dimension 3, etc. How-
ever, for almost all of what is done in this paper, the above description of the bor-
dism category will suffice. So, upon first engagement, the reader might only skim
the contents of this section, then proceed directly to subsequent materials.

8.2. Marked surfaces and bordisms. By a marked surface Σζ we mean a com-
pact oriented surface Σ with a choice of an oriented embedding
                                  ζ : D × I → Σ.
52                                       CRIS NEGRON


A bordism Mf : Σζ → Σ′η between marked surfaces is an oriented 3-dimensional
bordism between the underlying surfaces

     p2 : (Cyla ×I) ×M Σ = D × I → Σ and p′2 : (Cyla ×I) ×M Σ′ = D × I → Σ′
recover the markings on the incoming boundary, and send D × I into the image of
the markings on the outgoing boundary.
   We consider the collar on M only up to equivalence, where two collars are equiv-
alent if they are equalized by a third, i.e. if there is a diagram

                                  Σ × [0, ε) ⨿ Σ̄′ × (1 − ε, 1]

for some small ε > 0.

8.5. The trivially marked bordism category. The ∗-marked, non-anomalous,
simplicial bordism category Bord∗ = Bord3,2 (∗) is the category of oriented surfaces
with colored markings ζ : D × I → Σ, and mapping spaces as in Definition 8.2.
This simplicial category is symmetric monoidal under disjoint union. Note that we
have the symmetric monoidal forgetful functor
                                    Bord∗ → Bord3,2
to the usual (discrete) bordism category of unmarked oriented surfaces and un-
marked bordisms. This non-anomalous bordism category is not precisely what we
want to consider however, as we want to introduce the anomaly.
   First, we have the usual anomalous bordism category Bord3,2 which consists
of oriented surfaces Σ equipped with a Lagrangian λ ⊆ H1 (Σ, R) and bordisms
M : Σ → Σ′ equipped with an integer nM . Composition is defined by gluing
bordisms and translating the integer parameters via the Maslov index. See for
example [22, Section 4.3].
   The anomalous bordism category is again symmetric monoidal under disjoint
union, and we have the symmetric monoidal forgetful functor Bord3,2 → Bord3,2 .
We then construct the marked, anomalous, simplicial bordism category via pullback
              Bord∗ := Bord3,2 ×Bord3,2 Bord∗                         / Bord∗      (29)


                                                                         
                           Bord3,2                                   / Bord3,2 .

Here the objects are marked surfaces Σζ,λ with a Lagrangian, and the mapping
complexes are the fiber products
               HomBord (Σλ , Σ′λ′ ) ×HomBord (Σ,Σ′ ) HomBord∗ (Σζ , Ση ).
This is again a symmetric monoidal simplicial category under disjoint union.

                                Bord∗ := N(Bord∗ ),
were Bord∗ is as in (29)
8.6. Anomalous notations! Throughout this document we work with anomalous
bordisms Bord∗ , but we always ignore the anomaly data both in our notations and
in our arguments. We only work in the anomalous setting because our theories are,
quite simply, anomalous.
8.7. Non-compact bordisms. The non-compact bordism category Bordnc         3,2 in
Bord3,2 is the symmetric monoidal subcategory consisting of all surfaces but only
those bordisms M : Σ → Σ′ in which each component Mc in M has non-vanishing
outgoing boundary.
   In the marked setting, we have the full symmetric monoidal simplicial subcat-
egory Bordsurf.nc
            ∗     of surfaces with nontrivial markings. Specifically, we consider
marked surfaces D × I → Σ in which each component Σc in Σ is marked by at least
one disk. We take the fiber product with the anomalous, unmarked, non-compact
bordisms to get the symmetric monoidal simplicial category
                      Bordnc      nc               surf.nc
                          ∗ = Bord3,2 ×Bord3,2 Bord∗       .
Definition 8.4. The non-compact ∗-marked bordism ∞-category Bordnc
                                                                ∗ is the
homotopy coherent nerve
                          Bordnc          nc
                              ∗ := N(Bord∗ ).

   The ∞-category Bordnc
                       ∗ admits the apparent description as the subcategory in
Bord∗ spanned by nontrivially marked surfaces and those bordisms Mf : Σζ → Σ′η
for which each component in the underlying 3-manifold Mc ⊆ M has non-vanishing
outgoing boundary.
Remark 8.5. The term “non-compact” is more about the possible targets for a
symmetric monoidal functor L : Bordnc    ⋆ → V . The point is that coevaluation
bordisms coev : Σ̄ ⨿ Σ → ∅ are omitted when we move from Bord⋆ to Bordnc     ⋆ , so
that the value of such a field theory L on a surface is allowed to be non-compact,
or more specifically non-rigid, in V in the non-compact setting.
Remark 8.6. When working in the non-compact setting there is an apparent
choice of orientation. Namely, by applying the orientation reversal equivalence
         ∼
Bordop
     3,2 → Bord3,2 we exchange outgoing and incoming boundaries, and hence ex-
change a non-compact category of bordisms with non-vanishing outgoing boundary
with an alternate non-compact category of bordisms with non-vanishing incoming
boundary. One should think of this incoming vs outgoing choice as largely an issue
of preference. For example, we prefer to allow for vanishing incoming boundary so
that S 2 is an algebra in Bordnc
                              3,2 , rather than a coalgebra, and so that all surfaces
in Bord3,2 are S -modules in Bordnc
        nc       2
                                     3,2 .

8.8. Disk arrangements at the boundary. We have a symmetric monoidal
simplicial functor dout : Bord∗ → f rDisk given by intersecting at the outgoing
boundary. In particular, we send each marked surface ζ : D × I → Σ to the colored
marking set I in f rDisk and for an n-simplex
                              σ : |∆n | × Cyl ×I → M,

                    9. An ∞-category of marked bordisms
   Given a f rDisk-monoidal ∞-category q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ , we define an associ-
ated symmetric monoidal ∞-category Bordnc  E of E -marked bordisms. In the case
of cochains A = Ch(A♡ ) over a ribbon tensor category A♡ , we describe the bor-
dism ∞-category Bordnc A in explicit terms. In Section 10 we show, subsequently,
that results from [22, 20] imply the existence of a symmetric monoidal functor
LAf in : Bordnc                                            ♡
             Af in → Ch(Vect) whenever the input category A is finite and modu-
lar.

boundary are compensated for algebraically via the appropriate application of the
ribbon structure on A− or A+ .
9.3. Including bounding constraints. We recall that we have fully faithful in-
clusions
                      A⊗         / A⊗ o      A⊗
                       f in                    l-f in


                                       #  z
                                     f rDisk
for the f rDisk-categories of finite and locally finite complexes (Proposition 7.11).
Pulling back, we obtain full, symmetric monoidal subcategories
                         BordAf in → BordA ← BordAl-f in
spanned by surfaces labeled by finite and locally finite cochains, respectively. In
particular, the above explicit description of BordA restricts to provide explicit de-
scriptions of both BordAf in and BordAl-f in .
9.4. Comments on general marking schemes. Given a symmetric monoidal
∞-category T ⊗ , and a corresponding “T -flavored” bordism ∞-category, by which
we mean a symmetric monoidal ∞-category of bordisms with a symmetric monoidal
functor
                           Bord⊗triv → T
                                         ⊗
                                           × Bord3,2 ,
we obtain a corresponding class of marked bordisms just as above. Namely, for
any symmetric monoidal cocartesian fibration A ⊗ → T ⊗ we get an ∞-category of
A -marked bordisms via pullback
                           BordA = Bordtriv ×T ⊗ A ⊗ .
This situation is what we would call a 3-dimensional T -flavored marking scheme.
   We’ve provided above a 3-dimensional f rDisk-flavored marking scheme, but
certainly there are other natural schemes one might consider. We have some inter-
est, for example, in the case where T is the 2-dimensional bordism category (see
[16, 9]). Though we operate within the scheme of framed disks, we note that many
of our arguments through the remainder of the text apply equally well under any
marking scheme.
   The main point of contention, as far as our arguments are concerned, is the exis-
tence (or non-existence) of an abelian theory for cochains under the given scheme.

        10. Discrete Lyubashenko-Reshetikhin-Turaev theories
  For any modular tensor category A♡ –which we recall is a special type of rib-
bon tensor category–we construct a symmetric monoidal functor LA : Bordnc    A →
Ch(Vect)pro-f in from the ∞-category of A-labeled bordisms. We describe the values
LA (Σx ) for a connected marked surface Σ via mapping complexes in A.
  The field theory LA is constructed by completing pre-existing TQFTs from De
Renzi-Gainutdinov-Geer-Patureau-Mirand-Runkel [22] and Czenky-Negron [20].

10.2. Merging bordisms. Let ζ : D × I → Σ be a marked connected surface. A
basic merging bordism for Σζ is a bordism Mf : Σζ → Ση to the same surface Σ
equipped with a single positive marking η : D → Σ, and with Mf satisfying the
following:

    (0) Σ admits an auxiliary embedding τ : D → Σ which contains the images of
        both ζ and η in τ (D).
    (1) The underlying 3-manifold for Mf is the product Σ × [0, 1].
    (2) The mappings from the cylinders f : Cyl ×I → Σ × [0, 1] factor through
        the image of the auxiliary disk embedding τ : D → Σ.
    (3) The corresponding map

               f ×(Σ×[0,1]) (D × [0, 1]) : Cyl ×I = D × [0, 1] × I → D × [0, 1]


          is induced by a smooth homotopy h : |∆1 | × D × I → D, in the sense that
          f (z, t, i) = (h(t, z, i), t).
62                                    CRIS NEGRON


For example, a basic merging bordism might appear as
                     τ (D)                                τ (D)
                                          Mf




                                                                   .

Σ, a merging bordism Mf : Σζ → Ση is a bordism which decomposes over the

Lemma 10.3. For any marked surface Σζ in Bord∗ , there is a choice of single
positive markings η : D × π0 (Σ) → Σ on the components of Σ which admits a
merging bordism Mf : Σζ → Ση . We may assume further that the outgoing diagram
∂out (Mf ) lies in Linf rDisk.

Proof. It suffices to deal with the case where Σ is connected with specified markings
ζ : D × I → Σ. We consider a path [0, 1] → Σ which passes through the centers of
the ζ(Di ), and by taking a tubular neighborhood we obtain an embedding from a
disk η : D → Σ which contains the centers of the ζ(Di ) in its interior. We can take
a smooth homotopy κ : |∆1 | × D × I → Σ which shrinks the image of each Di so
that ζ ′ = κ1 : D × I → Σ has image in η(D).
    We consider now ζ ′ as a disk embedding ζ ′ : D × I → D by pulling back along
η, and we are free to assume that the images of the disks Di are arbitrarily small
in D. In particular, we assume that each Di has image in the interior Do . We
consider, for each index i, the differential A−1
                                               i   = T0 (ζ ′ )i ∈ GL2 (R). Since Ai has
positive determinant we can connect the identity matrix I2 to Ai via a smooth path
Ai (t) : |∆1 | → GL2 (R).
    We take now A(t) : |∆1 | × D × I → D with A(t)|Di = s−1        i Ai (t)si where si :
R → R2 is the translation which sends the center of ζ ′ (Di ) to 0. Since the disk
  2

is compact, we can assume the images ζ ′ (Di ) are sufficiently small so that A(t) is
a diffeomorphism at each time t which does in fact have image in the unit disk
D ⊆ R2 . Then ζ ′′ = A(1) : D × I → D is a disk embedding which sends the center
of each Di to the x-axis in D and has differentials T0 (ζ ′′ )i = idR2 . We are free to
assume that κ(t) and A(t) are constant in neighborhoods of both 0 and 1, so that
we obtain smooth homotopies

give LA (Nβ Mα ) = LA (Nβ ) LA (Mβ ). For general z the equality LA (Nβ Mα ) =

Definition 10.7. For a finite modular tensor category A♡ , and A = Ch(A♡ ), the
discrete LRT theory is the symmetric monoidal functor
                             LA : Bordnc

constructed in Theorem 10.1 and Proposition 10.6.

Remark 10.8. The letters LRT reference Lyubashenko, Reshetikhin, and Turaev.
It would also be reasonable to reference De Renzi, Gainutdinov, Geer, Patureau-
Mirans, and Runkel here, though the label DGGPR is a bit too rambunctious for
our liking.
10.5. State spaces for discrete LRT.
Lemma 10.9. Let A♡ be a finite modular tensor category, and A = Ch(A♡ ). For
any connected genus g surface Σ with fixed markings ζ : D × I → Σ, and any map
f : D×I → D to a positively colored disk in f rDisk, there is a natural isomorphism
                                       ∼
                              LA (Σ− ) → Hom∗A (C ⊗g , mf −)
of functors from (Bordnc       I
                      A )Σζ = A to Ch(Vect)pro-f in .

Proof. We note that all embeddings f : D × I → D are smoothly homotopic
(Proposition A.2) so that all of the associated product functors mf : AI → A+ =
Apro-f in , i.e. all transport functors along the maps f , are naturally isomorphic. It
follows that the functor Hom∗A (C ⊗g , mf −) is independent of the choice of f , up to
natural isomorphism.
   Now, for any marking object x in AI with structural expression x = limλ xλ ,
                                                                 ∼

   We recall that any product functor mf : AI → A+ = Apro-f in sends homotopy
equivalences in each factor to homotopy equivalences in Apro-f in , and hence sends
any product of homotopy equivalences ξ : x → x′ to a homotopy equivalence
in Apro-f in . This follows by the duality of Corollary 7.9 and the fact that the
product on Ch(A♡ ) preserves homotopy equivalence in each factor. Furthermore,
the functor
                    Hom∗A (C ⊗g , −) : Apro-f in → Ch(Vect)pro-f in
preserves homotopy equivalences as well. It follows from the identification of Lemma
10.9, and symmetric monoidality, that the LRT theory preserves homotopy equiv-
alences.

               11. Deriving LRT at the 1-categorical level
   Consider the discrete derived category D = D(A♡ ) of a modular tensor category
A♡ . We have the induced ribbon structure on D and corresponding duality equiv-
                        ∼
alence −∗ : D(A♡ )op → D(A♡                                               ♡
                               pro-f in ). This equivalence gives D(A ) a f rDisk-
            ⊗             ⊗
structure D → f rDisk via Proposition 7.1, and we produce the subsequent
marked bordism category Bordnc  D.
   Low dimensional simplices in Bordnc    D are described precisely as in Section 9.2,
from which one deduces an explicit description of the homotopy truncation h Bordnc    D.
Vaguely, h Bordnc
                D is (equivalent to)  a symmetric  monoidal   category   of surfaces with
markings from the discrete categories D(A♡ ) and D(A♡   pro-f in ), and ribbon bordisms
between such surfaces. The underlying graphs for such ribbon bordisms are required
to be trees in which each component has a unique internal vertex, and a unique
external vertex which is attached to the outgoing surface.
Theorem 11.1. Let A♡ be a finite modular tensor category with corresponding
discrete derived category D = D(A♡ ). There is a symmetric monoidal functor
                           LD : h Bordnc
                                      D → D(Vectpro-f in )

which is obtained by “deriving” the theory from Proposition 10.6.
   We note that LD is a map between 1-categories, and can therefore be approached
in completely 1-categorical terms. We outline a proof of Theorem 11.1 below in
two parts. Following the proof, we discuss various means of “expanding” the theory
from Theorem 11.1, and requisite liftings to the ∞-categorical setting.

11.1. Step 1: Introducing the homotopy category. We consider the homo-
topy category K = K(A♡ ) with its f rDisk-structure induced by the duality equiv-
                     ∼
alence −∗ : K(A♡ )op → K(A♡pro-f in ). We show that discrete LRT induces a unique
symmetric monoidal functor from h Bordnc K which fits into a diagram

     One can show that the functor LD : h Bordnc
                                              D → D(Vectpro-f in ) is a left Kan

can also check that the state spaces for LD over a genus g marked surface Σζ are
identified with derived Homs
                                    ∼
                          LD (Σ− ) → RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , mf −),
in direct analogy with Lemma 10.9 (cf. Proposition 15.5). From this description
of the state spaces one sees that LD restricts to a D(Vect) valued theory from the
full subcategory of surfaces labeled by objects in the bounded derived category
Db (A♡f in ) of finite length complexes, i.e. that we can forgo pro-finite completion in
the finite bounded setting,
                            LDf in : h Bordnc
                                           Df in → D(Vect).

11.3. Unmarking the discrete derived theory. We recall that all nonempty
surfaces in h Bordnc
                   D are equipped with markings from the discrete derived category.
In a skein theoretic setting, we could remove all such markings by employing unit
labels on surfaces and noting that the particular positions of unit markings do not
matter, modulo skein relations.
   In our setting we can still label all surfaces by the identity object 1D in Db (A♡
                                                                                    f in )
to obtain a symmetric inclusion
                                h Bordnc        nc
                                      ∗ → h BordDf in

from the trivially marked bordism category. We then institute certain coarse skein
relations on h Bordnc
                    ∗ via a localization process which we outline in Section 16.
While we defer the details, and direct the interested reader to Sections 16 and 17
below, let us record our ultimate conclusion.
Theorem (17.10). For any finite modular tensor category A♡ , the discrete derived
theory from Theorem 11.1 induces an unmarked, anomalous topological field theory
in 3 dimensions
                          LDf in : Bordnc
                                       3,2 → D(Vect).
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                           69


For each genus g surface Σ, the corresponding state space admits a natural identi-
fication with derived Homs LDf in (Σ) ∼
                                      = RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , 1).
   As we explain in Section 17.6, the existence of such a field theory implies a
natural, projective action of each mapping class group MCG(Σ) on the associated
derived Homs RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , 1) (cf. [51, 72]). We also discuss implications in
dimension 3 in Section 19.

11.4. Expanding from Theorem 11.1? At the center of our project, even be-
yond the present paper, is the following question:
   – To what extent do the symmetric monoidal functors LD and LDf in expand into
a system of (T)QFTs, in analogy with the fusion or 1-categorical settings? –
  To list a few explicit points:
(A) For a given finite modular tensor category A♡ , is there a once extended, anoma-
lous 3-d topological theory L′ whose circle value L′ (S 1 ) recovers the derived category
for A♡ ? Does such an extended theory imply a natural marking scheme which su-
persedes the one employed in our construction of LD ? (See Conjectures 17.12 and
18.5 below, as well as [4, 21, 50] for the 1-categorical setting.)
(B) Is there an extended 4-dimensional topological theory associated to the derived
category of a modular tensor category, and can we recover our 3-dimensional theo-
ries from such a 4-d theory via a boundary construction? (See Question 18.8 below
and compare with [39].)
(C) Is there an effective notion of skeins, and skein relations, for the derived cate-
gory which allows one to calculate values in the 4-dimensional theory from (b)? In
particular, can one calculate factorization homology using skeins? Thinking further
about (a), can this skein theory be used to implement skein relations into a suffi-
ciently receptive 3-dimensional marking scheme? (See again Sections 2 and 9.4,
Question 18.9 below, and compare with [14, 10].)
   Questions (A)–(C) all ask, in the non-semisimple/derived setting, if there is an
ecosystem of interconnected topological field theories which parallel those one finds
in the fusion setting, for say Crane-Yetter vs. Chern-Simons/Reshetikhin-Turaev
theories. We record, finally, a principle concerning flat connections which is unique
to the non-semisimple world.
(D) Does the theory LD , or some enhanced version of it, couple naturally to flat
connections? (See Conjectures 18.10 and 18.11 below, and also [47].)
    We defer further discussions of point (D) to Section 18, though it is certainly a
deep and interesting story, both in mathematics and in physics.
    Thinking seriously about any of the questions above requires one to think about
the derived category in some locale where one has an effective theory of categories.
It is apparent that the triangulated setting will not suffice for this task, which then
prompts our transition to the world of ∞-categories.

11.5. Derivating LRT in ∞-categories. The remainder of the text–or more
precisely, the next five sections–is dedicated to the construction of a lift for the
marked theory from Theorem 11.1 to the level of ∞-categories. We suggest this lift
as a projection of a more expansive system of TQFTs, as proposed above, and so as
an indication that such a system should in fact exist. We return to the fundamental
70                                    CRIS NEGRON


questions (A–D), and provide a more thorough discussion of these issues, in Section
18 at the conclusion of the text.
   In lifting the theory LD to the homotopical setting, we follows essentially the
same processes outlined above. We begin in Sections 12 and 13 with the movement
from the abelian category of cochains to the homotopy ∞-category, then Kan extend
to produce a theory for the derived ∞-category. In comparing with Step 1 above,
we note that the homotopy ∞-category K (A♡ ) is no longer obtained from chains
over A♡ via a quotient construction, but via localization.
Remark 11.2. The methods employed below are not sensitive to our specific choice
of marking scheme (see Section 9.4). What’s required is the existence of a preced-
ing abelian theory at the level of cochains, for the given marking scheme, as in
Proposition 10.6.

                12. Localization for monoidal ∞-categories
   We claim that the discrete LRT theory LA : Bordnc      A → Ch(Vect)pro-f in from
Proposition 10.6 localizes to provide a symmetric monoidal functor, i.e. field theory,
from a bordism ∞-category Bordnc  K with labels in the homotopy ∞-category. While
this is more-or-less an immediate consequence of Corollary 10.10, one needs to
understand the behaviors of localization when applied to various types of fibrations
in order to effectively proceed in this direction.
   In this section we present an overview of localization for cocartesian fibrations,
and monoidal ∞-categories, which essentially follows work of Hinich on the topic
[43]. (See also [57, Propositions 2.2.1.9 & 4.1.7.4] and [65, Appendix A].)

12.1. Vertical localization. Let W be a collection of morphisms in an ∞-category
C . Recall that a localization for C along W is the choice of a functor F : C → C ′
for which restriction
                           F ∗ : Fun(C ′ , D) → Fun(C , D),
at arbitrary D, provides an equivalence onto the full subcategory Fun(C , D)W ⊆
Fun(C , D) spanned by those functors which send all morphisms in W to isomor-
phisms in D. Via a pushout construction [58, 05ZL], or fibrant replacement [56,
Proposition 3.1.3.7], we understand that localizations always exist.
Proposition 12.1 ([43, Proposition 2.1.4]). Let q : C → T be a cocartesian
fibration and W ⊆ C [1] be a collection of edges. Suppose that every edge in W lies
in the fiber Ct over some object t in T , that each fiber Wt = W ∩ Ct [1] is stable
under isomorphism in Fun(∆1 , Ct ), and that the transport functor f! : Ct → Cu
over each map f : t → u in T sends Wt into Wu . Then there is a pairing of a
localization F : C → C [W −1 ] with a map q ′ : C [W −1 ] → T which fts into a strictly
commuting diagram
                              C
                                               F    / C [W −1 ]                   (34)

                                  q                q′
                                              {
                                           T             ,
and for which the following hold:
    (i) q ′ : C [W −1 ] → T is an isofibration.
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                                71


   (ii) For any isofibration D → T , restriction provides an equivalence
                                                       ∼
                     F ∗ : FunT (C [W −1 ], D) → FunT (C , D)W .
   (iii) q ′ : C [W −1 ] → T is a cocartesian fibration, and F is a map of cocartesian
         fibrations over T .
   (iv) For each t in T the induced map on the fibers Ft : Ct → (C [W −1 ])t realizes
         the target category as a localization for Ct along the class Wt .
    (v) For any cocartesian fibration D → T , restriction provides an equivalence

  As in Section 3.11, FunT (C , D)W denotes the fiber

base are unique, this just follows from the fact that F itself preserves cocartesian
edges.                                                                                □
Definition 12.2. Given a cocartesian fibration q : C → T , a collection of edges W
in C is said to be a vertical class over T if it satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition
12.1. Given such such a vertical class W , a vertical localization over T is a choice
of strictly commuting diagram

in which q is a cocartesian fibration and F is a localization functor which preserves
cocartesian edges.
   Proposition 12.1 says that such vertical localizations for cocartesian fibrations
always exist. We also observe that vertical localizations are stable under pullback.
Proposition 12.3. Let q : C → T be a cocartesian fibration, W ⊆ C [1] be a
vertical class over T , and C [W −1 ] → T be the associated vertical localization over

is an equivalence of cocartesian fibrations over R.
Proof. We note that the fibers of CR over R are identified with the fibers of C over
T , and under these identifications the transport functors for CR are identified with
the transport functors for C . So it is clear that WR is a vertical class. We consider
the claim that the map (36) is a equivalence.
   By Proposition 12.1 (v), the map of cocartesian fibrations CR → R ×T C [W −1 ]
induced by the map of cocartesian fibrations C → C [W −1 ] localizes to provide a
map of cocartesian fibrations

over R. This is our functor (36). Note that for each object r : ∗ → R, with image

and Proposition 12.1 (iv), we find that each fiber natr over R is an equivalence. It

12.2. Aside: Generation of classes.
Lemma 12.4 (Morphism generation). Let E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be a f rDisk-category
and W ± be classes of maps in the underlying ∞-categories E ± which are stable
under isomorphism in Fun(∆1 , E ± ) and contain all identities. Suppose also that
     • The transition map d : E − → E + sends W − into W + .
     • The product functors m : E ± × E ± → E ± send (W ± )2 into W ± .

                                                                             ⊗

E .

   To recall, the transition functor d : E − → E + is the transport functor along the
color negating identity id : D− → D+ , and the product functors m : E ± × E ± are
the transport functors along any like colored disk embedding D ⨿ D → D.

Proof. For the union W = ∪(I,µ) W(I,µ) , stability under transport follows by the
description of transport along maps in f rDisk⊗ provide in Lemma 6.12.         □

Definition 12.5. For a f rDisk-category q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ and classes W ± ⊆
E ± [1] as in Lemma 12.4, we call the corresponding collection W ⊆ E ⊗ [1] from
Lemma 12.4 the class generated by W ± .

12.3. Localization for monoidal ∞-categories. In general, one can use Propo-
sition 12.1 to effectively localize O-monoidal ∞-categories along classes of maps
which are stable under any collection of “generating morphisms” in O ⊗ , for a given
∞-operad or equifibered symmetric monoidal ∞-category O ⊗ . We cover two spe-
cific instances which are relevant for us.
Proposition 12.6. Let q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be a f rDisk-category, and W ± be col-
lections of edges in the underlying ∞-categories E ± which are stable under isomor-
phism in Fun(∆1 , E ± ) and contain all identities. Suppose that the W ± are stable
under the product functors on E ± as well as the transition functor d : E − → E + .
Then the localization q ′ : E ⊗ [W −1 ] → f rDisk⊗ along the corresponding vertical
class (see Lemma 12.4) is a f rDisk-monoidal ∞-category.
Proof. Take G ⊗ = E ⊗ [W −1 ]. By Proposition 12.1, q ′ : G ⊗ → f rDisk is a co-
cartesian fibration and the fibers of the localization functor E ⊗ → G ⊗ induce

     The analogous statement for symmetric monoidal ∞-categories is well-known.
Proposition 12.7 ([43, Proposition 3.2.2]). Let q : F ⊗ → Fin∗ be a symmetric
monoidal ∞-category, and W0 ⊆ F [1] be a class of maps which is stable under
isomorphism in Fun(∆1 , F ) and contains all identities. Suppose also that W0 is
stable under the product functor m : F × F → F , and define W ⊆ F ⊗ [1] to be
the collection of edges in the varied fibers (F ⊗ )I which are sent to W I under the
                                ∼
transport equivalences (F ⊗ )I → F I .
   The collection W is a vertical class in F ⊗ and the corresponding cocartesian
fibration q ′ : F ⊗ [W −1 ] → Fin∗ realizes F ⊗ [W −1 ] as a symmetric monoidal ∞-
category.
  One can show that in fact all “symmetric monoidal localizations” are of the form
considered in Proposition 12.7. We record a variation on this point.

12.4. Derived ∞-categories as f rDisk-categories. Let A♡ be a (discrete) rib-
bon tensor category and q : A⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be the corresponding f rDisk-monoidal
∞-category of unbounded cochains. Recall the values
           A− = Aop = Ch(A♡ )op and A+ = Apro-f in = Ch(A♡
                                                         pro-f in ).

We have the classes Htop± ⊆ A± [1] of homotopy equivalences. These classes are
stable under duality −∗ : A− → A+ and under the tensor products A± ×A± → A± .
Stability under the tensor product, in particular, follows from the fact that the
tensor product preserves homotopy equivalences between morphisms in each factor.
We then have the class Htop in A⊗ generated by {Htop− , Htop+ }, and localization
produces a f rDisk-category
                              qK : K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗
whose underlying ∞-categories are the homotopy ∞-categories
         K − = K op = K (A♡ )op and K + = Kpro-f in = K (A♡
                                                          pro-f in ),

by the calculations K ± = A± [(Htop± )−1 ] [57, Proposition 1.3.4.5]. We note that
the localization qK : K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ is the unique f rDisk-category which admits
a monoidal functor
                          A⊗                       /K⊗

                                    f rDisk⊗
with the fibers A± → K ± over the ±-colored disks inverting homotopy equivalences

   We can also consider the collections Qiso± ⊆ A± [1] of quasi-isomorphisms. These
classes are again stable under duality and tensoring, by Proposition 7.10, and hence

                          13. Homotopy LRT theories
   We construct a homotopical variant LK : Bordnc    K → V ectpro-f in of the discrete
Lyubashenko-Reshetikhin-Turaev theory from Section 10, and calculate the state
spaces in this theory. The state spaces are, in particular, identified with inner
mapping spaces for the action of V ectpro-f in on K (A♡  pro-f in ).
   In order to tame the topology in the target category, we show that LK also
restricts to a V ect-valued TQFT LKl-f in : Bordnc
                                                 Kl-f in → V ect from the ∞-category
of bordisms with labels in the homotopy ∞-category of locally finite cochains.

13.1. Homotopical vector spaces.

Definition 13.1. We take
     V ect := Ch(Vect)[Qiso−1 ] and V ectpro-f in := Ch(Vect)pro-f in [Qiso−1 ],
and refer to these localizations as the ∞-categories of homotopical vector spaces
and homotopical pro-finite vector spaces, respectively.

   Since quasi-isomorphisms are preserved under the tensor products on Ch(Vect)
and Ch(Vect)pro-f in , by Proposition 7.10, we see that these localizations inherit
unique symmetric monoidal structures under which the localization functors Ch(Vect)⋆ →
V ect⋆ are symmetric monoidal functors. This follows by Proposition 12.7.

Remark 13.2. As with any abelian category, one can explicitly construct the ∞-
categories V ect and V ectpro-f in by taking the dg nerves of the dg categories of
unbounded cochains Chdg (Vect) and Chdg (Vectpro-f in ), respectively [57, Proposi-
tion 1.3.4.5]. (We note that in this case the classes of homotopy equivalences and
quasi-isomorphisms agree, so that there are no distinctions between the homotopy
and derived ∞-categories.)
   However, one can also construct V ect as the stabilization of the ∞-category
K ank of linear Kan complexes–aka simplicial vector spaces.

   We observe that the pair of fully faithful, symmetric monoidal functors from
Ch± (Vectf in ) to Ch(Vect) and Ch(Vect)pro-f in (see Section 7.4) localizes to produce
fully faithful, symmetric monoidal embeddings

                14. Adjoints and Kan extension over a base
   From our theory LK : Bordnc    K → V ectpro-f in for the homotopy ∞-category we
can produce a theory LD : Bordnc    D → V ectpro-f in for the derived ∞-category as
well. We proceed, in particular, via a Kan extension along the localization functor
Bordnc            nc
      K → BordD . In order to control this extension to a sufficient degree–for
example, in order to calculate the state spaces in LD –we want to understand what
such a Kan extension actually looks like.
   In this section we give a general presentation of relative Kan extension, and
relative adjoints for fibrations over a fixed base. We employ relative constructions
in order to account for behaviors of monoidal structures under Kan extension, for
example.
Remark 14.1. There is a more complicated notion of operadic Kan extension [57,
Definition 3.1.2.2]. For our purposes, we expect that one can employ operadic Kan
extension instead of relative Kan extension, and obtain the same output. So the
distinction is not a deep one for us, and the reader might consult [57, Section 3.1.2]
for an alternate point of view.
14.1. Relative adjoints.
Definition 14.2. We say a functor between isofibrations

                                C
                                          F                /E

                                     q                 p
                                                   ~
                                          T
admits a right adjoint relative to T if there is another functor

                                E
                                          G                /C

                                    p                  q
                                               ~
                                          T
and a transformation ε : GF → idC in FunT (C , C ) which exhibits G as right

  We note that this notion is symmetric with respect to units and counits.

  Since the unit and counit transformations have constant projections to the base,
