14.2. Adjoints and (relative) Kan extension. Let l : C → C ′ be a functor
between ∞-categories. We recall that a left Kan extension for a functor F : C → E ,
along l, is the choice of a functor F ′ : C ′ → E and transformation α : F → F ′ l
which realizes, at each x in C ′ , the value F ′ (x) as a colimit of the diagram

                                   f orget   8C      F

                                C/x                      /& E .

(See [58, 02Y7].) Here C/x is the relative overcategory
                                  C/x := C ×C ′ C/x
                                                 ′
                                                    ,
i.e. the ∞-category which parametrizes objects x̃ in C equipped with a specified
map l(x̃) → x.
    The following is standard in 1-category theory, and should be well-known in the
∞-categorical setting as well.
Lemma 14.6. Let l : C → C ′ be any functor which admitis a right adjoint R :
C ′ → C . Then any functor F : C → E admits a left Kan extension along l, and
this Kan extension can be calculated as the composite F ′ = F R : C ′ → E along
with the shifted unit transformation F (u) : F → F ′ l.
Proof. At each x in C ′ we have the transformation
                                  F (u)           F ′ (can)
                           F |C/x → F ′ l|C/x       →         F ′ (x)               (42)
in Fun(C/x , E ), where F ′ (x) is the constant diagram of value F ′ (x). The transfor-
mation F ′ l|C/x → F ′ (x) is obtained as follows: We have the coslice equivalence
                     ∼
                C/x → C ′ ×Fun({0},C ′ ) Fun(∆1 , C ′ ) ×Fun({1},C ′ ) {x}
[58, 02GH] which specifies, and is specified by the choice of a canonical transfor-
mation
                               can : ∗ → Fun(∆1 × C/x , C ′ )
with can0 = l|C/x and can1 = x. Rather, the coslice equivalence chooses a
transformation can : l|C/x → x, and we apply F ′ to obtain the transformation
F ′ (can) : F ′ l|C/x → F ′ (x). All we need to know about this transformation is
that it evaluates at each object tx̃ = (x̃, t : l(x̃) → x) in Cx/ to the chosen map
cantx̃ = t : l(x) → x.
    To say that F ′ is a left Kan extension for F along l is, by definition, to say that
the transformation (42) realizes F ′ (x) as a colimit for the diagram F |C/x , for each
x in C ′ . Let us denote the transformation (42) by canF   u and argue this point.
    Since the functor l admits a right adjoint R : C ′ → C , the counit map εx :
lR(x) → x is a terminal object (R(x), εx ) in the relative overcategory Cx/ [58,
                                                           ′
02J9, 038S]. Hence the transformation canF     u realizes F (x) as a colimit of the given
diagram if and only if its restriction along the inclusion (R(x), εx ) : ∗ → C/x realizes
F ′ (x) as a colimit for the trivial diagram F R(x) : ∗ → E [58, 02XW]. But now we
simply observe
                                                                          
                   F                     F (uR(x) )         F R(εx )
              (canu )(Rx,εx ) = F R(x) → F RlR(x) → F R(x) .
86                                   CRIS NEGRON


By the definition of a unit and counit for an adjunction [58, 02EJ] this composite is
isomorphic to the identity, and in particular an isomorphism. Hence canF      u |(Rx,εx )
                                              ′
is a colimit diagram, canF  u  realizes all F   (x) as a colimit for the corresponding
diagram F |C/x , and F ′ is realized as a left Kan extension for F .                  □
     As with adjoints, we are interested in a relative notion of Kan extension.
Definition 14.7. Consider a partial diagram of isofibrations

                                                             7/ E
                                                 F
                                 C
                                         l   '
                                                 C′
                                     q           q′      p

                                                   
                                                 T             .
A left Kan extension F ′ : C ′ → E of F along l is said to be a relative left Kan
extension over T if F ′ is a map over T and if the given transformation α : F → F ′ l
satisfies p(α) = idq .
     We obtain immediately a relative version of Lemma 14.6.
Lemma 14.8. Consider isofibrations q : C → T and q ′ : C ′ → T . If l : C → C ′
admits a right adjoint over T then any functor F : C → E over T admits a left
Kan extension over T . In particular, for such a right adjoint {R, ε}, any choice
u : idC → Rl of a unit over T provides a left Kan extension {F R, F (u)} over T .
Remark 14.9. We expect that one can establish the existence of relative Kan
extensions, in the cocartesian setting, by checking at the fibers (cf. [56, Proposition
4.3.3.10]). However, we have no need for such a result.
14.3. Homotopy vs. derived ∞-categories. We consider a standard example
concerning the construction of the derived ∞-category from the homotopy ∞-
category, via reflective localization.
   Consider a finitely generated linear abelian category A♡ , in the precise sense
of Definition C.8, and its homotopy ∞-category K = K (A♡ ). Suppose also
that A♡ has enough projectives, so that K has enough K-projective complexes.
Explicitly, we assume that each complex x admits a quasi-isomorphism P → x from
a complexes P for which the functor Hom∗A (P, −) preserves quasi-isomorphisms, and
hence for which the inner-Hom functor MapsK (P, −) preserves quasi-isomorphisms.
See for example [48, Theorems 2.3, 2.6] or [25, Lemma 13.3].
   Dually, the ∞-category Kpro-f in = K (A♡   pro-f in ) admits enough K-injectives.
We take
                 Inj K − = (Proj K )op and Inj K + = Inj Kpro-f in
the full subcategories spanned by K-injectives in K − = K (A♡ )op and K + =
K (A♡ )pro-f in , respectively. We note that isomorphisms in these subcategories,
which are homotopy equivalences, agree precisely with quasi-isomorphisms, and
the statement that we have enough K-projectives is precisely the statement that
the full subcategories
                                   Inj K ± ⊆ K ±
are reflective [58, 01F5]. We therefore obtain the following.
           TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        87


Lemma 14.10. Let A♡ be a finitely generated linear abelian category with enough
projectives, and take K ± one of K (A♡ )op or K (A♡ pro-f in ). The fully faithful
inclusion R± : Inj K ± → K ± admits a left adjoint
                                l : K ± → Inj K ± .
Furthermore, this left adjoint realizes Inj K ± as a localization of K ± along the
class of quasi-isomorphisms.
Proof. Given the above analysis, this is an immediate consequence of reflective
localization. See in particular [58, 02FA, 02FE, 04JL].                      □
  Uniqueness for localizations now gives the following.
Corollary 14.11. Let A♡ be a finitely generated linear abelian category with enough
projectives, and let D ± = K ± [Qiso−1 ] denote one of the derived ∞-categories
D − = D(A♡ )op or D + = D(A♡pro-f in ). The following hold:
   (1) The localization functor loc : K ± → D ± admits a fully faithful right adjoint
       R± : D ± → K ± .
   (2) For each x in K ± , the unit transformation ux : x → R± ◦ loc(x) is a
       quasi-isomorphism, and for each y in D ± the counit transformation εy :
       loc ◦ R± (y) → y is an isomorphism.
   (3) The right adjoint R± is an equivalence onto the full subcategory Inj K ±
       spanned by K-injectives in K ± .
                                                                     ∼
   (4) The localization functor restricts to an equivalence Inj K ± → D ± .
   Let us suppose now that A♡ is a finite ribbon tensor category and consider
the corresponding cocartesian fibration q : K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ for the homotopy
∞-category (see Section 12.4). We consider the full subcategories Inj K ± and, as

                           Inj Kt⊗              / Inj K I

                                         ρ!

We have the following particular instance of Lemma 6.9.
Lemma 14.12. Let A♡ be a finite ribbon tensor category. The subcategory Inj K ⊗
of K-injective complexes is a symmetric monoidal ∞-category, and the inclusion
Inj K ⊗ → K ⊗ is a symmetric monoidal functor. Furthermore, Inj K ⊗ is stable
under isomorphism in K ⊗ .
   As a consequence of isoclosure we see that the structure map K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗
restricts to an isofibration Inj K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
Proposition 14.13. Suppose A♡ is a finite ribbon tensor category. For q : K ⊗ →
f rDisk⊗ and q ′ : D ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ the corresponding cocartesian fibration for the

    (1) The (vertical) localization functor loc : K ⊗ → D ⊗ admits a relative right
        adjoint R : D ⊗ → K ⊗ over f rDisk⊗ .
88                                   CRIS NEGRON


     (2) The right adjoint R is fully faithful, and an equivalence onto the full sub-
         category Inj K ⊗ ⊆ K ⊗ spanned by K-injectives.
     (3) The localization functor loc : K ⊗ → D ⊗ restricts to an equivalence of
                               ∼
         isofibrations Inj K ⊗ → D ⊗ .
Proof. (1) Over any object (I, µ) the fiber of localization reproduces the localization
functor
                                  locI : K I → D I ,
by Proposition 12.1, and each fiber locI admits a fully faithful right adjoint RI :
D I → K I by Corollary 14.11. Hence loc admits a right adjoint R : D ⊗ → K ⊗
over f rDisk⊗ , by Proposition 14.5.
   (2) & (3) By Proposition 14.4 the fiber of R over any (I, µ) recovers the right ad-
joint RI to the localization functor locI , and we know that this functor has image in
the full subcategory Inj K I by Corollary 14.11 (3). Furthermore, again after reduc-
ing to the fibers, the same result tells us that the unit and counit transformations
provide natural isomorphisms
                           ∼                               ∼
                        x → R ◦ loc(x) and loc ◦ R(y) → y
whenever x is in Inj K ⊗ and y is in D ⊗ . However, the unit and counits for the
fibers RI and locI are just obtained from the unit and counit transformations for R
and loc. In particular, these transformations realize R and loc|Inj K ⊗ as mutually
inverse equivalences over f rDisk⊗ .                                              □
Corollary 14.14. Suppose we are in the situation of Proposition 14.13. The struc-
ture map K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ restricts to a cocartesian fibration Inj K ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
Furthermore, the right adjoint R : D ⊗ → K ⊗ to localization is a fully faithful sym-
metric monoidal functor which restricts to an equivalence of cocartesian fibration
D ⊗ → Inj K ⊗ .
Proof. The first and third claims follow by [58, 028A], and the second claim follows,
subsequently, from the fact that the right adjoint factors as a composite
                                     ∼
                                D ⊗ → Inj K ⊗ ,→ K ⊗
of an equivalence of cocartesian fibrations composed with an inclusion of symmetric
monoidal ∞-categories.                                                           □
Remark 14.15. Since K-injective complexes are stable under tensoring, in the
complete setting, the only thing that is stopping the inclusion Inj K ⊗ → K ⊗ from
being a map of cocartesian fibrations over f rDisk⊗ is the unit. Specifically, the
unit in the category of K-injectives is provided by an injective resolution I of the
unit in K ± , so that the inclusion does not preserve unit object. See Lemma 15.6
below.

14.4. Uniqueness of Kan extension over a discrete base. We consider the
following particular expression of the universal property for Kan extensions.
Proposition 14.17. Consider functors F : C → E and l : C → C ′ . For any left
Kan extension F ′ : C ′ → E , the structural transformation α : F → F ′ l realizes F ′
as an initial object in the relative undercategory
                  Fun(C ′ , E )F/ = Fun(C ′ , E ) ×Fun(C ,E ) Fun(C , E )F/ .
Proof. For any choice of functor G : C ′ → E , the usual universal property for Kan
extensions tells us that the sequence
                                 l∗                                          −◦α
           HFun(C ′ ,E ) (F ′ , G) → HFun(C ,E ) (F ′ l, Gl) → HFun(C ,E ) (F, Gl)
is a homotopy equivalence [58, 0309]. (Here the H⋆ are arbitrary choices of Hom
functors [58, 03MR].) When we choose G = F ′ , this functor sends the identity idF
to α. We therefore have a natural isomorphism
                                                       ∼
                         HFun(C ′ ,E ) (F ′ , −) → HFun(C ,E ) (F, −l)
of functors from Fun(C ′ , E ) to K an and subsequent equivalence between the cor-
responding cocartesian fibrations

    Fun(C ′ , E )F ′ /
                                   ∼                          / Fun(C ′ , E ) ×Fun(C ,E ) Fun(C , E )F/


                              '            t
                             Fun(C ′ , E )

[58, 02GT, 01QC] which sends the object idF ′ to (F ′ , α). Since idF ′ is initial in
Fun(C ′ , E )F ′ / [58, 02J2], α is initial in the given fiber product.            □

   This universality statement directly implies uniqueness of Kan extensions over a
discrete base.
Proposition 14.18. Let Z be a discrete category, and consider a partial diagram

                                                                      7/ E
                                                      F
                                      C                                                                   (43)
                                              l   '
                                                      C′
                                          q           q′          p

                                                        
                                                       Z
of maps between cocartesian fibrations over Z. Suppose also that one of the following
conditions hold:
       i) For each x′ in C ′ the relative overcategory C/x′ is nonempty.
      ii) E has no initial object.
     iii) E admits an initial object o for which AutZ (p(o)) = 1.
If a left Kan extension of F over Z exists, then this extension is uniquely determined
up to a contractible spaces of choices. In particular, the full subcategory
              LK an(F ; Z) ⊆ FunZ (C ′ , E ) ×FunZ (C ,E ) FunZ (C , E )F/
spanned by left Kan extensions over Z is a contractible Kan complex.
90                                           CRIS NEGRON


Proof. Consider two ∞-categories over Z, qi : Bi → Z. Since Z is discrete, the
subcomplex
                         FunZ (B0 , B1 ) ⊆ Fun(B0 , B1 )
is the subcategory spanned by all functors over Z and all transformations ζ : F →
F ′ between such functors for which q1 (ζ) : q0 → q0 is the identity. Furthermore,
transformations in FunZ (B0 , B1 ) satisfy the 2-of-3 property in that for any diagram
                                                         ′
                                                    >F
                                            ζ0                  ζ1

                                                                      !
                                       F                             / F ′′
                                                     ζ2

in which two of the ζj are in FunZ (B0 , B1 ), the third transformation is also in
FunZ (B0 , B1 ).
   Considering now the situation (43). If we have two left Kan extensions over Z,
say F0 , F1 : C ′ → E with transformations αi : F → Fi l, then the universal property
of Kan extensions promises the existence of a natural isomorphism ζ : F0 → F1
which completes a diagram

                                                   > F0 l
                                           α0                   ζl

                                                                       !
                                   F                                 / F1 l.
                                                     α1

Since the αi are transformations over Z we conclude that ζl is a transformation
over Z as well, and hence that ζ itself is a transformation over Z.
     To see this clearly, we consider an object x′ in C ′ and the corresponding map
p(ζx′ ) : q ′ (x′ ) → q ′ (x′ ). If x′ admits a map t : l(x) → x′ from some x in C then, via
cocartesianness of q and q ′ and the fact that l is a map of cocartesian fibrations,
we can assume that x and t lie in a single fiber over Z, i.e. that q(x) = q ′ (x′ ) and
q ′ (t) = idq′ (x′ ) . In this case, we have p(ζl(x) ) = idq(x) and the diagram
                                                 p(ζl(x) )=id
                               q ′ (l(x))                            / q ′ (l(x))

                          q ′ (t)=id                                          q ′ (t)=id
                                                                            
                                q ′ (x′ )                             / q ′ (x′ )
                                                   p(ζx′ )

in Z implies p(ζx′ ) = id.
   On the other hand, if x′ admits no maps from an object of the form l(x) then
F0 (x) and F1 (x) are both colimits over the empty diagram C/x → E , and are hence
initial objects in E which lie in the same fiber over Z. In this case assumption (iii)
must hold, which forces p(ζx′ ) = idq′ (x) .
   Let us take now
                    ΠZ = FunZ (C ′ , E ) ×FunZ (C ,E ) FunZ (C , E )F/
and
                     Π = Fun(C ′ , E ) ×Fun(C ,E ) Fun(C , E )F/ .
We note that ΠZ includes into Π as the subcategory spanned by functors and
transformations over Z. The above arguments tell us that any map ζ : {F0 , α0 } →
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                          91


{F1 , α1 } between left Kan extensions over Z in Π is a map in ΠZ . So we see that
the full subcategory
                                   LK an(F ; Z) ⊆ Π
spanned by left Kan extensions over Z, all of which are initial in Π by Proposition
14.17, already lies in ΠZ . Since the full subcategory spanned by initial objects in any
∞-category is contractible–provided it’s not empty–we conclude that LK an(F ; Z)
is a contractible full subcategory in ΠZ .                                             □
Remark 14.19. Naı̈ve versions of Proposition 14.18 do not hold when the base
Z is allowed to be an ∞-category. Also, one can check in examples that some
hypotheses are needed in order to establish uniqueness for relative Kan extensions.
   As an example we can consider the case of a discrete operad. When Z is an
∞-operad and p : E → Z is a map of ∞-operads, then one of the conditions (ii)
or (iii) must hold. In particular, if E has an initial object that object lies in the
contractible fiber E∅ over the empty set in Fin∗ . Such an object maps to an object
in Z∅ , and contractibility of this discrete category implies that all automorphism
groups of objects in Z∅ are trivial.
Corollary 14.20. Suppose that we have a partial diagram of cocartesian fibrations
as in (43), with Z discrete. Suppose also that Z is an ∞-operad and that p : E → Z
is a map of ∞-operads. If a Kan extension F ′ : C ′ → E over Z exists, then this
extension is unique up to a contractible space of choices.
   Our main example of interest here is Z = Fin∗ , though Corollary 14.20 applies
to other operads as well, including those that control associative monoidal ∞-
categories and module ∞-categories over (symmetric) monoidal ∞-categories.
   Considering the case where the functor F : C → E is the identity on C , in the
statement of Proposition 14.18, we obtain the following.
Corollary 14.21. If a map of cocartesian fibrations over a discrete base Z admits
a relative right adjoint over Z, then this adjoint is unique up to a contractible space
of choices.
Proof. Given such a relative right adjoint G to l we only note that condition (i)
holds, as we always have the object {G(x′ ), εx′ : lG(x′ ) → x′ } in C/x′ .    □
Definition 14.22. Given a situation as in the statement of Proposition 14.18, we
denote the relative left Kan extension of F : C → E along l : C → C ′ , if it exists,
by Lanl (F ; Z) : C ′ → E .
   When no confusion will arise we drop the subscript l, and write simply Lan(F ; Z)
for Lanl (F ; Z).

               15. Deriving LRT at the ∞-categorical level
  For a finite modular tensor category A♡ with derived ∞-category D = D(A♡ ),
we construct a topological field theory
                                      D → V ectpro-f in

localization functor Bordnc        nc

Appendix C to calculate the state spaces for LD via maps in D.

bordisms with rigid labels defines a field theory which is valued in V ect, rather

original abelian theory from De Renzi et al. [22],

The theory LDf in can therefore be viewed as a derivation of the original abelian
Lyubashenko theory from [22], or conversely, we can view the abelian theory as a
homological truncation of a fundamentally derived theory LDf in .

Proof. For any map f : (I, µ) → (J , ν) in f rDisk⊗
                                                  surj , Lemma 6.12 tells us that
the corresponding transport functor
                                             ⊗            ⊗
                                  f! : Inj K(I,µ) → Inj K(J ,ν)

is expressible as a composite of duality functors and product functors. Since the
subcategories Inj K ± are identified under duality, by construction, we see that
the subcategory Inj K ⊗ ⊆ K ⊗ is stable under transport if and only if objects in
Inj K ± are stable under the respective product functors on K ± . Since Inj K ⊗ is
full in K ⊗ and stable under isomorphism, stability under transport is equivalent
to the claim that any cocartesian edge α : x → y from an object in Inj K ⊗ also
has y in Inj K ⊗ .
    Considering Inj K − = (Proj K )op , and the duality equivalence with Inj Kpro-f in ,
it suffices to show that a product P ⊗P ′ of two K-projectives remains K-projective.
For this we observe the formula
                      Hom∗A (P ⊗ P ′ , y) = Hom∗A (P, Hom(P ′ , y))
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                          95


via inner-Homs for the action of A = Ch(A♡ ) on itself, and note that the functor
Hom(P ′ , −) sends acyclic complexes to acyclic complexes. For this latter point one
can consider a finite projective generator G in A♡ and note the formula
        Hom∗A (G, Hom(P ′ , −)) = Hom∗A (G ⊗ P ′ , −) = Hom∗A (P ′ , G∗ ⊗ −).
The left hand functor preserves acyclics if and only if Hom(P ′ , −) preserved acyclics,
and the right hand functor preserves acyclics via exactness of the action functor
G∗ ⊗ − and K-projectivity of P ′ .                                                    □

                  16. Derived theories with universal skeins
     We aim to produce, in these final sections, an unmarked field theory
                                           3,2 → h V ect
                              LDf in : Bordnc                                     (48)
from the marked theory LDf in constructed in Section 15. The theory LDf in is ob-
tained in two basic movements: We first localize the bordism category Bordnc      Df in
along a special class of colored merging bordisms, and produce in this way a sym-
                                           −1
metric monoidal ∞-category Bordnc  Df in [ΘDf in ] of bordisms with embedded skein-like
diagrams. We also argue that the derived LRT theory LDf in sends all such colored
merging bordisms to isomorphisms in V ectpro-f in , and therefore induces a symmet-
ric monoidal functor from this localization. We clarify these points in the present
section.
   Subsequently, in Section 17 we establish an identification between the homotopy
category of localized, trivially marked bordisms h Bordnc        −1
                                                             ∗ [Θ∗ ] and the unmarked
              nc
category Bord3,2 , thus yielding the promised theory (48) after restricting along a
unit map Bordnc            nc
               ∗ → BordDf in .

16.1. Special merging bordisms. For any surface Σ with markings ζ : D × I →
Σ, and index i in I, we have the corresponding surface Σiζ obtained by deleting all
but the i-th marking disk. We also consider the apparent inclusion M Σiζ : Σiζ → Σζ
given by the 3-manifold Σ×[0, 1] with the embedded cylinder D ×[0, 1] → Σ×[0, 1],
(z, t) 7→ (ζ(z, i), t). The reader might recall at this point the notion of a merging
bordism from Section 10.2.
Definition 16.1. For a connected marked surface ζ : D×I → Σ and positive index
i in I, a merging bordism Mf : Σζ → Σiζ in Bordnc   ∗ is called special if the identity
on Σiζ is recovered as a composite idΣiζ = Mf ◦ M Σiζ . For a general marked surface
Σζ , a special merging bordism Mf : Σζ → Ση is a bordism whose components are
each special merging bordisms, or identities.
   For a f rDisk-category q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ with corresponding cocartesian fi-
bration q ′ : Bordnc          nc
                    E → Bord∗ , a special merging bordism Mα : Σx → Σy in BordE
                                                                                     nc
                                                                  nc
is a q ′ -cocartesian edge over a special merging bordism in Bord∗ .
Lemma 16.2. Let E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be a f rDisk-category. Consider any object
Σx in BordncE and positive index i for the underling ∗-marked surface top(x) :
D × I → Σ. Let Σc ⊆ Σ be the component of Σ which contains the image of this
i-th marking disk. There is a special merging bordism Mα : Σx → Σy in which the
              TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                        99


outgoing surface Σy is endowed with the single marking top(x)i : D → Σc on the
specified component Σc of Σ.
Proof. Since the forgetful functor Bordnc                nc
                                             E → Bord∗ is a cocartesian fibration it
suffices to prove the result in the ∗-marked bordism category. We can assume
further that the surface Σ is connected. Consider Σζ in Bordnc          ∗ and a positive
index i. We take an embedding from an auxiliary disk τ : D → Σ which contain
all of the markings ζ : D × I → Σ in its image and view ζ as a map into this new
disk ζ : D × I → D, by intersecting with the image of τ .
   Let N be large and consider any smooth function c : [0, 1] → R≥0 which is
constant around 0 and 1, is weakly decreasing, and has values c(0) = 1 and c(1) =
N −1 . We can take a smooth homotopy h : [0, 1] × D × I → D which satisfies the
following:
     (a) ht : D × I → D is a smooth embedding at all t.
     (a) h0 = ζ and h(t, z, i) = ζ(c(t) · z, i), where i is the specified index.
     (b) There exists ϵ > 0 for which h(t, z, j) = h(t′ , z, j) whenever t, t′ < ϵ or
         t, t′ > 1 − ϵ.
     (c) h1 : D × I → D has image in ζ(Di ).
The corresponding bordism Mf : Σζ → Σiζ with M = Σ × [0, 1] and cylinders
                f : D × [0, 1] × I → Σ × [0, 1], f (z, t, i) := (h(t, z, i), t)
is a special merging bordism. (See also Proposition A.2.)                             □
   We have the following analog of Lemma 10.4.

16.2. Color change bordisms.
Definition 16.4. In Bordnc
                        ∗ a color change bordism Mf : Σζ → Ση is one in which
the following hold:
       • The marking set I ′ for Ση is obtained from the marking set I for Σζ by
         changing some negatively colored indices in I to positive indices.
       • After forgetting the colors, the markings ζ̄, η̄ : D × I → Σ agree.
       • The bordism Mf is just Σ × [0, 1] along with the constant cylinder embed-
         ding
                     f : D × [0, 1] × I → Σ × [0, 1], f (z, t, i) = (ζ(z, i), t).
For E → f rDisk⊗ any f rDisk-monoidal ∞-category, a color change bordism in
       ⊗

Bordnc                                                                     nc
     E is a cocartesian lift Mα : Σx → Σy of a color change bordism in Bord∗ .

Lemma 16.5. Let A♡ be a finite modular tensor category. Any color change
bordism Mα : Σx → Σy in Bordnc
                            D evaluates to an isomorphism
                                                          ∼

                                                                     ∼

            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                       101

                     ∼
isomorphism αi : x∗i → yi when the colors of the i-th markings differ. Via the limit
expressions
               LA (Σx ) = lim LA (Σxλ ) and LA (Σy ) = lim LA (Σyλ )
                          ←−                           ←−
                           λ                              λ
we reduce to the case where x and y are finite.
   We recall, in the finite setting, that the Ch(Vect)-valued TQFT LAf in is obtained
by applying a symmetric map p : Bordnc                  nc

is an isomorphism in Ch(Vect). Consequently, after applying the localization loc :

  We are interested in a class of maps which mixes both special merging bordisms
and color change bordisms in BordncE .

Definition 16.6. A colored merging bordism in Bordnc ∗ is a bordism Mf : Σζ → Ση
which decomposes as a disjoint union of special merging bordisms and color change
bordisms. For any f rDisk-category E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ , a colored merging bordism in
Bordnc
     E is a cocartesian lift Mα : Σx → Σy of a colored merging bordism in Bord∗
                                                                               nc
                         nc        nc
along the fibration BordE → Bord∗ .

16.4. Describing the localization via “coarse skeins”. Let E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗
be an arbitrary f rDisk-category. We note that the forgetful functor Bordnc     E →
Bordnc3,2 to the unlabeled (anomalous)   bordism   category sends all colored merging
bordisms to the identity, and thus localizes to provide a symmetric monoidal functor
           −1
Bordnc                 nc
      E [ΘE ] → Bord3,2 . So the localization is some kind of decorated bordism
category.
   The objects in the localization are still marked surfaces–or at least, they can be
taken to be marked surfaces–but morphisms are now bordisms M : Σ → Σ′ with
a kind of “coarse wiring diagram” which is comprised of cylinders merging and
splitting in M .
                                        mf (x)            mg (y)




                           w            mh (z)

The interfaces of these cylinders are labeled by morphisms from products of objects
in E ± , so that one can imagine them as skein diagrams. Indeed, if one has some
notion of a bordism category SkBordnc  E with “skeins labeled by E ” then we expect
a symmetric monoidal functor Bordnc                nc
                                      E → SkBordE which inverts colored merging
bordisms, and hence expect a canonical map from the localization
                                    −1
                            Bordnc              nc
                                E [ΘE ] → SkBordE .
We leave it to the interested reader to explore the relation between the localization
BordA♡ [Θ−1 A♡
                ] and the skein bordism category for a ribbon category A♡ , in the
      f in   f in
discrete setting.
   From the above perspective, Corollary 16.9 says that the LRT theory for the
derived ∞-category D(A♡ ) can be taken as a theory from a category of bordisms
with “coarse D(A♡ )-labeled skeins” as above (cf. [22, 10, 15, 14]).
16.5. Proving Theorem 16.8.
Lemma 16.10. Given a map of cocartesian fibrations

                                C
                                            q
                                                          /D

                                    p
                                                 ~   p′
                                            T

in which q itself is a cocartesian fibration, and a morphism f : s → t in T , the
transport functor f! : Cs → Ct sends qs -cocartesian edges to qt -cocartesian edges.
Proof. First, for any p-cocartesian edge α : x → y in C over a map f : s → t
in T we have that q(α) : q(x) → q(y) is p′ -cocartesian over f . To compare, we
take a q-cocartesian lift α′ : x → y ′ of q(α), have that α′ is p-cocartesian, and by
uniqueness of p-cocartesian lifts of f we obtain a 2-simplex

                                                  @y

                                       x                            / y′ .

                                                   α

Since any isomorphism in C is q-cocartesian, the 2-of-3 property [58, 01TS] assures
us that α is q-cocartesian. So we see that any p-cocartesian edge in C is also
q-cocartesian.
   Considering now the cocartesian transformations Tf : ∆1 × Cs → C over f .
Recall that Tf evaluates to a p-cocartesian edge in C at each object x in the
fiber Cs , and hence to a p-cocartesian edge as well. It follows that, for each qs -
cocartesian edge β : x → u, which is just a q-cocartesian edge which lives in the
fiber, Tf produces a diagram

                                               (Tf )u

in C in which all map, save for possibly f! (β), are known to be q-cocartesian. We
again reference the 2-of-3 property for cocartesian edges to find that f! (β) is in fact
q-cocartesian.                                                                        □

                   17. Emergent (unmarked) field theories
   We extract from the derived theory LD : Bordnc   D → V ectpro-f in of Section 15–or
really its V ect-valued restriction LDf in –an unmarked field theory LDf in : Bordnc
                                                                                  3,2 →
h V ect. As explained in the preamble to Section 16, producing the theory LDf in
reduces to a somewhat delicate, hands-on analysis of the homotopy truncation
h Bordnc     −1
        ∗ [Θ∗ ].
   In human terms, we are claiming that after marking all surfaces by the unit
and washing out all homotopy relations, the values of the theory LDf in does not
actually depend on any choices of decorations on surfaces or bordisms.
17.1. Unit labeled bordisms. Let q : E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ be a f rDisk-category.
We consider the initial object ∅ in f rDisk⊗ and define the fibration of unit objects
in E ⊗ as the ∞-category
                          
                           the full subcategory in the fiber product
            Units(E ⊗ ) =           E∅⊗ ×Fun({0},E ⊗ ) Fun(∆1 , E ⊗ )
                                   spanned by q-cocartesian edges
                          

equipped with the isofibration
                                        ev        q
                           Units(E )⊗ −→
                                       1
                                         E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ .
Lemma 17.1. The structure map Units(E )⊗ → f rDisk⊗ is a trivial Kan fibration.
Proof. To ease notation take T = f rDisk⊗ . By [58, 01VK], the cocartesian fibra-
tion E ⊗ → T induces a trivial Kan fibration
                      Units(E )⊗ → E∅⊗ ×Fun({0},T ) Fun(∆1 , T ),
and since E∅⊗ is contractible the map E∅⊗ → {∅} over T is a trivial Kan fibration
as well. Hence the map
            E∅⊗ ×Fun({0},T ) Fun(∆1 , T ) → {∅} ×Fun({0},T ) Fun(∆1 , T )
is a trivial Kan fibration. Finally, since ∅ is initial in T , evaluation induces a trivial
Kan fibration
                        ev1 : {∅} ×Fun({0},T ) Fun(∆1 , T ) → T
[58, 06R2, 01KU]. As any composite of trivial Kan fibrations is a trivial Kan fibra-
tion, we obtain the desired result.                                                      □
   By Lemma 17.1 we observe the unique f rDisk-monoidal section f rDisk⊗ →
Units(E )⊗ of the structure map, and compose with the evaluation functor Units(E )⊗ →
E ⊗ to obtain a f rDisk⊗ -monoidal functor
                                unitq : f rDisk⊗ → E ⊗ .
108                                   CRIS NEGRON


We refer to this functor as the unit map.
                                                                        ⊗
  The unit map now determines, by pullback along the outgoing boundary ∂out :

(Bord∗ ) → f rDisk , a functor (Bord∗ ) → (BordE ) which is simultaneously

Remark 17.3. What these unit functors do is fairly intuitive. Namely, any
f rDisk-category E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ admits unique “unit objects” 1t which are the
images of the transport functors 1t : ∗ ∼= E∅⊗ → Et⊗ along each (unique) mor-
                          ⊗
phisms ∅ → t in f rDisk . At the level of the underlying ∞-categories, the map
unitE : Bordnc
             ∗ → BordE labels each marked surface ζ : D × I → Σ by the unit
object 1I , and labels each bordism by the unique cocartesian map between units
1I and 1J .

we restrict along the symmetric monoidal functor

                         LDf in ◦ unitDf in : Bordnc

17.3. Reductions in the homotopy bordism category. Our goal is to prove
that the forgetful functor h Bordnc      −1          nc
                                     ∗ [Θ∗ ] → Bord3,2 is an equivalence. We first es-
tablish a collection of relations for morphisms in the homotopy truncation h Bordnc    −1
                                                                                  ∗ [Θ∗ ].

Lemma 17.5.      (1) Any colored merging bordism Mτ : Σζ → Ση decomposes as
     a composite
                             Mτ = Mτ′ ′ ◦ Mτ′′′′
        where Mτ′ ′ is a special merging bordism and Mτ′′′′ is a color change bordism.
    (2) For any special merging bordism Mτ : Σζ → Ση , there is a bordism Mτ′ ′ :
        Ση → Σζ for which [Mτ ]−1 = [Mτ′ ′ ] in h Bordnc      −1
                                                       ∗ [Θ∗ ].
    (3) For any color change bordism Mτ : Σζ → Σζ ′ , and arbitrary Nω : Σζ → Σ′η
        there is a color change bordism Mτ′ ′ : Σ′η′ → Σ′η , and some other bordism
        Nω′ : Σζ ′ → Σ′η′ for which
                            [Nω ] ◦ [Mτ ]−1 = [Mτ′ ′ ]−1 ◦ [Nω′ ]
        in h Bordnc  −1
                 ∗ [Θ∗ ].

Proof. (1) By definition Mτ consists of a disjoint union of special merging bordisms
on some collection of components, and color change bordisms on others. Isolating
the relevant components provides the claimed factorization.
   (2) By definition (Definition 16.1) every special merging bordism admits a right
inverse in Bordnc                     ′                 ′
               ∗ . So there exists Mτ ′ with Mτ ◦ Mτ ′ = idΣη , and in the homotopy
category we get
                      [Mτ ]−1 = [Mτ ]−1 ◦ [Mτ ] ◦ [Mτ′ ′ ] = [Mτ′ ′ ].
  (3) Let Mτ′ ′ : Σζ → Σζ ′ be the unique color change bordism which changes all
negative markings to positive markings. Then we have
                                 Mτ′ ′ ◦ Nω = Nω′′ ◦ Mτ′′′′
where Mτ′′′′ : Σζ → Σζ ′′ changes all negative markings to positive markings and
Nω′′ is obtained from Nω by changing all negatively colored cylinders to positively
colored cylinders. Since Mτ : Σζ → Σζ ′ changes some select negative markings to

        [Nω ] ◦ [Mτ ]−1 = [Mτ′ ′ ]−1 ◦ [Mτ′ ′ ] ◦ [Nω ] ◦ [Mτ ]−1 = [Mτ′ ′ ]−1 ◦ [Nω′ ]
in the homotopy category.                                                                  □

      We also record a basic fact about localization.
             TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                       111


Lemma 17.7. For any ∞-category C and class W in C [1] which contains all
identity maps, C [W −1 ] is equivalent to its full subcategory which is spanned by all
objects which are in the image of the the localization functor loc : C → C [W −1 ].
Furthermore, any morphism ξ : loc(x) → loc(y) in the localization is a composite
                                ξ = α1 w1−1 α2 w2−1 · · · αr wr−1                  (56)
of morphisms αi in C and wi in W .
Proof. Consider C ′ ⊆ C [W −1 ] the subcategory spanned by those objects in the
image of the localization functor, and all morphisms which are expressible as a
composite as in (56). It suffices to show that the map on homotopy categories
h C ′ → h C [W −1 ] is an equivalence.
   By the universal property of localization, the identity on C [W −1 ] factors through
the inclusion C ′ → C [W −1 ]. It follows that the inclusion is essentially surjective
and that the injection
                         π0 MapsC ′ (x, y) → π0 MapsC [W −1 ] (x, y)
is bijective. Hence the inclusion h C ′ → h C [W −1 ] is fully faithful as well.     □

17.6. Mapping class group actions in dimension 2. If we consider any nonempty
surface Σ (with chosen Lagrangian), we have the non-anomalous bordism category
Bord3,2 in which the oriented diffeomorphisms of the surface generate a subgroup
in the automorphism group

                            MCG(Σ) ⊆ AutBord (Σ),

by way of mapping cylinders. We take the pullback under the forgetful functor
from Bordnc
         3,2 to obtain a subgroup of anomalous automorphisms

                            MCG(Σ)           / AutBord (Σ)


                                                  
                            MCG(Σ)          / AutBord (Σ).

             ˜
The group MCG(Σ)    is a central extension of MCG(Σ) by Z which we call the
projective mapping class group. The following is an immediate consequence of
Theorem 17.10.
Corollary 17.13. For each connected genus g surface Σ, the derived linear maps
RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , 1) carry the natural structure of a representation for the projective
mapping class group,
                      ˜       → AutD(Vect) RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , 1) .
                                                                
                 ψ : MCG(Σ)
   One can compute these mapping class group actions directly by taking resolu-
tions of the unit. The process is exactly as outlined in Section 19.5 below, where
we discuss similar calculations for 3-manifold invariants.
Remark 17.14. As relayed in the introduction, mapping class group actions on
cohomology were already observed in works of Lentner, Mierach, Schweigert, and
Sommerhäuser [51], and Schweigert and Woike [72]. We have not checked, but
we fully expect that our mapping class group actions agree with those of [51, 72].
Let us also note that the mapping class group actions from [72] occur at the level
of the ∞-category V ect, and so provide an advancement of the particular actions
obtained in Corollary 17.13. This V ect-level action also suggests the existence of a
lifted theory as in Conjecture 17.12.

                                 18. Conjectures
   This paper has focused exclusively on formalizations of TQFTs for derived cate-
gories of modular tensor (1-)categories. However, we are fundamentally interested
in a space of homotopically flavored TQFTs for “modular ∞-categories”. We ap-
proach this general topic through a series of conjectures.
   In addition to our interest in homotopical Reshetikhin-Turaev type theories, we
have a special investment in couplings of topological field theories to moduli of local
systems. This kind of coupling is well-established in the physics literature, and we
discuss the topic in some detail in Sections 18.4 and 18.5 below.
18.1. 3-d TQFTs from modular ∞-categories.
Conjecture 18.1 (cf. Conjecture 17.12). For any modular ∞-category A , appro-
priately defined, there is an associated 3-dimensional TQFT
                                      A → V ectpro-f in
                             LA : Bordnc
whose state spaces, for any connected genus g surface Σ, admit a natural identifica-
               ∼
tion LA (Σx ) → MapsA (C ⊗g , mf x). Furthermore, this TQFT induces an unmarked
theory after labeling by the unit
                                           3,2 → V ect.
                              LAf in : Bordnc
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                      115


   There are two main issues here. The first is in the definition of a modular ∞-
category. Again, we might say succinctly that such a category is a 2-dimensional
TQFT which satisfies certain non-degeneracy conditions [62, 73]. Examples should
include derived categories of finite modular tensor (1-)categories, and derived cat-
egories of sheaves on compact symplectic varieties, possibly quantized [68, Section
9.2].
Remark 18.2. Though the ∞-category of sheaves D(X) on a projective symplectic
variety X has a strong t-structure, the R-matrix for the quantization [68] should
not respect the t-structure. So we do not think of D(X) as admitting a t-structure
when considered as a modular ∞-category.
  In the derived setting, we propose specifically that there is some way to construct
our theory LD for D = D(A♡ ) in a manner which operates directly at the level of
∞-categories. As a related point, we conjecture that the derived theories LD are
natural in functors between ∞-categories.
Conjecture 18.3. Consider finite modular tensor categories A♡ and B ♡ with re-
spective derived ∞-categories D = D(A♡ ) and D ′ = D(B ♡ ). Any map of balanced
monoidal ∞-categories F : D → D ′ , i.e. f r E2 -monoidal ∞-categories, induces a
symmetric monoidal transformation
                             LF : LD → LD ′ ◦ Bordnc
                                                  F

which, at the level of state spaces, recovers the natural maps
                                                      ⊗g
                   MapsD C ⊗g , mf x → MapsD ′ C ′ , mf F (x)
                                                              

induced by the functor F and a natural map between canonical algebras C ′ → F (C).
In particular, the field theory LD is a derived invariant.
   For a functor F which is not t-exact, establishing such naturality would pre-
sumably require an understanding of the theory LD directly at the homotopical
level.
Remark 18.4. We expect such non-t-exact functors to arise from Kazhdan-Lusztig
type equivalences at bad levels. See for example [61, Theorem 7.13].
18.2. Extension down to S 1 . The TQFT LD is paired with a “conjugate” theory
          D → V ect whose state spaces are given by the continuous duals

                       L′D (Σx ) → MapsD (C ⊗g ⊗ mf (x), 1)∗ ,

and labels come from a f rDisk-category associated to the continuous duality −∗ :
               op ∼
D(A♡ pro-f in )   → D(A♡ ). (Compare with the original theory of [22], [50], and [20,
proof of Theorem 7.11].) Similarly, supposing that the theories LA from Conjecture
18.1 actually exist, we have the conjugate theory L′A .
Conjecture 18.5. Let A be a modular ∞-category. The conjugate theory L′A :
    3,2 → V ect extends down to a symmetric monoidal functor of (∞, 2)-categories
Bordnc

                                         3,2,1 → Prk
                               L′A : Bordnc
which takes values in the (∞, 2)-category of presentable V ect-module categories, and
for which L′A (S 1 ) = A .
116                                   CRIS NEGRON


   In the case of a derived ∞-category A = D(A♡ ), one can sketch the construction
of “most of” an extension for L′D –at least in principle–by following work of De
Renzi in the abelian setting [21] and applying various completion processes [56,
Proposition 5.5.8.22] [57, Corollary 1.4.4.5]. (See also [4, 50].) We omit further
details, for now.
   In the case of a derived ∞-category D(A♡ ), an obvious sub-conjecture here is
that D(A♡ ) appears as the circle value in a 2-dimensional TQFT.
Conjecture 18.6. Any finite modular ∞-category D(A♡ ) appears, along with its
E2 -structure, as the circle value in a 2-dimensional field theory
                                  L̄′D : Bord2,1 → Prk .
      We note that Conjecture 18.6 is not self-evident, though it may be accessible.
Remark 18.7. If we decide to conceptualize modular ∞-categories explicitly as
non-degenerate 2-dimensional field theories, then Conjectures 18.1 and 18.5 to-
gether assert that an extension up from dimension 2 to non-compact manifolds in
dimension 3 occurs if and only if the given 2-dimensional field theory satisfies a
finite number of checkable properties, and in particular that no additional struc-
tures are required. From the perspective of the cobordism hypothesis, say, such an
assertion seems immanently reasonable.
18.3. LRT bounding a 4-dimensional theory? In the semisimple setting we
have the 4-dimensional Crane-Yetter theory which is associated, modulo some de-
tails, to a given modular fusion category A♡ . This theory is invertible, and so
supports anomalous boundary theories [44]. It is shown in work of Haı̈oun [39]–
following proposals of Walker, Freed and Teleman [77, 78, 29]–that one can ob-
tain the (semisimple) Reshetikhin-Turaev theory for a modular fusion category A♡
as a boundary theory to 4-dimensional Crane-Yetter. One similarly obtains the
non-semisimple abelian theory LA♡ as a boundary to so-called non-semisimple
                                     f in
Crane-Yetter [7, 39].
    In the derived setting, considering D = D(A♡ ) for modular A♡ , the situation is
rather unclear. We expect the 4-dimensional theory obtained from D to be quite
a wild object, and in particular non-invertible. Specifically, the value on S 2 is
presumably identified with the E3 -center for D, which should be exceedingly non-
trivial in general. For example, in the case of a quantum group A♡ = (Repq G)small ,
all expectations are that the E3 -center should be an intricate category of sheaves on
the cone Ň of nilpotent elements in the Lie algebra for the Langlands dual group.
So the non-semisimple (derived) and semisimple settings seem quite dissimilar,
and one should think about whether the semisimple boundary intuition is actually
applicable when one works homotopically.
Question 18.8. Is there an extended 4-dimensional theory TD which recovers LD ,
or L′D , as a 3-dimensional boundary?
   As a slightly different, but maybe more fundamental set of questions, we can
investigate the 4-dimensional theory for D outright.
Question 18.9. To what extent can we describe the 4-dimensional theory associated
to D = D(A♡ ), where we consider the derived ∞-category as an object in the (∞, 4)-
category of E2 -monoidal ∞-categories and approach the issue–either philosophically
or materially–via the cobordism hypothesis [40, 44, 8, 7].
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                         117


   In particular, does the fully local topological theory for D extend up to dimension
3 (cf. [7, Theorem 1.2])? Is it, furthermore, a non-compact 4-dimensional theory?
Are its values on certain 3-manifolds definitively non-invertible? Is there an explicit
skein theory which describes its values on 3-manifolds (cf. Section 2)? Can we
describe factorization homology for D via a skein theory? Etc.
18.4. Coupling to local systems. In addition to the points discussed above, the
other (from our perspective) tangible issue which one is required to think about is
the coupling of 3-dimensional field theories to local systems. The following conjec-
ture is, as far as we can tell, a “fact” in the physics literature. See for example
[59, 35].
Conjecture 18.10. Let A be a modular ∞-category, and suppose a connected alge-
braic group H acts rationally on A via automorphisms which preserve the modular
structure. Suppose also that a certain extension problem for A has a solution (see
Conjecture 18.11). Then the unmarked TQFT LA : Bordnc       3,2 → V ect deforms nat-
urally along H-local systems. In particular, the state spaces LA (Σ) appear as the
trivial fiber of a naturally occurring dg sheaf LH
                                                 A (Σ) over LocSysH (Σ).

   In the case of a derived ∞-category, the extent to which LA deforms as a fully
functional, mathematically formal, topological field theory is a bit subtle. So, our
main ambition here is to “look at” Conjecture 18.10 through the lense of some
examples, and to extract some more explicit claims.
   Let us consider the case of quantum group representations. Here we can think
about the state spaces from the perspective of 2-dimensional conformal field theory,
via a bulk/boundary correspondence, at which point we conceptualize 2-d CFTs
through the theory of vertex tensor categories.
   From the perspective of the “logarithmic KL conjecture” we anticipate an equiv-
                                                     ∼
alence of modular tensor categories (Repq G)small → Wκ (G)-mod where Wκ (G) is
the logarithmic W -algebra associated to G at a prescribed level [52, 74, 53]. This
equivalence should be Ǧ-equivariant for the action of the Langlands dual group Ǧ
on Wκ (G)-mod induced by the natural action of Ǧ on Wκ (G) by VOA automor-
phisms. These Wκ (G) should furthermore be the associated boundary algebras for
a 3-dimensional physical theory associated to G [17, Section 6.2.2], and it is pro-
posed directly in [35, Section 2.1], for example, that this Ǧ-action is responsible for
the coupling of the topological theory LD to local systems (see also [28]).
   In categorical terms, one expects these kinds of deformed TQFT to be regulated
by an “H-crossed braided tensor ∞-category” A˜H which extends A , as in Turaev’s
HTQFTs [76].
Conjecture 18.11. Given an H-crossed braided extension A˜H of a modular ten-
sor ∞-category A , the associated topological field theory LA from Conjecture 18.1
deforms along local systems to produce a mathematically formal field theory LH  A
which is coupled to local systems.
   Returning to our particular logarithmic W -algebra, the Ǧ-extension of the cat-
egory of Wκ (G)-modules
                           Wκ (G)-mod → Wκ (G)-modtw
                                                  Ǧ

should be provided by the category of Ǧ-twists modules for W(G). Working directly
through the representation theory of the small quantum group, rather than through
118                                 CRIS NEGRON


a Kazhdan-Lusztig equivalence, we believe that the analogous Ǧ-crossed extension
                              (Repq Ǧ)small → DKq (G)
is realized by a variant of the De Concini-Kac module category. In this modified
De Concini-Kac category the Frobenius center in UqDK (g) is replaced by functons
on the Langlands–rather than Frobenius–dual group (cf. [17, 55]). We place special
emphasis on this case, as we expect the category DKq (G) to be of interest in both
quantum algebra and geometric representation theory.
Remark 18.12. If we are lucky, the desired category DKq (G) can be constructed
by applying some act of tensor categorical hoodoo to the recent “corrected” De
Concini-Kac quantum enveloping algebra of Losev, Tsymbaliuk, and Vu [55]. (Com-
pare with the corrected versions of small quantum groups at even order parameters
[18, 63, 19, 64].) Already they have produced stability for the De Concini-Kac alge-
bra across all orders in the quantum parameter, and their Ǧ-integrable subalgebra
Uqf in admits a central embedding O(Ǧ) → Uqf in from the algebra of functions on
the dual group. Hence the category of Uqf in -modules is naturally a sheaf over Ǧ.
What we are missing is the appropriate crossed tensor structure, and the relation
to small quantum groups.
Remark 18.13. The LocSysǦ -coupled theory for derived quantum group repre-
sentations lies, from a certain physics perspective, on the spectral side of a physical
Langlands duality. The automorphic side of this duality is a corresponding theory
which couples to BunG . See [46, 34, 35].
Remark 18.14. We expect the theory from Conjecture 18.10 to accept certain
types of markings, though the inclusion of such markings complicates the story.
18.5. What is a topological theory coupled to local systems, really? While
we cannot make a definitive statement in this regard, let us say a few words towards
the precise nature of the theory LH
                                  A from Conjecture 18.11.

  To come back to the real world, we return the quantum group case A♡ =
(Repq G)small , D = D(A♡ ). If we restrict our attention only to the torus Ť ⊆ Ǧ
we obtain a theory with abelian connections

                                              3,2 → V ect.
                              LŤD : Ť - Bordnc
By all indications, this Ť -coupled theory is the derived (and sheafified) variant of
the CGP theory for the unrolled quantum group [6, 24]
   We note also that deformed theories over local systems were produced in 4-
dimensions for non-semisimple (non-derived) Crane-Yetter theories in work of Kin-
near [47]. Here, for the quantum group, one considers the 5-dimensional theory
associated to Rep Ǧ and takes Repq G as an invertible map in the 5-category of
E3 -monoidal 1-categories. This situation enriches the 4-dimensional theory for the
fiber Vect ⊗Rep Ǧ Repq G = (Repq G)small in sheaves over local systems.
   It is possible that an analogous approach can be taken in dimension 3 as well,
though we don’t currently see a clear line of argumentation in this direction.

                    19. Post-credit scenes in 3 dimension
   From the author’s perspective at least, this paper is done. We include here some
post-credit materials, where we discuss sporadic outputs of our theory in dimension
3.
   At the most immediate level, we obtain diffeomorphism invariants of 3-manifolds.
However, as we explain below, these 3-manifold invariants collapse to invariants
which are already calculable at the abelian level. In order to see higher information
in dimension 3, one can performing higher genus operation to a closed 3-manifold.
For one example, we explain how LDf in can be used to produce framed knot in-
variants by excising tori from a 3-sphere.

19.1. Invariants from bored 3-manifold. We fix a finite modular tensor cate-
gory A♡ with finite derived category Df in = D(A♡f in ). We consider the unmarked
TQFT
                           LDf in : Bordnc
                                        3,2 → D(Vect)
from Theorem 17.10, and fix a bordism Σ : ∅ → Σ in Bord3,2 for a surface Σ. So,
this is just a chosen “filling” of Σ. Let Σ̄ : Σ̄ → ∅ denote the orientation reversal.
   For any closed 3-manifold with a pair of oriented embeddings

                                       Σ                     Σ̄                  (58)
                          M̌ =
                                                $       z
                                                    M
we remove the interiors, and adopt signature defect 0, to obtain a non-anomalous
bordism Σ → Σ. By an abuse of notation we denote this bordism by M̌ : Σ → Σ
as well.
   We note that the bordism M̌ : Σ → Σ associated to such a configuration (58) only
depends on the isotopy class of the embedding Σ⨿ Σ̄ → M , up to a diffeomorphism.
Hence this bordism, considered as a map in Bordnc  3,2 , is stable under isotopies for
the input data (58). This follows by the isotopy extension theorem [67, 54]. So, for
example, when M is connected and Σ = S 2 the bordism M̌ is in fact independent
of the choice of embeddings completely. We record this point.

19.2. Power series invariants and LDf in (S 2 )-linearity. By basic topological
considerations we understand that the sphere value LDf in (S 2 ) is a commutative
algebra and that each state space LDf in (Σ) for a connected surface is a module
over LDf in (S 2 ) in D(Vect). The multiplication on the sphere is obtained by boring
out the interiors of two small balls in a larger ball, and the action on LDf in (Σ) is
obtained by boring out the interior of a ball within the bordism Σ×[0, 1]. Under this
natural module structure, each bordism between connected surfaces N : Σ → Σ′
evaluates to a map of LDf in (S 2 )-modules under the field theory LDf in .
   Though we omit the details, we claim that under the identifications
         LD (S 2 ) ∼
             f in      = RHomA♡ (1, 1) and LD (Σ) ∼ f in = RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , 1)
                                             2
the natural algebra structure on LDf in (S ) agrees with the standard Yoneda prod-
uct on extensions, and the module structure agrees with the natural tensor action
of RHomA♡ (1, 1) on RHomA♡ (C ⊗g , 1).
   Now, it is a well-established conjecture that, for any finite tensor category, the
cohomology Ext∗A♡ (x, 1) of an arbitrary rigid object x is a finite module over
Ext∗A♡ (1, 1) [31, 27]. So, in any setting in which one can hope to compute anything,
each morphism
                                                      ∼
                     H ∗ LDf in (M̌ ) : H ∗ LDf in (Σ) → H ∗ LDf in (Σ)
for connected Σ is completely determined by its values in low-degree. As a conse-
quence, we expect the low degree coefficients
                    Inv(A♡ |M̌ ) = c0 (X) + c1 (X)t + c2 (X)t2 + · · ·
to carry the most important information.

19.4. Framed knot invariants in genus 1. Take a knot in 3-space ν : S 1 → R3 ,
which we may consider as a knot in S 3 which avoids the point ∞. We suppose the
differential ∂ν is non-vanishing, and assume ν comes equipped with an orthonormal
framing in which the first vector is the differential ∂ν/∂t. From this framing we
deduce an embedding from the solid torus ν ′ : T2 → R3 ⊆ S 3 where the center
of T2 traverses ν. We take another embedding µ : T̄2 → R3 ⊆ S 3 which wraps
around the exterior of ν ′ at an arbitrary time t ∈ S 1 . In this way we obtain an
assignment ν 7→ Šν3 from a framed knot S 2 → R3 to a bordism Š 3 : T 2 → T 2 .
Proposition 19.6. For any finite modular tensor category A♡ , the assignment

  Let us explain, finally, how one can calculate these invariants in practice.
19.5. Calculating Inv via resolutions. Though we won’t calculate any of the
above invariants explicitly, let us at least explain how one can calculate them. The
process is rather simple. Let us fix modular A♡ and closed connected M with
embeddings Σ → M ← Σ̄.
   Firt, take your favorite injective resolution 1 → Q of the unit in A♡ and endow
the surface Σ with a positive disk labeled by Q. Then produce a bordism
                                   M̌tube : ΣQ → ΣQ
in Bordnc
       A by connecting the markings via a single tube which traverses the interior
of M̌ arbitrarily. We then evaluate under discrete LRT to get an endomorphism of
cochains
                  LA (M̌tube ) : Hom∗A♡ (C ⊗g , Q) → Hom∗A♡ (C ⊗g , Q).
This endomorphism can be calculated, degree-by-degree, via the original abelian
LRT theory from [22]. Take cohomology to get an endomorphism
                H ∗ LA (M̌tube ) : Ext∗A♡ (C ⊗g , 1) → Ext∗A♡ (C ⊗g , 1).

gate. Hence the coefficient of tn in Inv(A♡ |M̌ ) is calculated as the determinant of

                             Appendix A. About f rE2
   We discuss equivalences between maps in f rE2 , while paying special attention
to issues of smoothness. We then prove Proposition 7.1, which claims that the
inclusion Linf rE2 → f rE2 is an equivalence, and discuss related issues for bordism
categories. Finally, we provide the proofs of Lemmas 6.9 and 6.12 from the body
of the text. The reader might recall at this point our explicit construction of the
∞-operad f rE2 from Section 5.3.

A.1. The homotopy category h f rE2 . The following results are well-known to
experts. Corollary A.3, in particular, follow by an equivalence between the space of
smooth embeddings Embed(D × I, D) ⊆ Maps(D × I, D) and the space of framed

Proposition A.2. Suppose two maps f0 , f1 : D × If → D × J in f rE2 have the
same image f : I → J in Fin∗ . There is a smooth homotopy h : |∆1 | × D × I →
D × J with h0 = f0 and h1 = f1 , and for which the evaluation ht : D × If → D × J

  1See for example this mathoverflow answer form Najib Idrissi.
126                                  CRIS NEGRON


Proof. It suffices to prove the result when J is a singleton and f : I → {±0} is
active, since we can independently resolve the problem for the preimages D × Ij of
the disks in the codomain one-by-one. Furthermore, after shrinking the images of
the disks, then translating if necessary, we may assume each of the images f0 (Di )
and f1 (Di′ ) are disjoint in D for each i and i′ in If . Then, by isolating each of the
pairs f0 (Di ) ∪ f1 (Di ) in disjoint neighborhoods τi : D ,→ D we reduce to the case
where I is a singleton. This reduces us to the situation considered in Lemma A.1,
where the problem was already resolved.                                               □
Corollary A.3. The forgetful functor f rE2 → Fin∗ induces an equivalence from
                                ∼
the homotopy truncation h f rE2 → Fin∗ .
Proof. Morphisms in the homotopy category are given by the connected com-
ponents in the mapping space Mapsf rE2 (I, J ). We have the forgetful functor
Mapsf rE2 (I, J ) → HomFin∗ (I, J ) which separates this mapping space into a dis-
joint union
                       Mapsf rE2 (I, J ) = ⨿f Mapsf rE2 (I, J )f
over f in HomFin∗ (I, J ). So it suffices to show that each space Mapsf rE2 (I, J )f
is connected. As two maps in this space f0 , f1 : D × If → D × J are connected by
a path if and only if there is a homotopy h : |∆1 | × D × If → D × J with h0 = f0
and h1 = f1 , the result follows by Proposition A.2.                              □
A.2. Linearly arrangements of disks.
Lemma A.4. The inclusion Linf rE2 → f rE2 is an equivalence.
Proof. The inclusion is essentially surjective, as Linf rE2 contains all objects.
Given any pair of finite colored sets I and J , we need only show that the inclusion
of mapping spaces
                       MapsLinf rE2 (I, J ) → Mapsf rE2 (I, J )
is a homotopy equivalence. For this we can consider the corresponding simplicial
category Linf rE2 in f rE2 and show that the inclusion
                        HomLinf rE2 (I, J ) → Homf rE2 (I, J )
is an equivalence (see [58, 02LN]). Since the subspace HomLinf rE2 (I, J ) is full in
Homf rE2 (I, J ) we need only show that the inclusion is surjective on connected
components. However, this is a consequence of Proposition A.2, which shows that
there is only one map in Homf rE2 (I, J ), up to homotopy, over each map of finite
colored sets I → J .                                                               □
  We similarly have the subcategory Linf rDisk⊗ in f rDisk⊗ which fits into a
pullback diagram
                         Linf rDisk⊗      / f rDisk⊗


                                                      
                              Linf rE2            / f rE2 .
Since the disjoint union functor Linf rDisk⊗ → f rE2 is an isofibration, the ex-
istence of such a pullback diagram tells us that the inclusion Linf rDisk⊗ →
f rDisk⊗ is an equivalence.
Lemma A.5. The inclusion Linf rDisk⊗ → f rDisk⊗ is an equivalence.
             TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                             127


A.3. Linear arrangements for bordisms.

Lemma A.6. Fix two smooth oriented embeddings f0 , f1 : D × I → D. There is a
smooth homotopy h : |∆1 | × Cyl ×I → D, and subsequent homotopy

              H : |∆1 | × Cyl ×I → Cyl, H(s, z, t, i) 7→ (h(s, z, t, i), t),

which satisfies the following:
      • There exists ε > 0 for which h(s, z, t, i) = h(s, z, t′ , i) whenever t and t′ are
        simultaneously in the neighborhood [0, ε), or in (1 − ε, 1].
      • h(0, z, t, i) = h(s, z, 0, i) = f0 (z, i) for all s and t.
      • h(1, z, 1, i) = f1 (z, i).

Proof. Take µ : |∆1 | × D × I → D a smooth homotopy which has µ0 = f0 , µ1 = f1 ,
and for which µt is a smooth embedding at all t in |∆1 |. Such a homotopy µ exists
by Proposition A.2. Let b : [0, 1] → R be a smooth, weakly increasing function for
which b(t) is of constant value 0 in a neighborhood of 0 and of constant value 1 in
a neighborhood of 1. Let c : [0, 1] → R be the identity c(s) = s. Take now

              h : |∆1 | × Cyl ×I → D and H : |∆1 | × Cyl ×I → Cyl
                                                                                    
defined by h(s, z, t, i) = µ(c(s)b(t), z, i) and H(s, z, t, i) = µ(c(s)b(t), z, i), t .    □

Corollary A.8. For any f rDisk-category E ⊗ → f rDisk⊗ the projection

                     Linf rDisk⊗ ×f rDisk⊗ Bord⊗       ⊗
                                               E → BordE

is an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories.

Proof. It suffices to show that the underlying map on ∞-categories is an equiva-
lence. Consider the case of the trivial famed pair

                       Linf rDisk ×f rDisk Bord∗ → Bord∗ .                     (61)

The left-hand ∞-category is the subcategory in Bord∗ consisting of all marked
surfaces, and bordisms Mf : Σζ → Σ′η whose outgoing map ∂out (Mf ) lies in
Linf rDisk. Equivalently, the fiber product is the nerve of the simplicial sub-
category LinBord∗ in Bord∗ containing all objects, and whose mapping complexes

                   HomLinBord∗ (Σζ , Ση ) ⊆ HomBord∗ (Σζ , Ση )

are the full subspaces spanned by all bordisms Mf with ∂out (Mf ) in Linf rE2 . The
corresponding inclusions of mapping complexes are clearly fully faithful, and by
(the proof of) Proposition A.7 they are essentially surjective. Hence each inclusion
is a homotopy equivalence, and the corresponding functor on homotopy coherent
nerves is an equivalence [58, 01Q1].
   For a general f rDisk-category, we have a pullback diagram

                     Linf rDisk ×f rDisk BordE                   / BordE


                                                                    
                     Linf rDisk ×f rDisk Bord∗                   / Bord∗

in which the map BordE → Bord∗ is a cocartesian fibration, and hence an isofi-
bration. Since the bottom projection is an equivalence, it follows that the top
projection is an equivalence as well.                                         □

           Appendix B. Framed pairs from balanced categories
   We construct a canonical f rE2 -monoidal ∞-category q : E ⊙ → f rE2 associated
to a map of balanced monoidal categories d : E − → E + . As a preliminary point,
we recall how one constructs an (uncolored) f r E2 -monoidal ∞-category from any
balanced monoidal category. Pulling back along the disjoint union functor, we
obtain a corresponding f rDisk-category E ⊗ = Env(E ⊙ ) → f rDisk⊗ .
B.1. Framed E2 -algebras from balanced categories. We consider the stan-
dard operad f r E2 of (uncolored) disk embeddings. This operad is defined via a
corresponding simplicial operad f rE2 , exactly as in Section 5.3, but without col-
ors. So, objects are finite sets, morphisms f : I → J are oriented disk embeddings
f : D × If → D × J, 2-simplices are generally non-commuting diagrams

                                                    ?J                                  (62)
                                            f                    g

                                                                         
                                   I                                     /K
                                                        h

whose underlying maps of finite pointed sets commute ḡ f¯ = h̄, and which are
equipped with a homotopy σ : |∆1 | × D × Ih̄ → D × K between gf and h. As in
the colored setting, we have the non-full subcategory
                                       Linf r E2 → f r E2
132                                  CRIS NEGRON


spanned by all objects and linear disk arrangements f : D × If¯ → D × J. The same
arguments employed in the colored case tell us that this inclusion is an equivalence.
   From any 2-simplex σ : ∆2 → Linf r E2 as in (62) the underlying homotopy
traces out a framed braid [σ] = (β, n) in Brf r (gf ) which transaltes between the
orderings on the subsets Ik ⊆ I specified by the two maps gf and h. This framed
braid is defined exactly as in Section 7.1. The following is well-known, and can be
obtained from a calculation of the 2-categorical truncation π1 Linf rE2 via framed
braids. See for example [71, Definition 7.1, Propositions 7.4 and 7.6] (cf. [30,
Proposition 6.2.2]).

Proposition B.1. Any balanced monoidal (1−)category B has an associated func-
tor FB : Linf r E2 → Cat with the following values:
      • FB (I) = B I .
      • For f : I → J in Linf r E2 , FB (f ) = mf : B I → B J .
      • For a 2-simplex σ as in (62), with associated framed braid (β, n),
                                                     ∼
                         FB (σ) = ζβ,n : mg mf ∼
                                               = mgf → mh .

                              ζβ,n = β ◦ mgf (θn ) ◦ assoc .

Sketch proof. We have the simplicial operad LinConf f r whose objects are finite
pointed sets, and maps are linear embeddings of disks f : D × If¯ → D × J.

embeddings of points Ij → D for which σ̄r = fr |{0}×Ij at each index r. Composition

is described entirely by is 0, 1 and 2-simplices as in Lemma 3.24.

Cat[d] as prescribed in the statement, and it is a practical matter to check that these

the projection from Linf r E2 to obtain the promised map FB .                          □

Proposition B.4. For any balanced monoidal category B, the cocartesian fibration
B ⊙ → f r E2 from Definition B.3 is a f r E2 -monoidal ∞-category. Furthermore,
for each map f : I → J in Linf r E2 , the transport functor along f recovers the
associated product functor mf : B I → B J .
B.3. f rE2 -monoidal ∞-categories from balanced functors. Given a balanced
monoidal functor d : E − → E + we have the associated fibration p : Ed → ∆1
obtained from the weighted nerve of d. Specifically, Ed is the (1-)category whose
fibers over 0 and 1 are E − and E + respectively, and whose maps from the negative
to positive fiber are
                          HomEd (x, y) = HomE + (d(x), y).
Note that there are no maps from the positive to negative fiber.
   The balanced structure on d endows Ed with the unique structure of a balanced
monoidal category so that the two inclusions E ± → Ed are maps of (non-unital)
balanced monoidal categories, with trivial tensor compatibility, and with the prod-
uct between the fibers given by
  x ⊗ y = d(x) ⊗ y and y ⊗ x = y ⊗ d(x) whenever x is over 0 and y is over 1.
The unit for this category is 1 = 1E − , and the associator between the fibers is
provided by the monoidal structure on d.
Remark B.5. The inclusion E − → Ed is unital while the inclusion from E + is
non-unital. Indeed, the unit for E + is sent to the object 1E + in (Ed ){1} , and this
object admits a distinguished p-cocartesian morphism 1 → 1E + provided by the
                        ∼
unit structure d(1E − ) → 1E + on d.
  We have the associated fibration qd : Ed⊙ → f r E2 from Definition B.3 which
admits two apparent maps of operads
                          (E − )⊙        / E⊙ o        (E + )⊙
                                            d

                                            qd
                                    q−   $  z    q+
                                         f r E2           .
We pull back along the forgetful functor f rE2 → f r E2 to get a cocartesian fibration
f rE2 ×f r E2 Ed⊙ → f rE2 .
Definition B.6. Given a map d : E − → E + between balanced monoidal (1-
)categories and associated fibration qd : Ed⊙ → f r E2 , we take
                              E ⊙ ⊆ f rE2 ×f r E2 Ed⊙
136                                  CRIS NEGRON


the full subcategory whose fibers are the colored exponents EI⊙ = E I ⊆ EdI . We
consider E ⊙ along with the inner fibration q : E ⊙ → f rE2 given by restricting the
cocartesian fibration f rE2 ×f r E2 Ed⊙ → f rE2 to E ⊙ .
   We note that E ⊙ is stable under isomorphism in f rE2 ×f r E2 Ed⊙ , so that the
map q : E ⊙ → f rE2 is in fact an isofibration.
   Recall that each f : I → J in Linf rE2 determines an associated colored product
mf : E I → E J , and the corresponding map f¯ : I → J in Linf r E2 determines an
uncolored product mf¯ : EdI → EdJ . Given such f in Linf rE2 and x in the fiber
EI⊙ = E I , we define the map 1f : x → mf (x) in E ⊙ over f which we recall is
defined by an underlying map 1f : mf¯(x) → mf (x) in EdJ as follows:
      • For each negatively colored index j ∈ J , we have that the j-th components
        agree mf¯(x)j = mf (x)j and we take (1f )j : mf¯(x)j → mf (x)j to be the
        identity (1f )j = idmf (x)j .
      • For each positively colored index j ∈ J whose preimage Ij also contains a
        positive index, we again have mf¯(x)j = mf (x)j and take (1f )j = idmf (x)j .
      • For each positively colored index j ∈ J whose preimage Ij contains no
        positive indices, we have mf (x)j = mf¯(dIj x)j , where dIj applies d at each
        index in Ij ⊆ I, and we let (1f )j : mf¯(x)j → mf (x)j be the map in Ed
                                                        ∼
        given by the natural isomorphism d(mf¯(x)j ) → mf¯(dIj x)j = mf (x) given
        by the monoidal structure on d.
Proposition B.7. For any balanced monoidal functor d : E − → E + , the associated
isofibration q : E ⊙ → f rE2 from Definition B.3 is a cocartesian fibration which
gives E ⊙ the structure of a f rE2 -monoidal ∞-category. Furthermore, for each
f : I → J in Linf rE2 and x in E ⊙ , the map 1f : x → mf (x) is a q-cocartesian
edge over f .
Proof. Call a map f : I → J in f rE2 consistently colored if the preimage Ij of
each positive index j in J is nonempty and contains a positive index i. Since this
property is determined by the underlying morphism of finite pointed colored sets,
we see that such consistency is stable under isomorphisms of maps in f rE2 .
    For consistently colored f : I → J in Linf rE2 we note that the uncolored
product mf¯ : EdI → EdJ for Ed sends E I into E J , and in fact recovers the colored
product mf : E I → E J under restriction. Hence for any x in E I ⊆ EdI the
distinguished cocartesian lift 1f : x → mf (x) in Ed⊙ provided by the identity on
mf (x) is already in E ⊙ . For general consistently colored f ′ : I → J in f rE2
we have mf ′ ∼ = mf for some consistently colored f in Linf rE2 , so that again the
cocartesian lift x → mf ′ (x) in Ed⊙ lies in E ⊙ , by fullness of E ⊙ and stability under
isomorphism.
    Now, by a color change map in f rE2 we mean a map ω : I → I ′ in f rE2 which
maps to idI : I → I ′ = I in f r E2 . We note that every map f ′ in f rE2 is a
composite f ′ = f ωu of consistently colored maps f and u, and a color change map
ω. Here u is just an inclusion D × I 0 → D × I 1 induced by an inclusion of sets
I 0 → I 1 in which all added indices in I 1 are negatively colored. So, via the 2-of-3
property for cocartesian edges [58, 01TS], it suffices to show that each color change
map admits a q-cocartesian lift.
                                                               ′
    For a color change map ω : I → I ′ let dω : E I → E I be the functor which is
the identity at each index i with color(i) = color(ωi), and which applies d at each
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                          137


index i with color(i) = − color(ωi). (This map dω is equal to the colored product
mω over ω.) For x in E I take 1ω : x → dω (x) the map over ω in Ed⊙ which is just
the identity on each factor. We claim that 1ω is q-cocartesian.
