Proposition B.8. Let d : E − → E + be a balanced monoidal functor. For any map
f : I → J in Linf rE2 the associated colored product functor
                                     mf : E I → E J
is the transport functor for q : E ⊙ → f rE2 along f .
Proof. When f is consistently colored, in the sense that Ij contains a positive index
whenever j is positive in J , then the colored product is obtained by restricting the
corresponding uncolored product on Ed . Since the uncolored product is reproduced
via transport along f for Ed⊙ → f r E2 , it follows that the colored product is also
recovered via transport along f . (Here we are using the fact that the map 1f : x →
mf (x) is a cocartesian lift of f in both E ⊙ and Ed⊙ .)
   For a color change map ω : I → I ′ , i.e. a map over the identity in f r E2 , recall
that mω : E I → E J just applies d at all indices where the color changes from
negative to positive. We have the uniquely specified cocartesian transformation
                                   Tω : ∆1 × E I → E ⊙
with Tω |{0} = id, Tω |{1} = mω , Tω (0 < 1, α : x → y) = mω (α) : x → mω (y). Note
that for each x in E I this transformation has Tω |∆1 ×{x} : ∆1 → E ⊙ equal to the
q-cocartesian edge 1ω . Hence, restricting along {1} → ∆1 , we see that mω provides
the transport functor for E ⊙ along ω.
    For general f ′ : I0 → J which is not consistently colored we have a factorization
  ′
f = f ωu for color change ω, f consistently colored, and u induced by an inclusion
I0 → I1 . Here we can take I1 obtained from I0 by adding in those indices in J
which are positively colored but have vanishing preimage, and negating the colors
on these indices. Note that u is consistently colored. Since composites of transport
functors are transport functors, we find that the composite mf mω mu : E I → E J
is a transport functor along f ′ .
    The functor mf mω mu inserts a copy of d(1E − ) at each index j in I, and
the structural isomorphism d(1E − ) ∼    = 1E + induces a natural isomorphism ξ :
            ∼
mf mω mu → mf ′ which composes with the cocartesian transformations to produce
a cocartesian lift
                           ξ ◦ Tf ◦ Tω ◦ Tu : ∆1 × E I → E ⊙
                               f
of the map ∆1 × E I → ∆1 → f rE2 . This transformation realizes mf ′ as another
transport functor along f ′ in E ⊙ .                                         □

   Proposition B.8 tells us, in particular, that transport along the inert projections
ρi : I → {±0} recovers the identity map on the colored exponent ρ! = id : EI⊙ =
EI → EI .
Corollary B.9. For any balanced monoidal functor d : E − → E + , the associated
cocartesian fibration q : E ⊙ → f rE2 gives E ⊙ the structure of a f rE2 -monoidal
∞-category.

             Appendix C. Module categories and inner-Homs
   We cover basics information regarding module categories over symmetric monoidal
∞-categories. As a primary point of interest, we calculate inner-Homs for the action
of V ectpro-f in on Dpro-f in . For this section one does need to have some familiarity
with ∞-operads, and we refer the reader to [57, Sections 2.1.1 and 2.1.2] for the
details.
C.1. ∞-Categories of cocartesian fibrations. For any ∞-category T and co-
cartesian fibrations qi : Ci → T we have the complexes
                   FunT (C0 , C1 ) = {q0 } ×Fun(C0 ,T ) Fun(C0 , C1 )
of functors over T , and consider the full subcategory FunccT (C0 , C1 ) spanned by
those maps which preserve cocartesian edges. Since the map (q1 )∗ : Fun(C0 , C1 ) →
Fun(C0 , T ) is an inner fibration [58, 01BV], the fiber FunT (C0 , C1 ) is an ∞-
                                         T (C0 , C1 ).
category, as is the full subcategory Funcc
   The composition operations on the ambient complexes Fun(⋆, ⋆) restrict to pro-
vide composition operations for Funcc  T , so that we obtain a simplicial category
Cocart(T ) which is enriched in ∞-categories. Applying the associated Kan com-
plex functor on mapping complexes, then applying the homotopy coherent nerve,
we obtain an ∞-category Cocart(T ) = N(Cocart(T )+ ).
   We note that pulling back along any map w : S → T provides a functor
Cocart(T ) → Cocart(S ) between Cat∞ -enriched categories, so that we obtain a
pullback functor
                            w∗ : Cocart(T ) → Cocart(S ).
                                                                                 ⊗
Definition C.1. Given an ∞-operad O ⊗ → Fin∗ , the ∞-category MonO        ∞ is
the full subcategory in Cocart(O ⊗ ) spanned by O-monoidal ∞-categories. In the

C.2. Module categories over symmetric monoidal ∞-categories.
Definition C.2. Let Mod denote the subcategory of Fin∗ (see Section 5.1) con-
sisting of all finite colored sets I and all morphisms f : I → J which satisfy the
following:
     (a) Every positively colored index in the specified subset If ⊆ I is sent to a
         positively colored index in J .
140                                  CRIS NEGRON


      (b) For each positively colored j in J , the preimage Ij = f −1 (j) is nonempty
          and contains precisely one positively colored index.
We consider Mod as a category over Fin∗ via the forgetful functor Mod → Fin∗ .
Remark C.3. Condition (a) in Definition C.2 is redundant, in the sense that it is
already included in the notion of morphisms for Fin∗ .
Lemma C.4. The forgetful functor Mod → Fin∗ gives Mod the structure of an
∞-operad.
Proof. Any inert map ρ̄ : I → J in Fin∗ specifies a bijection from the specified
                  ∼
                  =
subset I ′ = Iρ̄ → J. For any coloring I of I we have the corresponding colored
subset I ′ and coloring J of J induced by the given bijection. We thus obtain the
colored lift ρI : I → J of ρ̄ which is observed directly to be cocartesian over ρ̄. As
the coloring of J is canonically determined by the coloring of I and the map ρ̄ in
Fin∗ , we have the associated transport functor
                             ρ̄! : ModI → ModJ , I 7→ J ,
with cocartesian transformation Tρ̄ : ∆1 × ModI → Mod provided by the ρI .
The remaining requirements from [57, Definition 2.1.1.10]l are now also observed
directly.                                                                     □

   We note that there is a unique section i : Fin∗ → Mod of the forgetful func-
tor which sends each finite pointed set I to I along with its complete negative
coloring. This section is a map of ∞-operads, in the sense that it preserves inert
edges. In particular, any Mod-monoidal ∞-category pulls back along i to produce
a symmetric monoidal ∞-category.
Definition C.5. Let F ⊗ → Fin∗ be a symmetric monoidal ∞-category. A module
category over F is a Mod-monoidal ∞-category M ⊗ → Mod which is equipped
with an equivalence of symmetric monoidal ∞-categories

C.3. Module ∞-categories from discrete module categories. Let S be a
discrete symmetric monoidal category and M be a discrete module category over
S, in the usual 1-categorical sense of the term. (See for example [26, Definition
7.1.1].) We construct the corresponding cocartesian fibration M ⊗ → Mod whose
objects consist of a choice of finite colored set I, a linear ordering on the negatively
                                                                    −1            −1
colored indices in I, and a choice of an object x in M I = S color (−) × M color (+) .
Morphisms α : x → x′ consist of a map f : I → J in Mod and, for each j in J , a
choice of map xIj → x′j in S or M where
                                             O
                                      xIj =      xi .
                                            i∈Ij

Here the product is ordered via the chosen ordering on Ij when j is negative, and
otherwise is taken in the unique ordering which extends the given ordering on the
negative indices and takes the positive index in Ij to be maximal. In this way
any module category in the 1-categorical sense becomes a module category in the
∞-categorical sense.

              dim HomA♡ (x, y) < ∞ whenever x and y are compact.

C.4. Inner-Homs. For symmetric F and an F -module category M ⊗ → Mod
we have the action map actM : F × M → M . Hence, for any Hom-functor
HM : M op × M → K an we have the corresponding sequence

                      F op × M op × M → M op × M → K an

given by composing the action with HM . Via adjunction this specifies a map

      act∨ : M op × M → Fun(F op , K an), (m, m′ ) 7→ {x 7→ HM (x ⊗ m, m′ )}.

Remark C.10. Recall that a Hom functor HM is simply a “bi-functorialization”
of the mapping spaces

                 MapsM (x, x′ ) = {x, x′ } ×Fun(∂∆1 ,M ) Fun(∆1 , M ).

Such bifunctors are obtained by straightening the twisted arrow fibration TW(M ) →
M op × M . See [58, 03K1, 03K6].

  Recall the Yoneda embedding F → Fun(F op , K an), which is an equivalence
onto the full subcategory spanned by representable functors [58, 03NF, 03M5].

Definition C.11. An F -module category M is said to admit inner-Homs if there
is a bifunctor Maps : M op × M → F which completes a diagram

                                       :F
                             Maps                Y on

                                                      &
                     M op × M                   / Fun(F op , K an)
                                            ∨
                                        act

in Cat∞ .

    Clearly inner-Homs exist if and only if each functor act∨ (x, x′ ) = HM (− ⊗ x, x′ )
is representable. Furthermore, in this case the representing functor Maps is uniquely
determined up to a contractible space of choices. For us, existence of inner-Homs
over all of M is a bit too much to ask for. So we consider the following.

Definition C.12. An F -module category M is said to admit restrictive inner-
Homs, relative to a specified full subcategory Mf in in M , if there is a bifunctor
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                            143


Maps : Mfopin × M → F which completes a diagram

                                        :F

                                                           &

                                             f in


in Cat∞ .
  We note that the full subcategory Mf in is not assumed to be an F -module
subcategory, so that the restriction of act∨ is obtained from the sequence
                                        act ×1               H
                   F op × Mfopin × M                M
                                         → M op × M → K an.

Lemma C.15. Let A♡ be a finitely generated linear abelian category and Apro-f in
be the category of pro-finite cochains. The product and action functors,
               Ch(Vect)pro-f in × Ch(Vect)pro-f in → Ch(Vect)pro-f in
and
                     Ch(Vect)pro-f in × Apro-f in → Apro-f in ,
preserve both homotopy equivalences and quasi-isomorphisms, in each factor. Fur-
thermore, the classes of homotopy equivalences and quasi-isomorphisms agree in
Ch(Vect)pro-f in .
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                                   145


Proof. We can proceed abstractly, but let us make things very concrete. We
can identify A♡ with the category Corep(Λ) of comodules over a coalgebra Λ
[75, Theorem 5.1]. For Λ′ the co-opposite coalgebra, we have the equivalence
                    ∼
−∗ : Corep(Λ′ )op → Corep(Λ)pro-f in provided by linear duality, which then ex-
tends to a duality equivalence at the level of cochains
                                             ∼
                                Ch(Λ′ )op → Ch(Λ)pro-f in ,                                   (66)

just as in Sections 7.3 and 7.4. The inverse is provided by the continuous dual.
   This equivalence intertwines the Ch(Vect) action on the left with the Ch(Vect)pro-f in
action on the right, in the sense that we have a diagram

            Ch(Vect)op × Ch(Λ′ )op
                                         ∼       / Ch(Vect)pro-f in × Ch(Λ)pro-f in


                                                                
                   Ch(Λ′ )op
                                         ∼                / Ch(Λ)pro-f in .

   As with any equivalence of abelian categories, duality is additive and exact, and
in particular commutes with cohomology. Hence the equivalence (66) identifies
homotopy equivalence in the source category with homotopy equivalence in the
target category, and similarly preserves quasi-isomorphisms. So, the fact that the
usual action
                            Ch(Vect) × Ch(Λ′ ) → Ch(Λ′ )
preserves homotopy equivalence and quasi-isomorphisms implies that the pro-finite
action also preserves homotopy equivalence and quasi-isomorphisms (see also Propo-
sition 7.10). Considering the case Λ = k, we see that the product on Ch(Vect)pro-f in
similarly preserves these classes of maps, and that homotopy equivalence agrees with
quasi-isomorphisms in Ch(Vect)pro-f in since they agree in Ch(Vect).               □

C.6. Calculating inner-Homs under advantageous localization.
Proposition C.17. Suppose that an F -module ∞-category M admits restrictive
inner-Homs, relative to a full subcategory Mf in ⊆ M . Let W − and W + be classes
of maps in F and M , respectively, which satisfy the hypotheses of Proposition

                                    MapsM (x, −) : M → F
sends maps in W to maps in W − . Then the localized module category M [(W + )−1 ]
                  +

admits restrictive inner-Homs over F [(W − )−1 ], relative to the full subcategory
spanned by the image of Mf in under localization, and the inner-Hom functors
are calculated via localization. More precisely, for each x in Mf in the functors
MapsM [(W + )−1 ] (loc(x), −) is the unique one which completes a diagram

admits a right adjoint. (See [58, 045S].)
    For x in Mf in , the functor − ⊗ x specifies a cocartesian fibration
                                       q : Z → ∆1

with cotransport recovering MapsM (x, −). (The fibration q : Z → ∆1 can be
explicitly constructed by the weighted nerve [58, 025X, 02FN].)
   Take U ⊆ Z [1] the union of the collections U0 = S and U1 = T and consider
the weighted nerve q ′ : Z ′ → ∆1 of the localized action functor. Since U is vertical
for q, considered both as a cocartesian fibration and a cartesian fibration, we have

C.7. Inner-Homs in the derived setting. Our aim in this subsection is to prove
the following.
Proposition C.19. For a finitely generated linear abelian category A♡ which has
enough compact injectives, the V ectpro-f in -module ∞-category Dpro-f in = D(A♡
                                                                               pro-f in )
admits restrictive inner-Homs, relative to the subcategory Df in of complexes with
finite length, bounded cohomology.
   Recall that a complex x in Apro-f in = Ch(A♡pro-f in ) is called K-injective if the
Hom-complex Hom∗Apro-f in (−, x) preserves quasi-isomorphisms. One similarly de-
fines K-projective complexes. Note that K-injectives and K-projectives are stable
under homotopy equivalence.
Lemma C.20. The full subcategory Inj Kpro-f in of K-injectives in Kpro-f in is
stable under the action of V ectpro-f in , and hence forms a V ectpro-f in -module sub-
category in Kpro-f in .
            TQFTS FROM DERIVED QUANTUM GROUP REPRESENTATIONS                          149


Proof. Via duality and localization, it suffices to show that the subcategory Proj B
of K-projectives in B = Ch(B ♡ ) is stable under the action of Ch(Vect), for any
finitely generated abelian category B with enough compact projectives. However,
this is clear as any linear cochain complex v is homotopy equivalent to its cohomol-
ogy H ∗ (v). Hence the product with a K-projective v ⊗ P is homotopy equivalent
to a possibly-infinite sum of shifts of P . Since the class of K-projectives is stable
under arbitrary sums and shifting, we have that v ⊗ P is in fact K-projective.
   We consider the fibration k K ⊗                                                ⊗
                                  pro-f in → Mod and the full subcategory k Inj K pro-f in
        ⊗                                                      ⊗
in k K pro-f in spanned by those objects in the fibers (k K pro-f in )I which map to
objects in V ectI− × Inj K I+ under the transport equivalence
                              ∼                          I                 I
              (k K ⊗
                   pro-f in )I → (k K pro-f in ) = V ectpro-f in × Kpro-f in .
                                                I        −            +



Stability of Inj Kpro-f in under the action of V ectpro-f in tells us that the fibers
(k Inj K ⊗                                                                            ⊗
          pro-f in )I are stable under transport, and hence that the sequence k Inj K pro-f in →
     ⊗                                                               ⊗

of a Mod-monoidal ∞-category. Furthermore we have the equivalence
                     ∼                     ⊗                           ⊗

             pro-f in → Fin∗ ×Mod (k Inj K pro-f in ) = Fin∗ ×Mod (k K pro-f in ),

so that the inclusion k Inj K ⊗              ⊗

Lemma C.21. If A♡ has enough compact injectives, then the category Apro-f in =
Ch(A♡ )pro-f in has enough K-injectives.
   Of course, by enough K-injectives we mean that every object x admits a quasi-
isomorphism x → I(x) into a K-injective complex.
Proof. As in the proof of Lemma C.15, we can find a finitely generated linear abelian
category B ♡ with (B ♡ )op ∼
                           = A♡
                              pro-f in and B
                                             op ∼
                                                = Apro-f in , for B = Ch(B ♡ ). By our
hypotheses B ♡ has enough compact projectives, and therefore enough projectives.
It follows that B has enough K-projectives (cf. [25, Lemma 13.3]), and by duality
Apro-f in has enough K-injectives.                                                  □
   Existence of enough K-injectives tells us that the full subcategory Inj Kpro-f in
spanned by K-injectives in Kpro-f in is a reflective [58, 02F5]. We therefore ap-
ply standard results for reflective subcategories–as in Section 14.3–to see that the
derived ∞-category Dpro-f in is identified with the subcategory of K-injective com-
plexes in Kpro-f in , and that the localization functor Kpro-f in → Dpro-f in is defined
via an application of functorial K-injective resolutions.
Corollary C.22. Suppose A♡ has enough compact injectives. The inclusion Inj Kpro-f in →
Kpro-f in admits a left adjoint L : Kpro-f in → Inj Kpro-f in which realizes Inj Kpro-f in
as a localization of Kpro-f in along the class of quasi-isomorphisms. In particular,
the composite functor

                                          4 Kpro-f in
                                                              )
                     Inj Kpro-f in                           / Dpro-f in

is an equivalence, and the localization functor Kpro-f in → Dpro-f in admits a fully
faithful right adjoint R : Dpro-f in → Kpro-f in which is an equivalence onto the full
subcategory of K-injectives.
150                                      CRIS NEGRON


Proof. This is a special case of [58, 02FD, 02FE, 04JL].                                       □

Remark C.23. In general, the category Apro-f in will have enough K-projectives,
and one can construct the derived ∞-category Dpro-f in via K-projective resolutions.
For us the injective setting is more relevant.
      We can now prove our main result.

Proof of Proposition C.19. We have the linear functor k K ⊗                      ⊗
                                                                  pro-f in → k D pro-f in which
                                      ⊗              ⊗
restricts to a linear functor k Inj K pro-f in → k D pro-f in whose fibers are equivalence,
and hence which is an equivalence of V ectpro-f in -module categories [58, 028C]. So
it suffices to show that Inj Kpro-f in admits restrictive inner-Homs relative to the
subcategory of complexes with finite cohomology.
   Any complex x with finite cohomology admits a pair of quasi-isomorphisms
x → x′ ← x′′ in which x′′ is a bounded complex of finite length objects. (To
see this one can check the analogous claim in the dual category Ch(B ♡ ) for B ♡ =
(A♡          op
   pro-f in ) , where the result is standard.) Since Hom-complexes with K-injective
target preserve quasi-isomorphisms, and since the action of V ectf in preserves quasi-
isomorphisms by Lemma C.15, we have a natural isomorphisms of functors
            HK       (− ⊗ x, y) ∼
               pro-f in          = HK         (− ⊗ x′′ , y) : V ectpro-f in → K an
                                         pro-f in


whenever y is in Inj Kpro-f in . (Here HKpro-f in is a Hom functor for the homotopy
∞-category.) By Corollary C.18 the functor HKpro-f in (− ⊗ x′′ , y) is representable,
and hence HKpro-f in (− ⊗ x, y) is representable. Thus Inj K admits the proposed
inner-Homs.                                                                        □
